**Homeless Housing, Assistance and Prevention (HHAP) Round 6, County and Fresno Madera CoC CA-514**
- Agency: Fresno County Administrative Office, Office of Housing and Homelessness (administrative entity for the County and the Fresno Madera Continuum of Care)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HHAP-6 (agreement 25-HHAP-10013 approved June 30, 2026, term to June 30, 2030): County $4,301,909.86 plus CoC $5,246,520.28, combined $9,548,430 administered by the County; 50% ($4,774,215) disbursed up front, balance after 75% obligation and 50% expenditure by June 30, 2027. Administration capped at 7% ($668,390); at least 10% ($954,843) for youth 18 to 24. The City of Fresno receives its own $10,491,196.56 and Madera County $610,483.23 under the shared Regionally Coordinated Homelessness Action Plan (August 5, 2025).
- AMI requirement: Homeless and at-risk households per HHAP; uses planned are emergency shelter, rental assistance, rural outreach, prevention and diversion, data systems and youth services.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: No County HHAP capital NOFA. Subrecipient agreements return to the Board as needed; HHAP-7 planning will run through the Office of Housing and Homelessness and the CoC in 2027.)
- Layering notes: Fresno County splits housing work three ways: the Department of Public Works and Planning, Community Development Division (ComDev) runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA and the annual Affordable Housing Development NOFO; the County Administrative Office's new Office of Housing and Homelessness (OHH, created November 18, 2025) administers HHAP, the Continuum of Care and Transitional Rent and is absorbing housing programs from Behavioral Health and ComDev (Phase 2 approved November 3, 2026); and the Department of Behavioral Health co-applies for Homekey+ and NPLH and funds BHSA housing. Fresno Housing (the joint City and County Housing Authorities) is the dominant developer and voucher source. The City of Fresno and Clovis are separate entitlements with their own programs (Pass 3). fresnocountyca.gov and fresnohousing.org open normally; Board records are on fresnocounty.legistar.com and the Legistar web API client 'fresnocounty'. The County has not used HHAP for capital; it funds the shelter and services system and provides the 100% match for ESG. For a developer, HHAP matters as a possible operating or rental subsidy source for PSH units referred through Coordinated Entry, negotiated with OHH. Source is the June 30, 2026 Board item (file 26-0639); allocation figures match HCD's HHAP-6 allocation table.
- Source: https://fresnocounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=77828&GUID=F217E85A-6DC8-47AE-BDD9-0A692B4AE258
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Office of Housing and Homelessness: Transitional Rent, Housing Flex Pool and Continuum of Care administration**
- Agency: Fresno County Administrative Office, Office of Housing and Homelessness (Dylan McCully, Division Manager)
- Type: other
- Typical award: OHH administers more than $12 million a year of homelessness grants (HHAP, ESG, CalWORKs housing, state Transitional Housing and Housing Navigation programs) and is the Collaborative Applicant and Management Entity for the Fresno Madera CoC (HUD CoC program and Coordinated Entry) and the county lead for the CalAIM Transitional Rent benefit; Phase 2 (approved November 3, 2026) moves Behavioral Health housing programs and ComDev housing programs under OHH with a budget and salary resolution to follow.
- AMI requirement: Homeless and at-risk households referred through Coordinated Entry; Transitional Rent covers up to six months of rent for eligible Medi-Cal members.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No NOFA. OHH is to return to the Board within 12 months (by November 2027) with an update; the Housing Flex Pool operator RFP is planned under the BHSA Integrated Plan. Developers seeking referrals or operating subsidy for PSH units should contact OHH.)
- Layering notes: Fresno County splits housing work three ways: the Department of Public Works and Planning, Community Development Division (ComDev) runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA and the annual Affordable Housing Development NOFO; the County Administrative Office's new Office of Housing and Homelessness (OHH, created November 18, 2025) administers HHAP, the Continuum of Care and Transitional Rent and is absorbing housing programs from Behavioral Health and ComDev (Phase 2 approved November 3, 2026); and the Department of Behavioral Health co-applies for Homekey+ and NPLH and funds BHSA housing. Fresno Housing (the joint City and County Housing Authorities) is the dominant developer and voucher source. The City of Fresno and Clovis are separate entitlements with their own programs (Pass 3). fresnocountyca.gov and fresnohousing.org open normally; Board records are on fresnocounty.legistar.com and the Legistar web API client 'fresnocounty'. OHH is now the single door for County operating money (HHAP, Transitional Rent, BHSA subsidies routed through the flex pool) and for CoC letters of support, which the County's own NOFO requires for projects with homeless set-asides or HOME-ARP requests. Capital lending stays with ComDev for now. Source is the November 3, 2026 Board item (file 26-1099) with the Phase 2 organization chart.
- Source: https://fresnocounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=78288&GUID=4C74CAC9-E2D6-490E-8C5A-CC99327C7BE9
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA), County formula allocation and PLHA development loans**
- Agency: Fresno County Department of Public Works and Planning, Community Development Division
- Type: loan
- Typical award: HCD formula allocations: $1,643,348 (2019), $2,554,272 (2020), $2,810,897 (2021), estimated five-year 2019-2023 total $9,860,088; 2024-2028 plan estimated $7,209,494 with $1,153,519 (2024) and $1,235,588 (2025). Cycle One Year Three funds of $2,670,353 were committed by resolution on February 10, 2026 to Cherry Crossing II in Sanger (64 units, Self-Help Enterprises) after the Sanger Modular project missed a November 1, 2025 commitment deadline; Sendero Commons carries a replacement PLHA reservation of $2,364,219. The County retains 5% ($140,544 of Year Three) for administration.
- AMI requirement: 55-year affordability; the County's PLHA plan targets households to 80% AMI with half the PLHA-assisted units at or below 60% AMI and half at or below 80% AMI (new construction or rehabilitation of multifamily rental).
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: PLHA is offered through the combined Affordable Housing Development NOFO (January to March each year). HCD commitment deadlines drive timing: Year Three had to be committed by April 30, 2026 or revert to the state Multifamily Housing Program.)
- Layering notes: Fresno County splits housing work three ways: the Department of Public Works and Planning, Community Development Division (ComDev) runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA and the annual Affordable Housing Development NOFO; the County Administrative Office's new Office of Housing and Homelessness (OHH, created November 18, 2025) administers HHAP, the Continuum of Care and Transitional Rent and is absorbing housing programs from Behavioral Health and ComDev (Phase 2 approved November 3, 2026); and the Department of Behavioral Health co-applies for Homekey+ and NPLH and funds BHSA housing. Fresno Housing (the joint City and County Housing Authorities) is the dominant developer and voucher source. The City of Fresno and Clovis are separate entitlements with their own programs (Pass 3). fresnocountyca.gov and fresnohousing.org open normally; Board records are on fresnocounty.legistar.com and the Legistar web API client 'fresnocounty'. PLHA is the County's largest non-federal source and is conditional on HCD review (allow at least 45 days after County approval). The County has concentrated each year's allocation on a single tax credit project in a JPA city (Sanger twice); it is paired with a HOME or HOME-CHDO loan at 0% or 3% simple and 55-year covenants. Source is the February 10, 2026 Board resolution (file 25-1504); allocation figures from the County's PLHA standard agreement (fresno-county-plha-agreement.pdf) and HCD's 2024-2028 PLHA NOFA tables.
- Source: https://fresnocounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=77181&GUID=D6D23411-4846-4C95-8274-C95360065D4F
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Project-Based Vouchers and RAD conversions, Fresno Housing (Housing Authority of the County of Fresno CA028)**
- Agency: Fresno Housing (Housing Authorities of the City and County of Fresno); PBV program contact Aurora Ibarra (559) 443-8459, real estate Michael Duarte (559) 443-8492
- Type: other
- Typical award: The 2027 County Annual Plan lists PBV and RAD assistance proposed for 2025-2026 at County public housing sites: Pinedale I and II (57 units), DeSoto Gardens (40), Marcelli Terrace (24), Del Rey Complex (30), Laton Apartments (20), Sunset Terrace I (20), Mendoza Terrace (44) and II (29), Firebaugh Elderly (30), Cardella Courts (32), Helsem Terrace (40), Biola Apartments (12), Taylor Terrace (27), San Joaquin Apartments (20) and Cazares Terrace I (24). Pipeline with PBVs: Firebaugh La Joya Commons Phase II (40 units, up to 39 PBVs), Orange Cove Senior Housing (60 units, up to 50 PBVs or RAD transfer), Caruthers Commons (60), Kingsburg (80), Selma (80), Del Rey Family Housing (up to 127 multifamily and 45 single-family), Cordillera Commons Phase I, San Joaquin (50), Firebaugh RAD (152). Past competitive awards to third parties ran 15 to 50 PBVs per project.
- AMI requirement: PBV households generally at 30% to 40% AMI selected through Fresno Housing waiting lists and Coordinated Entry for PSH; RAD conversions preserve public housing affordability.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Fresno Housing attaches PBVs to its own and affiliate-owned projects without competition (Administrative Plan exception for PHA-owned units, typically with $40,000 per unit minimum hard costs); third-party owners must respond to a published RFP advertised in newspapers and on fresnohousing.org for three consecutive weeks. No RFP was open on fresnohousing.org this pass; past public notices (Avalon Commons, Crossroads Village, Sun Lodge, Step Up at 99) show awards of 15 to 50 PBVs. Watch the Boards of Commissioners meeting books for RFP authorizations.)
- Layering notes: Fresno County splits housing work three ways: the Department of Public Works and Planning, Community Development Division (ComDev) runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA and the annual Affordable Housing Development NOFO; the County Administrative Office's new Office of Housing and Homelessness (OHH, created November 18, 2025) administers HHAP, the Continuum of Care and Transitional Rent and is absorbing housing programs from Behavioral Health and ComDev (Phase 2 approved November 3, 2026); and the Department of Behavioral Health co-applies for Homekey+ and NPLH and funds BHSA housing. Fresno Housing (the joint City and County Housing Authorities) is the dominant developer and voucher source. The City of Fresno and Clovis are separate entitlements with their own programs (Pass 3). fresnocountyca.gov and fresnohousing.org open normally; Board records are on fresnocounty.legistar.com and the Legistar web API client 'fresnocounty'. Fresno Housing is both the PHA and the county's most prolific developer (over 2,100 units since 2010, $600 million of development), so most County PBVs flow to its own tax credit and RAD deals; a third-party developer's realistic path is a joint venture with Fresno Housing as co-general partner, as at Sendero Commons' sister project Davu Village, or a competitive RFP when one is published. Davis-Bacon applies to PBV new construction of nine or more units. Source is the 2027 Annual Plan for the Housing Authority of the County of Fresno (draft) on fresnohousing.org; selection rules from the 2027 County Administrative Plan chapter 17.
- Source: https://fresnohousing.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/01/Attachement-A2-2027-Annual-Plan-Housing-Authority-of-the-County-of-Fresno-Draft.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Public Facilities Impact Fees suspended countywide (unincorporated Fresno County)**
- Agency: Fresno County Department of Public Works and Planning, Development Services and Capital Projects Division
- Type: fee waiver
- Typical award: The County's Public Facilities Impact Fee ordinance has been suspended since 2017: the Board continued the suspension at hearings on October 31, 2017 and October 9, 2018 'until further notice', and the 2025 Housing Element Annual Progress Report (accepted March 17, 2026) states there has been no change. Unincorporated projects therefore pay no County public facilities impact fee; school district, water and sewer district and traffic mitigation fees still apply.
- AMI requirement: None; the suspension applies to all development regardless of affordability.
- Cycle: rolling (next: In effect until the Board acts to reinstate the fee. Confirm current status with Development Services before underwriting, and note that fees in the eight JPA cities are set by each city.)
- Layering notes: Fresno County splits housing work three ways: the Department of Public Works and Planning, Community Development Division (ComDev) runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA and the annual Affordable Housing Development NOFO; the County Administrative Office's new Office of Housing and Homelessness (OHH, created November 18, 2025) administers HHAP, the Continuum of Care and Transitional Rent and is absorbing housing programs from Behavioral Health and ComDev (Phase 2 approved November 3, 2026); and the Department of Behavioral Health co-applies for Homekey+ and NPLH and funds BHSA housing. Fresno Housing (the joint City and County Housing Authorities) is the dominant developer and voucher source. The City of Fresno and Clovis are separate entitlements with their own programs (Pass 3). fresnocountyca.gov and fresnohousing.org open normally; Board records are on fresnocounty.legistar.com and the Legistar web API client 'fresnocounty'. Fresno County has no affordable housing fee waiver program because it has no public facilities impact fee to waive; this is a cost advantage for unincorporated sites and County islands relative to the City of Fresno (which runs its own affordable housing fee deferral and infill waiver). The County also has no inclusionary ordinance, in-lieu fee, housing trust fund or Measure C housing component (Measure C is a transportation tax; its transit-oriented development program can fund infrastructure near affordable projects). Source is the 2025 Housing Element APR Board file (26-0110), Attachment B program implementation details.
- Source: https://fresnocounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=77296&GUID=3F6232C5-33D6-4BB4-8A59-D0F504DFEFAE
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Welfare exemption from property tax for nonprofit-owned affordable housing, Fresno County Assessor**
- Agency: Fresno County Assessor-Recorder, Exemptions section ((559) 600-5956)
- Type: tax abatement
- Typical award: Full or partial exemption of the property tax on qualifying low-income rental housing owned by a nonprofit or by a limited partnership with a nonprofit managing general partner holding a BOE Organizational Clearance Certificate; the Assessor's exemptions page lists the Welfare Exemption among available exemptions.
- AMI requirement: Units restricted to households at or below 80% AMI under a recorded regulatory agreement or tax credit allocation; exemption prorated to qualifying units.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: File BOE-267 and supplemental affidavits with the Assessor by February 15, 2027 for the 2027-28 roll; first-year claims may be filed after acquisition with late-filing penalties.)
- Layering notes: Fresno County splits housing work three ways: the Department of Public Works and Planning, Community Development Division (ComDev) runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA and the annual Affordable Housing Development NOFO; the County Administrative Office's new Office of Housing and Homelessness (OHH, created November 18, 2025) administers HHAP, the Continuum of Care and Transitional Rent and is absorbing housing programs from Behavioral Health and ComDev (Phase 2 approved November 3, 2026); and the Department of Behavioral Health co-applies for Homekey+ and NPLH and funds BHSA housing. Fresno Housing (the joint City and County Housing Authorities) is the dominant developer and voucher source. The City of Fresno and Clovis are separate entitlements with their own programs (Pass 3). fresnocountyca.gov and fresnohousing.org open normally; Board records are on fresnocounty.legistar.com and the Legistar web API client 'fresnocounty'. Standard state exemption administered locally; Fresno Housing deals and Self-Help Enterprises deals in the JPA cities rely on it to carry 30% AMI rents. Source is the Assessor's Property Tax Exemptions page.
- Source: https://www.fresnocountyca.gov/Departments/Assessor/Exemptions
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)


### Madera County

**Behavioral Health Services Act Integrated Plan FY 2026-29 and Behavioral Health Bridge Housing (Hotel Casa Grande)**
- Agency: Madera County Behavioral Health Services
- Type: other
- Typical award: Madera County (population about 160,000) is eligible for the small-county housing interventions exemption; its FY 2026-29 Integrated Plan and any exemption or transfer request could not be read (County website blocked). Known housing activity: Behavioral Health Bridge Housing at the Hotel Casa Grande (37 units) with a three-year, $6.9 million supportive services contract to RH Community Builders approved August 2024; a May 2026 two-year shared-housing agreement with Madera Rescue Mission reserving 34 beds (not to exceed $1,212,576); BHCIP Round 5 award of $24,989,161 (July 2023) for a youth and adult crisis stabilization unit and sobering center (facility, not housing).
- AMI requirement: BHSA eligible adults and youth with serious mental illness or substance use disorder.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Contact Behavioral Health Services about housing intervention contracts; no NOFA or capital program was found.)
- Layering notes: Madera County is a small non-entitlement county: the Community and Economic Development Department (planning, state CDBG and HOME applications) and Behavioral Health Services (BHSA, bridge housing) are the only County housing actors, the Housing Authority of the City of Madera (HACM) is the PHA for the county, and the Fresno Madera Continuum of Care (CA-514, led by Fresno County) handles HHAP and CoC funds. There is no County housing trust fund, NOFA, inclusionary ordinance or impact fee waiver program. The City of Madera is a separate CDBG and HOME entitlement with its own PLHA plan (Pass 3). maderacounty.com blocks automated access (403 on pages and documents), the PrimeGov and IQM2 portals returned no 2026 meetings, and Board agendas were read from PDFs mirrored on maderacountywater.com; other sources were HCD allocation tables, maderaha.org, zoneomics (County Code Title 18), CEQAnet and press coverage. Confirmation needed: BHSA housing dollars, exemption status and any PSH capital plans. Madera's pattern is motel-based bridge housing and contracted beds rather than capital; a developer would be looking at an operating or master-lease contract. Source is the County MHSA/BHSA page (blocked to automated access); the bridge housing and BHCIP details come from the Madera Tribune (July 9, 2023) and Board meeting summaries.

**Welfare exemption from property tax for nonprofit-owned affordable housing, Madera County Assessor**
- Agency: Madera County Assessor, Brett Frazier ((559) 675-7710, assessor@maderacounty.com)
- Type: tax abatement
- Typical award: Standard state welfare exemption for low-income housing owned by nonprofits or limited partnerships with a nonprofit managing general partner holding a BOE Organizational Clearance Certificate; the County publishes the BOE-267-L2 tenant data affidavit for lower income housing.
- AMI requirement: Units restricted to households at or below 80% AMI under a recorded regulatory agreement; exemption prorated to qualifying units.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: File BOE-267 and supplemental affidavits with the Assessor by February 15, 2027 for the 2027-28 roll.)
- Layering notes: Madera County is a small non-entitlement county: the Community and Economic Development Department (planning, state CDBG and HOME applications) and Behavioral Health Services (BHSA, bridge housing) are the only County housing actors, the Housing Authority of the City of Madera (HACM) is the PHA for the county, and the Fresno Madera Continuum of Care (CA-514, led by Fresno County) handles HHAP and CoC funds. There is no County housing trust fund, NOFA, inclusionary ordinance or impact fee waiver program. The City of Madera is a separate CDBG and HOME entitlement with its own PLHA plan (Pass 3). maderacounty.com blocks automated access (403 on pages and documents), the PrimeGov and IQM2 portals returned no 2026 meetings, and Board agendas were read from PDFs mirrored on maderacountywater.com; other sources were HCD allocation tables, maderaha.org, zoneomics (County Code Title 18), CEQAnet and press coverage. ### Marin County

**Behavioral Health Services Act (BHSA) housing interventions, Marin County Behavioral Health and Recovery Services**
- Agency: Marin County Health and Human Services, Behavioral Health and Recovery Services (BHRS)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: The Board approved and certified the BHSA FY 2026-27 through FY 2028-29 Three-Year Integrated Plan on Dec 9, 2025 (consent item CA-6l). The plan's housing intervention totals were not obtained because the County and HHS sites are blocked; a 2025 County transition estimate put the housing component near 30% of a roughly $19,000,000 BHSA allocation, about $5,700,000 a year. Known commitments: up to $1,931,918 in MHSA/BHSA capital match for the nine-unit Carmelita House Homekey+ project (April 14, 2026) plus $321,425 in BHSA operating subsidies and services; a scattered-site assisted independent housing and rental subsidy RFP for BHSA-eligible adults with serious mental illness (RFP HHS-2026-02, due April 17, 2026); and an early intervention RFP (HHS-2026-05).
- AMI requirement: Adults and youth with serious mental illness or substance use disorder who are chronically homeless, homeless or at risk; Housing First; BHRS referral. Capital grants are forgivable if the project completes and keeps units for the target population (Carmelita: nine PSH units).
- Cycle: rolling (next: Plan in effect July 1, 2026 with annual updates each spring; capital has been awarded project by project (Homekey+ match) rather than through a NOFA. Ask BHRS about operating subsidies and capital match for PSH units in the 2027 update.)
- Layering notes: marincounty.gov, marincounty.org, assets.marincounty.gov, marinhhs.org and arcc.marincounty.gov sit behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06, so County program pages could not be opened; Board agendas were read on marin.granicus.com and staff reports through its MetaViewer links where available. Marin's BHSA housing money is a few million dollars a year and is being used as the local match that makes Homekey+ and small PSH conversions work, plus scattered-site rental subsidies. State funds trigger prevailing wage. The Dec 9, 2025 plan approval and the Carmelita capital grant were read in Board agendas and the April 14, 2026 staff report on Granicus (opened); the plan PDF itself was not reachable, so allocation figures are needs_confirmation.

**Development fee waiver for affordable housing (no countywide program)**
- Agency: Marin County Community Development Agency
- Type: fee waiver
- Typical award: None adopted. Development Code 22.24.020 states that nothing in the density bonus chapter requires direct financial incentives such as subsidies or fee waivers, though the County may offer them at its discretion. No fee waiver program appears in Board housing actions reviewed for 2025 and 2026.
- AMI requirement: Not applicable.
- Cycle: closed/inactive (next: Not applicable. Request fee relief case by case through the Affordable Housing Fund or as a density bonus incentive.)
- Layering notes: Negative finding. Labor Code 1720(b)(4) counts waived fees as public funds for prevailing wage purposes. Read on the Zoneomics mirror of Chapter 22.24.

**HOME and CDBG, Marin County Urban County (countywide entitlement with city planning areas)**
- Agency: Marin County Community Development Agency, Housing and Federal Grants Division, with the Countywide Priority Setting Committee
- Type: loan
- Typical award: FY 2026-27: about $1,600,000 CDBG and $676,000 HOME approved June 23, 2026. Housing awards: Eden Housing Oak Hill (unincorporated, between San Quentin and Larkspur Landing, about 230 units with more than 130 for educators and school employees) $531,000 CDBG; Eden Housing Mill Valley rental development $360,000 HOME; Homeward Bound of Marin permanent supportive housing for formerly homeless seniors in San Rafael $317,000 HOME; Habitat for Humanity Greater SF home rehabilitation program $70,000 CDBG (requested $296,000); Homeward Bound shelter capital repairs about $132,000 CDBG; the balance to child care facilities, a San Rafael preschool and public services. FY 2025-26 CDBG entitlement was $1,512,738; that NOFA drew 33 applications requesting about $3,940,000 CDBG, $1,600,000 HOME and $1,390,000 PLHA. CDBG is split into planning-area shares (San Rafael's 2026-27 share about $508,000) recommended by the cities and the County.
- AMI requirement: HOME: households at or below 80% AMI at HOME rents with a County regulatory agreement; CDBG: low and moderate income benefit. The Consolidated Plan for FY 2025-2029 (adopted June 10, 2025) sets priorities including family, rental, special needs and homeowner housing and an eastern Marin land trust with specific inclusion for African American households.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Next NOFA expected January 2027 (applications due February 2027, PSC in May, Board in June) alongside PLHA and Trust Fund match; a two-year funding cycle covers 2026-27 and 2027-28 for services.)
- Layering notes: marincounty.gov, marincounty.org, assets.marincounty.gov, marinhhs.org and arcc.marincounty.gov sit behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06, so County program pages could not be opened; Board agendas were read on marin.granicus.com and staff reports through its MetaViewer links where available. Marin is the HUD entitlement for the whole county, including its cities, so a project in San Rafael or Novato competes for the same CDBG and HOME as unincorporated sites and needs the planning-area committee's support first. Federal money is a few hundred thousand dollars per project and is used to complete 9% deals (Homeward Bound) or as early money for large 4% deals (Oak Hill). Davis-Bacon applies at 12 HOME-assisted units or eight CDBG units; County loans carry state prevailing wage. Figures from the Local News Matters report of June 24, 2026 (opened), the June 23, 2026 Board agenda (opened) and the Dec 5, 2024 Priority Setting Committee packet on GovDelivery (opened).
- Source: https://localnewsmatters.org/2026/06/24/marin-county-affordable-housing-community-projects/
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Inclusionary housing, affordable housing impact fee and commercial linkage fee (Development Code Chapter 22.22) with state density bonus (Chapter 22.24)**
- Agency: Marin County Community Development Agency, Planning Division (unincorporated area); fees deposited to the Affordable Housing Fund
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Chapter 22.22: 20% of units or lots in new residential projects must be affordable (projects of four or fewer units round up only above 0.70 of a unit), restricted in perpetuity unless shortened to match financing; income tiers very low (30% to 50% AMI), low (50% to 80%), moderate (80% to 120%) and middle (120% to 150%); alternatives in priority order are off-site units, land dedication and an in-lieu fee set at 125% of the requirement when a waiver is granted; single-family homes over 2,000 square feet pay an Affordable Housing Impact Fee (Ordinance 3500, 2008); non-residential projects pay a jobs-housing linkage fee by floor area and use (Ordinance 3798 and Resolution 2023-102 of Sept 19, 2023; Resolution 2016-122 for residential care and skilled nursing). Fees are indexed annually to the California Construction Cost Index; the June 23, 2026 adjustment was 3.9% and the FY 2026-27 in-lieu fee is $421,886.99 per required unit per the County fee page (2023-24 fee was $399,654). Chapter 22.24 implements Government Code 65915: base 20% bonus at 10% low or 5% very low income units, one to five concessions by affordability level, unlimited waivers of standards that physically preclude the project, height increases up to three stories near major transit for 80% lower income projects; the County may offer incentives to deed-restricted projects without a bonus but is not required to give fee waivers.
- AMI requirement: Inclusionary units restricted in perpetuity (or the maximum financing allows); density bonus rental units 30 years minimum under 22.24, moderate-income for-sale units in perpetuity. Rents capped at 50%, 65% and 100% of AMI levels for very low, low and moderate tiers.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Applies at entitlement; fees adjust each June (next adjustment June 2027). A multi-jurisdictional Affordable Housing Impact Fee Study by Strategic Economics for the cities and towns was authorized April 15, 2025, and the County is also studying development impact fees for infrastructure (public hearing March 10, 2026).)
- Layering notes: Marin's unincorporated inclusionary rule is one of the strictest in the state (20% in perpetuity, in-lieu fee only after off-site and land dedication are shown infeasible), and fee receipts flow to the Affordable Housing Fund that this database records separately. Fee revenue is modest because unincorporated development is small. A 100% affordable project satisfies the chapter automatically and uses the state density bonus. Density bonus alone does not trigger prevailing wage; Labor Code 1720(b)(4) would treat any fee waiver as public funds. Sources opened: Zoneomics mirror of Development Code Article III (Chapters 22.22 and 22.24) and the June 23, 2026 fee adjustment staff report on Granicus; the County fee calculator page was blocked.
- Source: https://marin.granicus.com/MetaViewer.php?view_id=33&clip_id=13163&meta_id=1442466
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Marin County Affordable Housing Fund (Housing Trust Fund) and the annual federal and state housing NOFA**
- Agency: Marin County Community Development Agency, Housing and Federal Grants Division, 3501 Civic Center Drive, San Rafael; affordablehousing@marincounty.gov
- Type: loan
- Typical award: The Affordable Housing Fund (also called the Housing Trust Fund) is capitalized by inclusionary in-lieu fees, the single-family Affordable Housing Impact Fee, commercial linkage fees, County general fund appropriations and Measure W housing dollars, and is used both as the local match for PLHA and as standalone loans and grants. Guidelines adopted Dec 12, 2023 run through 2027: awards generally $50,000 per unit, up to $100,000 per unit depending on funds, project type, location and bedrooms, as loans with terms set project by project; projects in cities need a contribution from that city. Recent actions read in Board agendas: $5,239,098 Housing Trust Fund loan to Habitat for Humanity Greater San Francisco for predevelopment and construction (April 15, 2025); $500,000 grant to capitalize a Marin Housing Authority preservation loan pool (June 10, 2025); $300,000 Housing Trust and $210,000 Measure W to the Community Land Trust Association of West Marin for 55/65 Second Street, Point Reyes Station, plus $300,000 for the Sacred Heart rectory in Olema (April 7, 2026); Housing Trust Fund loan documents for Marinwood Plaza (125 units, April 2026); $3,000,000 predevelopment loan to Marin Housing Authority for Golden Gate Village (Dec 5, 2023) restructured April 14, 2026 into a 30-year deferred, interest-free loan. The 2026-27 NOFA (open Jan 19 to Feb 19, 2026) offered about $700,000 HOME and $900,000 PLHA matched by the Affordable Housing Fund; the Board approved FY 2026-27 CDBG, HOME, Housing Trust Fund and PLHA recommendations on June 23, 2026 (Resolution 2026-060), with about $477,000 PLHA to be matched 1:1 by the Trust Fund once the state agreement is signed.
- AMI requirement: Priority to projects in unincorporated Marin and to households at or below 60% AMI when paired with PLHA; County loan documents carry long-term affordability covenants (County inclusionary units are restricted in perpetuity). Projects in cities require a local government contribution.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: The 2027-28 cycle should open in January 2027 with applications due in February, a Countywide Priority Setting Committee hearing in May and Board approval in June, matching the 2026-27 schedule (open Jan 19, 2026, closed Feb 19, 2026, PSC May 19, Board June 23, 2026). Trust Fund requests outside the NOFA have gone to the Board on their own (Habitat, CLAM, Golden Gate Village).)
- Layering notes: marincounty.gov, marincounty.org, assets.marincounty.gov, marinhhs.org and arcc.marincounty.gov sit behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06, so County program pages could not be opened; Board agendas were read on marin.granicus.com and staff reports through its MetaViewer links where available. Marin's county money is small and project-specific: the Trust Fund mostly supplies PLHA match (about $500,000 a year) and five- to six-figure gap grants, with occasional larger loans for County-priority sites (Habitat in Novato, Oak Hill workforce housing next to San Quentin, Golden Gate Village). The County also weighed joining an Affordable Housing Guarantor Financing Program for Oak Hill in April 2025. Public funds trigger state prevailing wage; HOME and CDBG in the same award add Davis-Bacon. Sources opened: Board agendas for April 15, 2025, June 10, 2025, Dec 5 and 12, 2023, April 7 and 14, 2026 and June 23, 2026 on Granicus, the April 14, 2026 Golden Gate Village loan staff report, and the Local News Matters reports of April 6 and June 24, 2026. NOFA amounts and dates come from search engine excerpts of the County NOFA page, which is why this record is needs_confirmation.
- Source: https://marin.granicus.com/AgendaViewer.php?view_id=33&clip_id=13163
- Confidence: needs_confirmation (researched 2026-11-06)

**Marin County Continuum of Care (CA-507) and HHAP**
- Agency: Marin County Health and Human Services, Division of Homelessness and Coordinated Care (CoC lead and HHAP administrative entity for the County and CoC)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HHAP Round 5: County $1,542,420 and CoC $1,607,673; Round 6: County $1,243,788 and CoC $1,328,387 (regional plan puts all CoC Round 6 funds in Permanent Housing Solutions, including $500,000 for rapid rehousing and rental subsidies, landlord incentives and $25,722 for HMIS). State accountability site: $12,500,000 HHAP rounds 1 to 5 in the Marin area, $10,700,000 directly to the County ($8,700,000 obligated, $5,900,000 spent by mid-2025); Encampment Resolution Funding $22,200,000 in the area, $21,400,000 to the County (Binford Road and Encampment Resolution and Housing contracts with Episcopal Community Services, $2,217,907, Dec 2025). HUD CoC program FY 2024 award to CA-507: $2,016,283 (Shelter Plus Care, administered by MHA); CoC planning grant $256,052 (FY 2026-27); state ESG to the County $462,495 (2024). 2024 PIT: 1,090 people, 788 unsheltered, 1,487 year-round beds. Homekey rounds 1 and 2 brought $24,700,000 to Marin projects (Casa Buena in Corte Madera, 1251 S. Eliseo in Larkspur, Novato veterans housing opened Jan 2026); Homekey+: $2,700,000 for nine PSH units at Carmelita House, 530 Blackstone Drive, San Rafael, with Catholic Charities (awarded Oct 2025), matched by up to $1,931,918 in County MHSA/BHSA capital (April 14, 2026) and $321,425 in BHSA operating subsidies.
- AMI requirement: People experiencing or at risk of homelessness per HUD and state definitions; CoC permanent housing through Coordinated Entry (run by MHA); HHAP-6 must obligate 75% and spend 50% by June 30, 2027.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: HHAP-7 is available to HHAP-6 grantees; the CoC runs the HUD NOFO each summer with Homebase as consultant. A Board study session on the homelessness system of care was held Oct 14, 2025. Homekey+ sites are pursued jointly by HHS and nonprofit operators.)
- Layering notes: Small CoC: HHAP money (about $2,500,000 a region per round) goes to rapid rehousing, rental subsidies and Shelter Plus Care, not capital. Marin's capital for PSH has come from Homekey and Homekey+ matched by County MHSA/BHSA dollars, which is the pattern to repeat. Figures from the HCD HHAP Round 6 Marin regional plan and Round 5 application (opened), HCD allocation lists, the HUD FY 2025 CoC award report, accountability.ca.gov, Board agendas on Granicus and the April 14, 2026 BHRS staff report.

**Measure W West Marin transient occupancy tax, community housing share**
- Agency: Marin County Community Development Agency (community housing share) with the Measure W West Marin TOT Oversight Committee and Working Group
- Type: grant
- Typical award: Measure W (Nov 2018) added 4 percentage points to the hotel and short-term rental tax in unincorporated West Marin, raising about $1,200,000 to $1,300,000 a year, split half to fire and emergency services and half to community housing. County guidelines in force through June 30, 2026 allocate $100,000 a year to direct housing assistance and the remainder to community housing projects. Awards include $210,000 to the Community Land Trust Association of West Marin for 55/65 Second Street in Point Reyes Station (April 7, 2026, alongside $300,000 from the Affordable Housing Fund); the County's stated practice is about $50,000 per affordable unit. The 2024-25 compliance audit was accepted June 9, 2026.
- AMI requirement: Housing in the West Marin tax area (Point Reyes Station, Inverness, Olema, Bolinas, Stinson Beach, Tomales, Marshall, Dillon Beach) for local residents and workers; grants and loans carry affordability covenants set by the County.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Priorities are set annually by the Board on the Working Group's recommendation; the guidelines through June 30, 2026 were due for renewal for FY 2026-27. Requests have gone to the Board as individual items (April 7, 2026).)
- Layering notes: marincounty.gov, marincounty.org, assets.marincounty.gov, marinhhs.org and arcc.marincounty.gov sit behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06, so County program pages could not be opened; Board agendas were read on marin.granicus.com and staff reports through its MetaViewer links where available. About $600,000 a year for a coastal area where land trusts, not tax credit developers, do most deals; useful for small acquisition and rehab projects (two to eight units) paired with the Affordable Housing Fund. Public funds trigger state prevailing wage. **Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA), Marin County entitlement, matched 1:1 by the Affordable Housing Fund**
- Agency: Marin County Community Development Agency, Housing and Federal Grants Division (HCD entitlement grantee)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: 2020 five-year plan estimated $4,353,426 from HCD, each dollar matched by the County Affordable Housing Fund; Formula Round 5 award $476,762 (March 2025); 2026-27 cycle about $477,000 PLHA plus match. HCD 2024-2028 Formula Allocation NOFA estimates: CY 2024 $509,302, CY 2025 $545,537, five-year $3,183,138. Example award: $605,062 to EAH Housing in 2024 ($302,531 PLHA plus $302,531 Trust Fund). HCD now requires 40% of new PLHA for affordable owner-occupied workforce housing (up to 150% AMI in high-cost Marin), and the County noted its owner-occupied spending was trailing.
- AMI requirement: PLHA guidelines: rental development for households at or below 60% AMI emphasized (PLHA allows up to 80% AMI rental and 120% to 150% AMI ownership), 55-year covenants on rental; County goals cover family, rental, homeowner and special needs housing and an eastern Marin land trust.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: PLHA is awarded through the County's annual NOFA (next expected January to February 2027) after HCD executes each calendar year's agreement; the Board approves PLHA by separate resolution once the state contract arrives (June 23, 2026 direction).)
- Layering notes: Roughly $1,000,000 a year including County match, spread across two or three projects. Marin's housing element was certified in 2025, so PLHA flows without the compliance holds seen in San Mateo. State funds trigger prevailing wage. Figures from the HCD 2024-2028 PLHA NOFA (opened), the Dec 5, 2024 PSC packet on GovDelivery (opened) and search excerpts of County pages.

**Project-Based Vouchers and public housing conversion, Marin Housing Authority (MHA)**
- Agency: Housing Authority of the County of Marin, 4020 Civic Center Drive, San Rafael, 415-491-2525; procurement on procurement.opengov.com/portal/marinhousing
- Type: other
- Typical award: MHA holds 496 public housing units and 2,541 Housing Choice Vouchers (3,037 combined, FY 2027 draft PHA Plan) and serves the whole county under cooperation agreements with every city. The FY 2027 plan begins converting the roughly 300-unit senior and disabled public housing portfolio (Mill Valley, San Rafael, Novato) to project-based vouchers through a RAD and Section 18 blend, and Golden Gate Village (296 units, Marin City) is converting under RAD with Burbank Housing: Phase 1 (88 units, $76,000,000 to $85,000,000) closed on CMFA bonds up to $50,000,000 (TEFRA Oct 14, 2025), a CDLAC allocation of $41,748,341 and about $3,985,091 in annual federal credits, with construction starting February 2026; Phase 2 TEFRA for up to $160,000,000 of bonds was set for Oct 20, 2026; the full program is estimated at $266,600,000. MHA runs PBV waitlists for partner properties (Victory Village in Novato, Edgewater, Piper Court, Fireside, Toussin Senior, Vivalon Healthy Aging Campus, 1251 S. Eliseo PSH in Larkspur) but has issued no developer PBV RFP recently; 94 Emergency Housing Voucher households were told in Oct 2025 that funding ends in 2026. The County contributes $250,000 a year for MHA's resident services director and has committed more than $6,250,000 to Golden Gate Village since 2022.
- AMI requirement: Households at or below 50% AMI at admission (75% at or below 30% AMI); PBV waitlists per property; Shelter Plus Care and HOPWA administered by MHA for the CoC; HAP contracts up to 20 years.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No PBV solicitation open as of Nov 2026; the FY 2027 Annual Plan was out for comment May 30 to July 14, 2026 and HUD's December 2025 budget letter limits new voucher commitments. Ask MHA about PBVs for specific PSH projects; recent awards went to Homeward Bound and Vivalon buildings.)
- Layering notes: MHA's capacity is going into its own RAD conversions through 2029, so new PBV awards for third-party projects are scarce; Shelter Plus Care and County-funded subsidies (Binford Road temporary rental support, $744,432 contract Dec 16, 2025) fill the gap for PSH. A HAP contract is not a prevailing wage trigger; Davis-Bacon applies to PBV construction of nine or more contract units, and RAD and public housing work carry Davis-Bacon regardless. Sources opened: MHA website pages, the FY 2027 draft PHA Plan (HUD-50075-ST), the April 14, 2026 Golden Gate Village loan staff report, Board agendas on Granicus and Patch and Local News Matters coverage of the bond approvals.
- Source: https://irp.cdn-website.com/4e4dab0f/files/uploaded/HUD-50075-ST+-+draft+052926k.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Tax-exempt multifamily bonds: County TEFRA approvals for CMFA issuances (County not an issuer)**
- Agency: Marin County Board of Supervisors (TEFRA hearings) with the County Executive and Community Development Agency; bonds issued by the California Municipal Finance Authority
- Type: bond
- Typical award: The County holds TEFRA hearings as the CMFA member jurisdiction but does not issue bonds or take on liability: Golden Gate Village Phase 1, up to $50,000,000 (hearing and resolution Oct 14, 2025; CDLAC allocation $41,748,341); Golden Gate Village Phase 2, up to $160,000,000 (hearing Oct 20, 2026). Other Marin deals through CMFA include Centertown II in San Rafael ($18,442,396) and a $30,075,000 acquisition issue; a 2023 Marin City bond approval drew a lawsuit against the County and CMFA. Marinwood Plaza (125 units, 124 restricted) used CalHFA's Mixed-Income Program (April 2026).
- AMI requirement: CDLAC and Section 142(d) minimums under the issuer's regulatory agreement; County loans in the same project add County covenants (Golden Gate Village units stay public housing or PBV-assisted under RAD).
- Cycle: rolling (next: Schedule the TEFRA hearing on a Board agenda with 7-day newspaper notice before the CDLAC application; for projects inside cities the city council holds the hearing (Daly City-style). No County issuer fee.)
- Layering notes: Plan on CMFA or CSCDA as issuer in Marin. Bonds alone do not trigger state prevailing wage; County Trust Fund, HOME or MHA money in the deal does. Sources opened: Board agendas of Oct 14, 2025 and Oct 20, 2026 on Granicus (the Oct 2025 staff report is image-only), Patch and Local News Matters coverage, and the CalHFA Marinwood Plaza listing in search results.

**Welfare Exemption (property tax), Marin County Assessor-Recorder-County Clerk**
- Agency: Marin County Assessor-Recorder-County Clerk, Shelly Scott, Marin Civic Center, San Rafael; 415-473-7215
- Type: tax abatement
- Typical award: Full or partial exemption of property tax on units occupied by households at or below 80% AMI under Revenue and Taxation Code 214(g); worth roughly 1.1% or more of assessed value a year depending on the tax rate area. The Assessor-Recorder posts the BOE-267 series including BOE-267-L, L1 and L2 for low-income housing.
- AMI requirement: Nonprofit or LP with eligible nonprofit managing general partner holding a BOE Organizational Clearance Certificate (plus a Supplemental Clearance Certificate for LP low-income housing); units restricted by recorded regulatory agreement or occupied by households at or below 80% AMI; BOE-267 first filing and annual supplemental affidavits by Feb 15 (late claims receive a reduced exemption under Rev. and Tax. Code 270, and the County FAQ confirms partial relief for late filings).
- Cycle: rolling (next: 2027-02-15 (annual claim deadline for the 2027-28 roll). File the first claim on acquisition so the construction period is covered.)
- Layering notes: marincounty.gov, marincounty.org, assets.marincounty.gov, marinhhs.org and arcc.marincounty.gov sit behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06, so County program pages could not be opened; Board agendas were read on marin.granicus.com and staff reports through its MetaViewer links where available. Standard in every tax credit operating budget. The Assessor's exemption pages (arcc.marincounty.gov) were blocked, so the office contact comes from the BOE county contacts page (opened) and form details from search excerpts of the County forms pages.

### Mendocino County

**Behavioral Health Services Act Integrated Plan FY 2026-29 (housing interventions, bridge housing and Measure B)**
- Agency: Mendocino County Behavioral Health and Recovery Services (Jenine Miller, Director, (707) 472-2341)
- Type: other
- Typical award: Integrated Plan approved July 7, 2026: rental subsidies for up to 150 people a year (80 permanent, 90 interim; 80 units), operating subsidies for about 350 people (80 units), participant assistance and outreach for 50; no capital development projects; FSP exemptions requested as a county under 200,000 population; housing delivered through contracts such as Redwood Community Services Haven House ($400,000 FY 2026-27), Harmony House sober living interim housing ($300,000) and Mendocino Coast Hospitality Center shelter services, alongside Behavioral Health Bridge Housing and Transitional Rent. Measure B (2017 Mental Health Treatment Act sales tax) funds facilities; $7 million was used for the jail behavioral health unit in 2024.
- AMI requirement: BHSA eligible adults and youth with serious mental illness or substance use disorder; Medi-Cal eligible.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Behavioral Health issued an RFP for BHSA housing service providers in 2026; contact the department about subsidy agreements for new PSH units.)
- Layering notes: Mendocino County has no housing department: the Executive Office Grants Division administers state CDBG and HOME program income, Social Services is the administrative entity for the Mendocino County Homeless Services Continuum of Care (CA-509) and HHAP, Behavioral Health and Recovery Services holds BHSA, NPLH and bridge housing, Planning and Building Services handles density bonus and the Housing Element, and the Community Development Commission of Mendocino County (CDC, the housing authority) supplies vouchers and owns housing. The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on September 10, 2024, has no housing trust fund money, no NOFA and no impact fee waiver; County capital help has come from state pass-through grants (CDBG-DR, Homekey) and small HOME program income balances. Ukiah and Fort Bragg run their own programs (Pass 3). mendocinocounty.gov, the Legistar web API client 'mendocino', mendocino.granicusideas.com and cdchousing.org all open normally. BHSA will not fund capital in Mendocino; its value is a project-based rental or operating subsidy on a small number of PSH units. Source is the 2026-2029 Integrated Plan PDF (Legistar attachment to the July 7, 2026 item).
- Source: https://mendocino.legistar1.com/mendocino/attachments/16a82dac-7041-49d0-a22d-f3f414222b80.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Density bonus (Chapter 20.234, Inland Zoning Code) after repeal of the inclusionary ordinance; 2027-2035 Housing Element**
- Agency: Mendocino County Planning and Building Services (Julia Krog, Director, (707) 234-6650)
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Ordinance 4538 (September 10, 2024) rewrote the Inland Zoning Code: Chapter 20.234 grants density bonuses, incentives and concessions exactly as provided in Government Code 65915-65918 in all residential zones, requires a recorded density bonus agreement, and repealed the former Chapter 20.238 inclusionary housing ordinance so the County now defers entirely to state law (2025 Housing Element APR, Action 3.1d). The one large subdivision, Bella Vista near Ukiah (166 lots, modified February 3, 2026), keeps 36 moderate-income for-sale units and 42 senior lots under a development agreement and inclusionary housing plan approved before the repeal. The County hired PlaceWorks ($334,925, March 24, 2026) to prepare the 2027-2035 Housing Element (regional RHNA 6,456 units, adoption due August 2027) and the Ukiah Valley Area Plan mixed-use rezone of 192 parcels is expected in 2026.
- AMI requirement: Government Code 65915 thresholds with recorded restrictions; equity-sharing payments on for-sale bonus units go to the County Affordable Housing Trust Fund.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Requested with the land use application; expect new Housing Element programs and possible incentive changes in 2027.)
- Layering notes: Mendocino County has no housing department: the Executive Office Grants Division administers state CDBG and HOME program income, Social Services is the administrative entity for the Mendocino County Homeless Services Continuum of Care (CA-509) and HHAP, Behavioral Health and Recovery Services holds BHSA, NPLH and bridge housing, Planning and Building Services handles density bonus and the Housing Element, and the Community Development Commission of Mendocino County (CDC, the housing authority) supplies vouchers and owns housing. The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on September 10, 2024, has no housing trust fund money, no NOFA and no impact fee waiver; County capital help has come from state pass-through grants (CDBG-DR, Homekey) and small HOME program income balances. Ukiah and Fort Bragg run their own programs (Pass 3). mendocinocounty.gov, the Legistar web API client 'mendocino', mendocino.granicusideas.com and cdchousing.org all open normally. With the inclusionary ordinance gone there is no local affordability requirement or in-lieu fee outside the coastal zone; the County's land use posture is permissive but offers no local financial incentive. **Project-Based Vouchers and supportive housing vouchers, Community Development Commission of Mendocino County**
- Agency: Community Development Commission of Mendocino County (1076 N. State Street, Ukiah; (707) 463-5462)
- Type: other
- Typical award: CDC administers about 750 Housing Choice Vouchers, about 136 Permanent Supportive Housing vouchers (Coordinated Entry referrals), VASH, FUP and Mainstream vouchers, and project-based vouchers at Sun House, Ukiah (10 one-bedroom units for senior veterans via the VA), The Plateau, Fort Bragg (20 PSH units in a 69-unit Danco development, the most recent PBV award) and Orr Creek Commons, Ukiah (RCHDC). Administrative Plan Chapter XXI (adopted May 15, 2025) allows project-basing up to 20% of budget authority with selection by competitive RFP or without a new competition for projects already selected through LIHTC or HOME competitions within three years.
- AMI requirement: PBV households at or below 50% AMI (75% extremely low income); PSH units filled through Coordinated Entry.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No open RFP ('Project Bids and Awards: none at this time'); a tax credit project can request PBVs without a new competition under the prior-competition rule. CDC's FY 2026-27 streamlined PHA plan went to hearing July 9, 2026.)

CDC PBVs underwrote The Plateau and Orr Creek Commons and are the only operating subsidy available for a Mendocino PSH project; the prior-competition exemption means a CTCAC award can be converted into a PBV request directly. ### Merced County

**Behavioral Health Services Act Integrated Plan FY 2026-29 (Housing Interventions component)**
- Agency: Merced County Behavioral Health and Recovery Services
- Type: other
- Typical award: Three-Year Integrated Plan approved by the Board June 2, 2026 and accepted by DHCS June 19, 2026; 30% of the County's BHSA allocation is reserved for housing interventions. Known housing activity: a December 16, 2025 award to CalLINKS to provide supportive housing under the BHSA housing component, and $12.6 million of Proposition 1 behavioral health infrastructure grants announced June 2025 (facilities). Housing dollar amounts, unit counts and any capital project could not be read because the County website blocks automated access.

The County is a non-entitlement jurisdiction for CDBG and HOME, has no housing trust fund, inclusionary ordinance, in-lieu fee or fee waiver, and Measure V (MCAG half-cent sales tax) is transportation only. **Density bonus and commercial development bonus (County Code Chapter 18.66) and 2024-2032 Housing Element status**
- Agency: Merced County Community and Economic Development Department, Planning Division (Tiffany Ho, Deputy Director)
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Chapter 18.66 (Ordinance 1976, 2019; Ordinance 1990, 2020) grants state-law density bonuses for 10% lower income, 5% very low income, senior, 10% moderate for-sale common interest, 10% transitional foster youth, disabled veteran or homeless units and 20% lower income student housing, with incentives and concessions approved by the Board unless written findings are made, reduced parking (one space for 0-1 bedroom, two for 2-3 bedrooms), land donation bonuses and a commercial development bonus (up to 20% intensity, FAR, height or parking relief) for commercial developers who partner with a housing developer providing 30% low income or 15% very low income units; the chapter states the bonus does not waive any affordable housing fee. Housing Element: HCD issued a Notice of Violation on March 24, 2026 after three rounds of findings (drafts October 2024, August 2025, January 2026); the Board adopted the multi-jurisdictional 2024-2032 Housing Element on May 19, 2026 and HCD found it compliant in June 2026.
- AMI requirement: Government Code 65915 thresholds with recorded continued affordability (Section 18.66.070).
- Cycle: rolling (next: Requested with the land use application. Housing Element certification restores the County's eligibility for state funds (HHAP second disbursement, Prohousing, PLHA) that require compliance.)
- Layering notes: Merced County has no County housing finance program: the Community and Economic Development Department handles planning and the Housing Element, the Human Services Agency is Collaborative Applicant and Administrative Entity for the Merced City and County Continuum of Care (CA-520) and HHAP, Behavioral Health and Recovery Services holds BHSA, and the Housing Authority of the County of Merced (HACM) is the countywide PHA. The County is a non-entitlement jurisdiction for CDBG and HOME, has no housing trust fund, inclusionary ordinance, in-lieu fee or fee waiver, and Measure V (MCAG half-cent sales tax) is transportation only. The City of Merced is the entitlement jurisdiction with PLHA and Homekey awards (Pass 3). countyofmerced.com is Cloudflare-blocked to automated access (pages, DocumentCenter and AgendaCenter), so records were built from HCD tables and letters, mercedcoc.org reports, merced-pha.com, gocodebook (County Code Title 18) and press coverage; County documents are flagged for confirmation. The County's land use incentives follow state law with no local enhancement or fee relief; the two-year Housing Element delay had put state funding at risk until the May 2026 adoption. Source is Chapter 18.66 on gocodebook.com; Housing Element history from HCD's March 24, 2026 notice of violation letter.

### Monterey County

**Behavioral Health Services Act Integrated Plan FY 2026-29: housing interventions and capital development**
- Agency: Monterey County Health Department, Behavioral Health Bureau
- Type: other
- Typical award: Three-Year Integrated Plan and budget approved by the Board in June 2026 (Legistar file 26-552): Housing Interventions expenditures $9,025,327 (FY 2026-27), $9,296,089 (FY 2027-28) and $9,574,972 (FY 2028-29); capital development projects $1,130,582 rising to $1,199,435 a year (12.5% of the component); permanent rental subsidies $915,771 a year and interim rental subsidies $305,257 a year plus operating subsidies and a housing flex pool; 51.9% of housing funds dedicated to chronically homeless people; about 1,345 adults and 61 youth served a year.
- AMI requirement: BHSA eligible adults and youth with serious mental illness or substance use disorder; at least half of housing funds for chronically homeless people.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No NOFA; the Behavioral Health Bureau programs capital and subsidies through contracts. The June 2026 Board item also reported 22 prevention providers losing MHSA PEI funding under the BHSA restructuring.)
- Layering notes: Monterey County's Housing and Community Development Department (HCD, Craig Spencer, Director; Darby Marshall, Housing Program Manager) runs the Inclusionary Housing Ordinance, the Monterey County Housing Trust Fund, PLHA, the Urban County CDBG and HOME programs and County land dispositions; the County Administrative Office Homelessness Strategies and Initiatives Division (CAO-HSI) runs HHAP and the SHARE Center; the Health Department Behavioral Health Bureau holds BHSA; the Coalition of Homeless Services Providers leads the CoC (CA-506, shared with San Benito); and the Housing Authority of the County of Monterey (HACM) and its Development Corporation supply vouchers and develop housing. Salinas, Monterey, Seaside and other entitlement cities run their own programs (Pass 3). countyofmonterey.gov blocks automated access (pages and documents), so records were built from the Legistar web API client 'monterey' and its Granicus attachments, hamonterey.org, chsp.org, zoneomics and HCD tables. Monterey is budgeting roughly $1.1 million a year of BHSA capital, enough for a small PSH gap or acquisition contribution, plus about $900,000 a year of permanent rental subsidies that can be project-based; pair with HHAP-6 operating money and HACM vouchers. Source is the June 2026 Integrated Plan approval item (Legistar file 26-552) with the budget attachment.

**CDBG and HOME, Monterey Urban County (FY 2026-27 Annual Action Plan)**
- Agency: Monterey County Housing and Community Development Department (Urban County Standing Committee)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: FY 2026-27: CDBG $1,361,629 plus $173,385 carried over; HOME $403,480 ($399,969.98 per the CAPER) plus $612,500 carried from the first HOME year ($403,479.96 federal HOME, $96,520.04 state HOME to reach the $500,000 entitlement threshold, $112,500 County match). The Urban County (first designated 2013, more than $17 million of CDBG since) covers the unincorporated area, Gonzales, Greenfield, Marina and Sand City; three-year CDBG total estimated at $4,015,543 for 2025-2027. HOME, first received in 2025, is restricted to unincorporated areas and may fund new construction, acquisition, rehabilitation or rental assistance.
- AMI requirement: HOME income and rent limits with federal affordability periods; CDBG national objective.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Cycle: workshops on rating criteria (June, September and October 2026), applications due in winter, Urban County Standing Committee scoring in March (March 20, 2026 for FY 2026-27), public hearing and Board adoption of the Annual Action Plan in May (May 12, 2026), CAPER hearing in September. The FY 2027-28 round opens after the October 29, 2026 criteria workshop.)
- Layering notes: Monterey County's Housing and Community Development Department (HCD, Craig Spencer, Director; Darby Marshall, Housing Program Manager) runs the Inclusionary Housing Ordinance, the Monterey County Housing Trust Fund, PLHA, the Urban County CDBG and HOME programs and County land dispositions; the County Administrative Office Homelessness Strategies and Initiatives Division (CAO-HSI) runs HHAP and the SHARE Center; the Health Department Behavioral Health Bureau holds BHSA; the Coalition of Homeless Services Providers leads the CoC (CA-506, shared with San Benito); and the Housing Authority of the County of Monterey (HACM) and its Development Corporation supply vouchers and develop housing. HOME is new and small (about $400,000 a year, unincorporated only) and the County has not yet put it into a construction project; it is best viewed as a $400,000 to $1 million soft loan for a tax credit project in a community such as Castroville, Pajaro, Boronda or Chualar. Source is the April 30, 2026 Action Plan public hearing item (Legistar file RES 26-068) with funding recommendations attached; allocation figures from the March 20, 2026 Standing Committee item and the FY 2025-26 CAPER.

- Agency: Monterey County Housing and Community Development Department and County Administrative Office, Homelessness Strategies and Initiatives

Developers should watch Surplus Land Act notices and Board referrals on County property (1220 Natividad Road was referred for trust fund capitalization in 2024).)

**Density bonus and incentives (Chapter 21.65) and Sixth Cycle Housing Element implementation (by-right approvals, objective design standards)**
- Agency: Monterey County Housing and Community Development Department, Planning Services
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Chapter 21.65 (Ordinance 5177, 2011) implements state density bonus law up to a 35% bonus (5% very low income units earn 20%, rising by 2.5 points per additional percent) with incentives, parking reductions, child care and land donation bonuses; Section 21.65.110 states the bonus does not satisfy the Inclusionary Housing Ordinance and inclusionary units are computed on base plus bonus units. The Sixth Cycle (2023-2031) Housing Element Update and implementation package went to the Board on October 23, 2026 (ORD 26-021) after the Planning Commission on October 7, 2026, adding Chapter 18.20 objective design standards, a coastal by-right approval chapter (20.59), rezoning of housing sites and amendments to Titles 18, 20 and 21; HCD had found the third submittal (April 2026) still needing revisions.
- AMI requirement: Government Code 65915 thresholds with recorded qualifying unit agreements (Section 21.65.120); inclusionary obligations remain.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Requested with the land use application; confirm Housing Element adoption and HCD certification status (late 2026) because HHAP second disbursements and state program eligibility depend on it.)

**Inclusionary Housing Ordinance (Chapter 18.40), in-lieu fees and the 2026 fee-based restructuring proposal**
- Agency: Monterey County Housing and Community Development Department; Housing Advisory Committee
- Type: other
- Typical award: Current ordinance (first adopted 1980, revised 2003): 20% of units in residential projects affordable, analyzed by Keyser Marston as 6% very low, 6% low and 8% moderate income plus a 5% Workforce I component, with in-lieu fees for fractional units and small projects; in-lieu fees collected at building permit 2021-2025 are tabulated in the September 2026 workshop packet. The 2026 KMA In-Lieu Fee Analysis found maximum supportable fees of $73.90 to $305.60 per square foot of saleable area by product type. Staff recommended (Housing Advisory Committee workshops September 2 and 11, 2026) converting to an entirely fee-based program for projects under 250 units, charging 33 to 35 percent of the maximum fee on habitable square footage above the first 1,500 square feet, exempting all multifamily (duplex to fourplex and ADUs under 1,200 square feet), encouraging land donation for 20% affordable units on projects of 250 or more units, indexing the fee annually and allowing fee revenue to be spent countywide.
- AMI requirement: Very low (50% AMI), low (80% AMI), moderate (120% AMI) and Workforce I (to 150% AMI) for-sale and rental units with long-term resale and rent restrictions; inclusionary units are calculated on base plus density bonus units.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Board action on the restructured ordinance and new fee schedule expected late 2026 or 2027 after Housing Advisory Committee recommendations; LeSar and KMA remain under contract (Amendment 10, April 2026) to complete the fee studies. Until adoption the 2003 ordinance and existing in-lieu fees apply.)
- Layering notes: Monterey County's Housing and Community Development Department (HCD, Craig Spencer, Director; Darby Marshall, Housing Program Manager) runs the Inclusionary Housing Ordinance, the Monterey County Housing Trust Fund, PLHA, the Urban County CDBG and HOME programs and County land dispositions; the County Administrative Office Homelessness Strategies and Initiatives Division (CAO-HSI) runs HHAP and the SHARE Center; the Health Department Behavioral Health Bureau holds BHSA; the Coalition of Homeless Services Providers leads the CoC (CA-506, shared with San Benito); and the Housing Authority of the County of Monterey (HACM) and its Development Corporation supply vouchers and develop housing. Salinas, Monterey, Seaside and other entitlement cities run their own programs (Pass 3). countyofmonterey.gov blocks automated access (pages and documents), so records were built from the Legistar web API client 'monterey' and its Granicus attachments, hamonterey.org, chsp.org, zoneomics and HCD tables. The County concluded the inclusionary mandate produces few units (about five per project) and wants a reliable fee stream for the Housing Trust Fund, land acquisition and leverage of state programs; a fee-based ordinance would make market-rate permits the County's main local revenue source for affordable housing. Source is the September 11, 2026 Housing Advisory Committee workshop item (Legistar file 26-799) with the staff report and KMA analysis attached.
- Source: https://monterey.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=66610&GUID=0F8A38C3-633D-4775-A610-99E87DBFA2A1
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Monterey County Housing Trust Fund (MCHTF) loans for affordable rental development**
- Agency: Monterey County Housing and Community Development Department
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Established May 17, 2022 (Resolution 22-100) to replace redevelopment set-aside; by April 2023 the County had committed $10,033,984 of local cash, land assets and PLHA to capitalize it, including about $420,000 of PLHA delegated by Carmel-by-the-Sea, Greenfield and Soledad plus about $289,000 a year from those cities' later allocations. Loans: Greenfield Commons (EAH Housing, 100 units, Greenfield) $1,929,046 ($1,028,833 trust fund plus $900,213 PLHA) approved March 2023 and increased by $550,000 trust fund plus $9,982 PLHA on December 22, 2025 for cost overruns; Lightfighter Village (EAH and Veterans Transition Center, 71 units, Marina) $500,115. Loan origination fee corrected to 0.5%. The County applied to the state Local Housing Trust Fund matching program in 2022 (unsuccessful) and 2023 (up to $5 million match on $3,544,645 eligible local funds); a 2024 Board referral explored using 1220 Natividad Road to recapitalize the fund.
- AMI requirement: Lower income rental housing with 55-year County regulatory agreements; LHTF rules (3% simple maximum, predevelopment, acquisition, construction and permanent loans) when matched by state funds.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No standing NOFA; awards have gone by Board resolution to projects identified in the County's LHTF application. Contact HCD housing staff about trust fund and PLHA availability for 2027 tax credit applications; the pending inclusionary fee restructuring is intended to feed this fund.)
- Layering notes: Monterey County's Housing and Community Development Department (HCD, Craig Spencer, Director; Darby Marshall, Housing Program Manager) runs the Inclusionary Housing Ordinance, the Monterey County Housing Trust Fund, PLHA, the Urban County CDBG and HOME programs and County land dispositions; the County Administrative Office Homelessness Strategies and Initiatives Division (CAO-HSI) runs HHAP and the SHARE Center; the Health Department Behavioral Health Bureau holds BHSA; the Coalition of Homeless Services Providers leads the CoC (CA-506, shared with San Benito); and the Housing Authority of the County of Monterey (HACM) and its Development Corporation supply vouchers and develop housing. Salinas, Monterey, Seaside and other entitlement cities run their own programs (Pass 3). countyofmonterey.gov blocks automated access (pages and documents), so records were built from the Legistar web API client 'monterey' and its Granicus attachments, hamonterey.org, chsp.org, zoneomics and HCD tables. The trust fund is the County's gap lender of record but is thinly capitalized (two projects to date); its future depends on inclusionary in-lieu fee revenue and PLHA delegations from small cities. Source is the December 22, 2025 Board item (Legistar file 26-009) with the Greenfield Commons loan resolutions; establishment and LHTF history from files RES 22-100 and RES 23-073.
- Source: https://monterey.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=64104&GUID=BDC529D7-8D60-4518-9694-BC145F3D3E03
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA), County five-year plan including delegated city allocations**
- Agency: Monterey County Housing and Community Development Department (Darby Marshall, Housing Program Manager)
- Type: loan
- Typical award: First cycle (2019-2023) County estimate $3,890,280, raised to $5,631,969 after Carmel-by-the-Sea, Greenfield and Soledad delegated their allocations to the County in 2024; 2024-2028 formula allocations $455,119 (2024) and $487,499 (2025). Uses: rental development (Greenfield Commons $900,213 plus $9,982), homeless assistance passed to Social Services for the Salinas SHARE Center navigation center (up to $1,593,176) and case management ($790,275) through December 31, 2026, homeownership assistance, administration, and a $1,680,063 reserve for affordable owner-occupied workforce housing (AOWH) that the City of Soledad asked to use for a moderate-income for-sale land purchase (committee March 16, 2026). PLHA fund balance $1,191,927 at September 2026.
- AMI requirement: PLHA activities: rental development at or below 60% AMI (55 years), AOWH to 120% AMI with 45-year resale restrictions, homeless services.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No NOFA; PLHA is committed by Board resolution alongside Housing Trust Fund loans and by inter-departmental agreements for homeless services. The AOWH reserve is the only uncommitted development money identified; ask HCD about the 2026 and 2027 allocation years.)
- Layering notes: Monterey County's Housing and Community Development Department (HCD, Craig Spencer, Director; Darby Marshall, Housing Program Manager) runs the Inclusionary Housing Ordinance, the Monterey County Housing Trust Fund, PLHA, the Urban County CDBG and HOME programs and County land dispositions; the County Administrative Office Homelessness Strategies and Initiatives Division (CAO-HSI) runs HHAP and the SHARE Center; the Health Department Behavioral Health Bureau holds BHSA; the Coalition of Homeless Services Providers leads the CoC (CA-506, shared with San Benito); and the Housing Authority of the County of Monterey (HACM) and its Development Corporation supply vouchers and develop housing. Salinas, Monterey, Seaside and other entitlement cities run their own programs (Pass 3). countyofmonterey.gov blocks automated access (pages and documents), so records were built from the Legistar web API client 'monterey' and its Granicus attachments, hamonterey.org, chsp.org, zoneomics and HCD tables. PLHA has been split between homeless operations (SHARE Center) and the trust fund's Greenfield Commons loan; a developer with an unincorporated or small-city project should ask for PLHA and trust fund money together. Source is the September 21, 2026 PLHA budget amendment item (Legistar file 26-831); Soledad request from file 26-261 and 2024-2028 figures from HCD's PLHA NOFA tables.

**Project-Based Vouchers and development partnerships, Housing Authority of the County of Monterey**
- Agency: Housing Authority of the County of Monterey and HACM Development Corporation (Nora Ruvalcaba, Director of Development)
- Type: other
- Typical award: HACM administers Housing Choice Vouchers countywide (including Salinas) with a Housing Choice Voucher Administrative Plan effective September 28, 2026 that provides for competitive selection of PBV proposals (Chapter 17) and noncompetitive selection of PHA-owned units; recent PBV properties include Alfred Diaz-Infante Apartments (Salinas, 2024 list), One Parkside (Salinas), Villa del Monte (Monterey Peninsula), Magnolia Place Senior II (Greenfield) and South County sites; the last public PBV request for proposals was in 2020. The HACM Development Corporation develops and co-owns housing (Haciendas 1-4 and Tynan Village in Salinas, Rippling River in Carmel Valley with a 2006 County HOME and CDBG loan of $1,262,500, Benito Farm Labor Center in Soledad, Oak Park in Paso Robles) and in May 2026 reaffirmed Milestone Housing Group as development partner for its Greenfield sites (Resolution 3160).
- AMI requirement: PBV households at or below 50% AMI (75% extremely low income); farm labor housing for agricultural workers.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No open PBV RFP on hamonterey.org as of November 2026; contact the Director of Development about PBV commitments or a joint venture. HACM is not a Moving to Work agency.)
- Layering notes: Monterey County's Housing and Community Development Department (HCD, Craig Spencer, Director; Darby Marshall, Housing Program Manager) runs the Inclusionary Housing Ordinance, the Monterey County Housing Trust Fund, PLHA, the Urban County CDBG and HOME programs and County land dispositions; the County Administrative Office Homelessness Strategies and Initiatives Division (CAO-HSI) runs HHAP and the SHARE Center; the Health Department Behavioral Health Bureau holds BHSA; the Coalition of Homeless Services Providers leads the CoC (CA-506, shared with San Benito); and the Housing Authority of the County of Monterey (HACM) and its Development Corporation supply vouchers and develop housing. HACM is both the voucher source and a frequent co-developer, so a third-party project should approach it early for PBVs or partnership; its portfolio shows the county's typical stack of PBVs, County HOME and CDBG loans and tax credits.
