**Western Nevada County Regional Housing Trust Fund (WNCRHTF) gap loans**
- Agency: Nevada County Housing and Community Services Department (with City of Grass Valley and Nevada City)
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Formed July 14, 2020 (Resolutions 20-315, 20-316, 20-317) by the County, Grass Valley and Nevada City, capitalized with PLHA, a state Local Housing Trust Fund (LHTF) matching grant awarded February 3, 2021, HHSA Intergovernmental Transfer funds, ARPA and a $1 million General Fund commitment repurposed as match in 2025. Awards to date: Cashin's Field (51 units, Nevada City, Resolution 21-116 loan, completed January 2023); Lone Oak Senior Apartments II (31 units, Penn Valley, Pacific West Communities) $1,024,775 under RFP 3 (Resolution 23-227, May 23, 2023) plus $1,281,373 (Resolution 25-167, May 13, 2025) for a $2,306,128 loan approved May 19, 2026 (Resolution 26-196); Habitat for Humanity Ventana Sierra (owner-occupied, May 2023 RFP).
- AMI requirement: Low income rental or owner-occupied housing; the Lone Oak II loan is an unsecured deferred loan repaid from residual receipts with affordability tied to the TCAC regulatory agreement. LHTF rules (lower income, 55-year rental covenants, 3% simple maximum) apply to the state-matched share.
- Cycle: closed/inactive (next: No open RFP. Resolution 24-121 lets HCS pre-select LHTF projects by RFP and publish preliminary awards on the trust fund page when a new LHTF round opens. The June 16, 2026 PLHA plan amendment states LHTF match is no longer available, so PLHA Rounds 4 and 5 will fund projects directly rather than through the trust fund. Check nevadacountyca.gov/3926 for any new RFP.)
- Layering notes: Nevada County's Housing and Community Services Department (HCS, Tyler Barrington, Director, 950 Maidu Ave, Nevada City, 530-265-1645, Housing@nevadacountyca.gov) sits inside the Health and Human Services Agency and administers the Western Nevada County Regional Housing Trust Fund, PLHA (as delegated applicant for Grass Valley and Nevada City), state HOME and CDBG awards, the MORE mobile home program and the new Down Payment Assistance Program; the Behavioral Health and Homelessness Department (Cari Yardley, Director) holds Homekey+, BHSA and the Truckee Navigation Center; HHAP is administered by the Homeless Resource Council of the Sierras as the CoC CA-531 lead; and the Regional Housing Authority (Sutter, Nevada, Yuba, Colusa and Lake counties, Yuba City) supplies vouchers. Grass Valley, Nevada City and Truckee run their own programs (Pass 3). The County has no inclusionary ordinance, no housing impact fee and no affordable housing fee waiver program; the only land use incentive is the state density bonus ordinance. nevadacountyca.gov opens directly and the Legistar web API client 'nevco' exposes staff reports and attachments at legistar.granicus.com/nevco/attachments/. The trust fund is the County's only local gap source and has been used as the TCAC local contribution that moved Lone Oak II from unfunded to a December 2025 4% award. Expect small amounts ($1 million to $2.3 million) and a Board resolution for each loan. Pair with state HOME (Lone Oak II received $11,041,628) and CMFA or CSCDA conduit bonds. Future awards are likely to come as direct PLHA loans scored jointly with the two cities instead of trust fund loans.
- Source: https://www.nevadacountyca.gov/3926/Western-Nevada-County-Regional-Housing-T
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)


### Orange County

**Apartment Development Revenue Bond Program, County of Orange conduit issuer**
- Agency: County Executive Office, Public Finance (Louis McClure, 714-834-5999, Luis.McClure@ocgov.com) with the Public Financing Advisory Committee (PFAC) and Board of Supervisors
- Type: bond
- Typical award: Tax-exempt multifamily bonds for new construction or acquisition-rehab anywhere in Orange County; a typical bond loan is $7,000,000 or more with issuance costs of about $300,000, 30-year amortization, credit enhanced (Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac, bank letter of credit) or privately placed at County discretion. Borrower pays all issuance and finance team costs. No recent County issuances were located; most Orange County deals use CMFA or CSCDA (for example Valencia Garden in Orange, CMFA $18,499,022).
- AMI requirement: At least 20% of units for very low income households, or 10% very low plus 30% at 60% AMI, for the longer of 15 years or the life of the bonds (about 30 years); County loans may be added for deeper affordability.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Apply after site control and a lender commitment: PFAC approves the financing concept and team, the Board holds the TEFRA hearing and adopts the inducement resolution, then the County applies to CDLAC; bonds must close within 90 days of the CDLAC award. Call the CEO public finance contact before preparing an application.)
- Layering notes: The County keeps a conduit program on paper (Attachment H to the 2026 Supportive Housing NOFA, opened) but the pipeline shows cities and developers using CMFA and CSCDA instead; expect PFAC review to add six to eight weeks versus a JPA issuer. Bonds alone do not trigger state prevailing wage; County loans in the same deal do. Confirm current issuer fees with the CEO, as none are published.
- Source: https://housing.oc.gov/sites/hcd/files/2026-03/Attachment%20H%20-%20Apartment%20Development%20Revenue%20Bond%20Program.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Development fee waiver for affordable housing (no countywide program)**
- Agency: OC Public Works, OC Development Services
- Type: fee waiver
- Typical award: None adopted. The County has no impact fee waiver or deferral program for affordable housing in the unincorporated area. The 2026 Supportive Housing NOFA treats city waivers of impact, school, sanitation and mitigation fees as eligible local match, and County loans may pay building permit fees as an eligible cost.
- AMI requirement: Not applicable.
- Cycle: closed/inactive (next: Not applicable. Ask the host city for fee waivers to satisfy the County NOFA match requirement.)
- Layering notes: Negative finding. Unincorporated projects pay full County, school district and sanitation district fees; the Affordable Housing Permit carries a processing deposit. Labor Code 1720(b)(4) counts any waived fee as public funds for prevailing wage purposes.
- Source: https://housing.oc.gov/sites/hcd/files/2026-03/2026%20NOFA%2003.06.26.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**HOME, CDBG and ESG, Orange County Urban County program**
- Agency: OC Housing and Community Development, Community Development Division; Craig Fee, Community Development Manager, 714-480-2996
- Type: grant
- Typical award: FY 2026-27 Annual Action Plan: CDBG $3,467,220 annual allocation plus $20,000 program income and $806,834 prior-year resources ($4,294,054 available); HOME $1,244,582.84 plus $135,000 program income; ESG $302,608. Five-year ConPlan FY 2025-29 expects about $12,900,000 CDBG and $4,100,000 HOME over the remaining years. HOME housing development dollars are awarded only through the Supportive Housing NOFA; CDBG goes mainly to public facilities, public services and housing rehabilitation through the annual Request for Applications (generally due in November under the Funding Allocation Policy and Process).
- AMI requirement: HUD income limits (80% AMI, HOME rents); the County's NOFA restricts HOME-assisted PSH units to 30% AMI for 55 years. CDBG new construction only by a CBDO.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: FY 2027-28 Funding Allocation Policy and Process posted Oct 2026; the RFA for CDBG, ESG and HOME public service and facility projects is generally due in November with the Action Plan draft out for 30-day comment in March and Board adoption in spring. Housing development applicants should use the Supportive Housing NOFA instead.)
- Layering notes: Small program for a county of 3.2 million because most cities are their own entitlements. Urban County members: unincorporated area plus Brea, Cypress, Dana Point, Laguna Beach, Laguna Hills, Laguna Woods, La Palma, Los Alamitos, San Juan Capistrano, Seal Beach, Stanton, Villa Park and the metro cities Placentia and Yorba Linda; Anaheim, Santa Ana, Irvine, Garden Grove, Huntington Beach and 15 other cities run their own CDBG and HOME (Pass 3). The County is not the HOPWA grantee. The County's HOME-ARP allocation was folded into the Supportive Housing NOFA rather than offered separately. Davis-Bacon and state prevailing wage apply. Pages and the FY 2026-27 Action Plan PDF opened on housing.oc.gov.
- Source: https://housing.oc.gov/community-development
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Housing Opportunities Overlay (Zoning Code 7-9-44), Affordable Housing Permit (7-9-124.3) and density bonus (7-9-87)**
- Agency: OC Public Works, OC Development Services, Planning Division (Deysi Munoz, 714-667-8815, Deysi.munoz@ocpw.ocgov.com)
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: The 2024 Housing Opportunities Overlay Manual allows 100% affordable rental or ownership projects, emergency shelters, multi-service centers and low-barrier navigation centers by right in commercial and industrial districts and on listed residential arterial parcels in the unincorporated area, approved through a ministerial Affordable Housing Permit. Qualified projects must be 100% affordable to low and very low income households (at or below 80% AMI) with at least 30% of units for very low income households; the County NOFA's Attachment J lists eligible sites. Density bonus (Sec. 7-9-87.1 to 87.4) follows Government Code 65915 for bonus amounts, incentives, concessions and waivers; the Mixed-Use district allows 12 feet of extra height as a bonus incentive. No county inclusionary housing ordinance or in-lieu fee.
- AMI requirement: Overlay projects: 100% affordable, 70% low income and 30% very low income, with recorded covenants; density bonus units per state law (55 years for rental).
- Cycle: rolling (next: Not applicable; ministerial. Unincorporated communities include Midway City, Rossmoor, North Tustin, Ladera Ranch, Rancho Mission Viejo, Silverado and El Modena. Confirm site eligibility with OC Development Services; processing fee deposit applies.)
- Layering notes: The overlay matters because most of the County's own land-use authority covers small pockets; the 2026 NOFA gives unincorporated overlay sites the first 60-day application window and the higher $137,500 per unit subsidy. Density bonus and by-right approval do not trigger prevailing wage. Manual PDF opened on ocds.ocpublicworks.com; zoning code mirror on Zoneomics shows section headings and overlay affordability terms.
- Source: https://ocds.ocpublicworks.com/sites/ocpwocds/files/2024-08/Orange%20County%20Housing%20Opportunties%20Overlay%20Manual%202024%20.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Housing Successor Low and Moderate Income Housing Asset Fund (Orange County Housing Authority as Housing Successor to the Orange County Development Agency)**
- Agency: Orange County Housing Authority as Housing Successor, administered by OC Housing and Community Development
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Housing Asset Fund balance $29,240,000 at June 30, 2024 (FY 2023-24 SB 341 report). Funds are offered only through the County's Supportive Housing NOFAs: $4,000,000 added to the 2016 PSH NOFA in April 2018; 2020 NOFA awards of $2,400,000 to Clara Vista (former Tahiti Motel, Stanton, 60 PSH units, completed Dec 2023) and $453,600 each to Mountain View (Lake Forest, 71 units) and Orchard View Gardens (Buena Park, 66 units); no Housing Successor funds were in the 2023 NOFA, but they are a named source in the 2026 NOFA.
- AMI requirement: Health and Safety Code 34176.1: households at or below 80% AMI, at least 30% of spending over five years for extremely low income households, 55-year covenants; excess surplus rules force spending when the unencumbered balance exceeds the greater of $1,000,000 or four years of deposits.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Request through the open 2026 Supportive Housing NOFA. The FY 2024-25 SB 341 report link on the County page returned 404 on 2026-11-06; the FY 2023-24 report was read.)
- Layering notes: A large idle balance (about $29,000,000) that the County must spend to avoid excess surplus findings, so it is the most flexible local money in the NOFA pool: no federal strings, 30% AMI target through the NOFA. Use it for sites outside HOME geography or where Davis-Bacon is a problem. Public funds, so state prevailing wage applies. Housing successor page and FY 2023-24 report PDF opened on housing.oc.gov.
- Source: https://housing.oc.gov/housing-development/orange-county-housing-authority-housing-successor
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**MHSA/BHSA permanent supportive housing capital and Capitalized Operating Subsidy Reserve (Orange County Health Care Agency through OC Community Resources)**
- Agency: OC Health Care Agency, Behavioral Health Services (MHSA/BHSA Housing Program), with OC Housing and Community Development administering loans
- Type: loan
- Typical award: BHSA Integrated Plan FY 2026-29 (Board May 19, 2026): Housing Interventions component $73,500,000 a year; planned capital development $18,375,000 a year (the 25% statutory maximum) plus about $990,508 from other sources; rental subsidies $14,000,000 a year; operating subsidies $6,787,829 a year; $36,750,000 a year reserved for chronically homeless people. Named capital pipeline: Mercury Senior Apartments (Brea), Lampson Workforce Housing (Los Alamitos) and projects to be determined in San Juan Capistrano, Anaheim Hills, Buena Park and Fountain Valley, 56 BHSA units of 416 at about $208,000 per BHSA unit. Since June 2018 the County has completed 476 MHSA units with 196 in the pipeline toward a goal of 2,396 PSH units by 2029; each MHSA dollar leveraged about $5.50. HCA also granted $20,500,000 of MHSA and General Fund to the Orange County Housing Finance Trust. Loan limit $207,235 per regulated unit for capital and another $207,235 for COSR under the 2026 NOFA.
- AMI requirement: Extremely low income (30% AMI) adults experiencing homelessness with a serious mental disorder (WIC 5600.3(b), BHSA eligibility under WIC 5830(a)), certified by HCA and referred through CES; rents at 30% of the 30% AMI limit or 30% of SSI; no minimum income where PBVs or COSR are provided; MHSA/BHSA units at most 49% of the project; units at least 450 square feet with full kitchens and furnishings; 55-year covenant.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Apply through the 2026 Supportive Housing NOFA (open since March 6, 2026) with an HCA review meeting before submission. The three-year plan runs July 1, 2026 to June 30, 2029 with annual updates; HCA moves money to OCCR under a three-year spending plan, so NOFA amounts rise when new BHSA allocations post.)
- Layering notes: Orange County's BHSA program is the deepest county PSH source in the 13 counties researched: $18,400,000 a year in planned capital plus a COSR that replaces PBVs when none are available (zero interest, forgivable over 15 to 20 years, sized to cover MHSA unit operating deficits). Pair the capital with 4% credits and OCHA or city PBVs; use COSR when PBVs are refused. MHSA capital alone can avoid Davis-Bacon but state prevailing wage still applies. Terms are in Attachment D of the 2026 NOFA (opened); budget figures from the Integrated Plan PDF on ochealthinfo.com (opened). HCA does not run its own developer NOFA.
- Source: https://housing.oc.gov/sites/hcd/files/2026-03/Attachment%20D%20-%20MHSA%20BHSA%20Term%20Sheet.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Orange County 2026 Supportive Housing NOFA (HOME, HOME-ARP, MHSA/BHSA, 15G Reserves, Housing Successor funds)**
- Agency: OC Housing and Community Development (OC Community Resources), 1501 E. St. Andrew Place, Santa Ana; Michelle Zdeba, Housing Development Manager, 714-480-2994; Sherluna Vien 714-480-2936; OCHCD.NOFA@occr.ocgov.com
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Up to $20,900,000 in loans authorized by the Board Feb 10, 2026 and released March 6, 2026 (OpenGov NOFA 2026-01), plus up to 100 Housing Choice, Mainstream or VASH project-based vouchers. Replaces the 2025 NOFA ($12,050,000 and up to 150 PBVs) and the 2023 NOFA ($67,100,000 authorized, $32,700,000 and 218 PBVs in Amendment 1). Maximum County subsidy per assisted unit: $137,500 (any bedroom size) in the unincorporated area and Urban County participating cities; $95,000 (0BR), $100,000 (1BR), $105,000 (2BR+) in all other cities. MHSA/BHSA capital limit $207,235 per regulated unit plus up to $207,235 per unit for a capitalized operating subsidy reserve. Minimum five assisted units; total development cost above TCAC's $650,000 per unit threshold needs justification.
- AMI requirement: All County-assisted units restricted to households at or below 30% AMI who are experiencing homelessness, filled through the Coordinated Entry System, with a 55-year regulatory agreement from the qualified project period. MHSA/BHSA units serve adults with serious mental illness and may not exceed 49% of project units. Projects outside the unincorporated area must show host-city financial support at least equal to the County request (land, fee waivers and infrastructure count) and accept a RHNA transfer or credit to the County.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Open first come, first served until funds are committed or a new NOFA replaces it. Geographic tiers: March 6 to May 4, 2026 unincorporated area and Urban County cities only; May 5 to June 3 added entitlement cities except Santa Ana, Anaheim and Garden Grove; from June 4, 2026 any eligible location. Allow at least eight months from a complete application to Board approval. PBVs only for sites in OCHA jurisdiction.)
- Layering notes: The County's only capital program and it is PSH only: every assisted unit must be 30% AMI and CES-referred, so it fits a 4% or 9% deal with a PSH set-aside, not a general affordable project. Terms: 3% simple interest, 55 years, residual receipts split 50% to public lenders pro rata and 50% to the developer, monitoring fees of $500 setup plus $3,795 a year plus $22 per unit, replacement reserves $250 to $500 per unit, partnership management fee up to $25,000. Davis-Bacon applies at 12 or more HOME-assisted units or nine or more PBV units; projects using only MHSA/BHSA or Housing Successor money may avoid Davis-Bacon but state prevailing wage applies to public funds. HOME-ARP, 15G Reserves and Housing Successor dollars are pooled here rather than offered separately. OCHA PBVs can be requested with no capital. Homekey+ projects vetted through the County RFI skip the geographic tiers. NOFA, exhibits and sample loan documents are posted on the NOFA page (opened 2026-11-06).
- Source: https://housing.oc.gov/housing-development/developer/notice-funding-availability
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Orange County Housing Finance Trust (OCHFT) permanent supportive and affordable housing loans**
- Agency: Orange County Housing Finance Trust, joint powers authority of the County of Orange and 27 cities (9-member board, 4 County and 5 city seats), administered by County staff
- Type: loan
- Typical award: 2024 NOFA (due March 15, 2024, availability extended by Addendum 2 through July 2026, now fully committed): $25,700,502 from County MHSA ($12,371,809), County General Fund ($1,000,000) and state Local Housing Trust Fund matching grants ($12,328,693), split in thirds across North, Central and South service planning areas, project cap $3,500,000. Awards 2020 to 2024: $55,035,131 to 32 project awards funding 441 Trust-restricted units in 2,477 total units (2024 round $22,143,076 for 152 units across 10 projects including Cypress Village Irvine $3,500,000 and Aspan Court Lake Forest $3,490,000). June 30, 2025 audit: notes receivable $25,013,554, net position $54,579,510; FY 2024-25 deposits $4,000,000 MHSA, $1,200,000 County, $3,000,000 CalOptima, $402,747 LHTF. $250,000 federal community project funding presented April 2026. Goal 2,396 PSH units by 2029.
- AMI requirement: Extremely low, very low and low income households and people experiencing homelessness; MHSA units for homeless adults with serious mental illness; LHTF-funded units at or below 60% AMI per state rules; 55-year covenant. Projects must be in the unincorporated area or a member city, and LHTF dollars only in cities compliant with state housing law; MHSA-only requests may be anywhere in the county.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: No open NOFA as of Nov 2026; the 2024 NOFA has no funds remaining and no 2025 or 2026 round was posted. Watch the Trust's NOFA page and board agendas; rounds have opened each spring since 2020 when state LHTF matches were awarded. An Affordable ADU Loan Program is also offered.)
- Layering notes: Regional JPA recorded at county level because the County seeded it and sits on its board; it is the vehicle that captured $12,300,000 of state LHTF matches for Orange County. Terms mirror the County NOFA: 3% simple, 55 years from conversion, 50% residual receipts, TCAC developer fee limits, $25,000 partnership management fee cap. Awards are small ($500,000 to $3,500,000) and used as last-in gap money on 4% and 9% deals, often alongside County MHSA loans and OCHA PBVs. Member cities: Aliso Viejo, Anaheim, Buena Park, Costa Mesa, Dana Point, Fountain Valley, Fullerton, Garden Grove, Huntington Beach, Irvine, La Habra, Laguna Beach, Laguna Hills, Laguna Niguel, Lake Forest, Los Alamitos, Mission Viejo, Newport Beach, Orange, Placentia, San Juan Capistrano, Santa Ana, Seal Beach, Stanton, Tustin, Westminster and Yorba Linda. Public funds trigger state prevailing wage. NOFA page, developments page, member list and 2025 audit opened; the 2024 NOFA PDF is hosted on Squarespace.
- Source: https://www.ochft.org/notice-of-funding-availability
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA), Orange County Urban County entitlement**
- Agency: OC Housing and Community Development (HCD entitlement grantee for the Urban County)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HCD 2024-2028 Formula Allocation NOFA: Orange County CY 2024 $892,973, CY 2025 $956,505, five-year estimate $5,581,081. The County's PLHA Five-Year Plan (2020) directs 88.55% of each year's allocation to homeless activities under PLHA 301(a)(6): 45.55% to the Buena Park and Placentia navigation centers through a North SPA collaborative treasured by the City of La Habra, 32.79% to the Yale Transitional Center in Santa Ana for the unincorporated area, 3.54% to the Laguna Beach Alternative Sleeping Location and 6.67% to Dana Point outreach and rapid rehousing; the remaining 11.45% is for rental housing development under 301(a)(1).
- AMI requirement: PLHA statute: rental development for households at or below 80% AMI (120% workforce allowed) with 55-year covenants; the County plan's development share has not been offered through a developer NOFA.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: HCD formula allocations arrive annually; no County PLHA NOFA to developers exists. Ask OCHCD whether the plan was amended for the state's 2024 requirement that 40% of new PLHA fund owner-occupied workforce housing, and whether the 11.45% development share is folded into the Supportive Housing NOFA.)
- Layering notes: Not a capital source in practice: under $1,000,000 a year and almost all of it pays for shelter and navigation center operations. Anaheim, Santa Ana, Irvine and other entitlement cities receive their own PLHA (Pass 3). Plan PDF and community development page opened on housing.oc.gov; amounts from the HCD 2024-2028 PLHA NOFA.
- Source: https://housing.oc.gov/community-development
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Project-Based Vouchers, Orange County Housing Authority (OCHA)**
- Agency: Orange County Housing Authority (OC Community Resources), 1501 E. St. Andrew Place, Santa Ana, 714-480-2700; PBV requests through OC Housing and Community Development NOFA staff
- Type: other
- Typical award: 26 PBV projects listed on the OCHA site total 693 PBV units (for example Stanton Inn 71, Oasis Huntington Beach 62, Tahiti 59, Ascent 57, Avon River 48, Casa Paloma 48, Crossroads at Washington 43, Salida Del Sol 40). The 2026 Supportive Housing NOFA offers up to 100 Housing Choice, Mainstream or VASH PBVs (150 under the 2025 NOFA; 218 under the 2023 NOFA Amendment 1). Pipeline as of Feb 2026: Cartwright Irvine (8 PBV, 60 units, May 2026), Avon River Costa Mesa (14 PBV plus 10 VASH, 78 units), Orion Orange (3 PBV, 166 units), Lincoln Avenue Buena Park (13 PBV, 55 units, Nov 2026), Casa Colibri Midway City (30 PBV, 71 units, Dec 2026). Total OCHA voucher count was not confirmed from an opened document.
- AMI requirement: Extremely low income households experiencing homelessness referred through the Coordinated Entry System; VASH units need VA eligibility; many projects are senior (62 plus) or MHSA/NPLH restricted. Tenants pay 30% of income. HAP contracts up to 20 years with a 20-year extension option.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Request PBVs through the open 2026 Supportive Housing NOFA (OpenGov 2026-01); PBVs are available only for sites in OCHA jurisdiction (the county except Anaheim, Santa Ana and Garden Grove, which run their own authorities) and may be limited by federal funding changes. OCHA posts PBV waitlist openings per project; the 2027 payment standards were posted Nov 2026.)
- Layering notes: OCHA PBVs are the standard operating subsidy for Orange County PSH and are awarded with or without County capital under the same NOFA; the 2026 NOFA lets the County claim part of the NOI the PBVs create for services. A HAP contract is not a prevailing wage trigger but Davis-Bacon applies to new construction or rehab of nine or more PBV units, and PBVs bring NEPA and a HUD subsidy layering review. Four PHAs operate in the county (OCHA, Anaheim, Santa Ana, Garden Grove); their PBV pipelines are tracked by the CoC Housing Opportunities Committee. OCHA is not a Moving to Work agency. Sources opened: OCHA project-based page, 2026 NOFA Section III, Feb 11, 2026 Housing Opportunities Committee slides.
- Source: https://housingauthority.oc.gov/project-based-0
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Santa Ana, Anaheim/Orange County Continuum of Care (CA-602) and HHAP**
- Agency: County of Orange, County Executive Office, Office of Care Coordination (CoC lead agency and HHAP administrative entity); Orange County CoC Board
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HHAP by round, County of Orange: R1 $7,486,576, R2 $3,422,435, R3 $9,582,817, R4 $7,131,233, R5 $8,346,727, R6 $8,355,062 (agreement through June 30, 2030). Orange County CoC: R1 $8,081,116, R2 $3,823,692, R3 $10,267,304, R4 $7,600,842, R5 $8,699,841, R6 $8,923,350; Anaheim, Santa Ana and Irvine each $5,947,855 in R6 for a regional R6 total of $35,100,000 awarded April 2026. State accountability site: $152,700,000 HHAP rounds 1 to 5 to the Orange County area, $36,000,000 directly to the County ($28,900,000 obligated, $15,300,000 spent by mid-2025); Encampment Resolution Funding $8,700,000 area, $3,600,000 County. HHAP-6 CoC budget: $3,976,421 permanent housing operating subsidies, $592,335 youth operating subsidies, $1,552,663 rapid rehousing, $1,222,663 interim housing operating subsidies, $330,000 youth interim, $624,635 services, $624,635 administration and HMIS. HUD CoC FY 2024 awards to CA-602 included $1,249,326 Permanent Housing Collaborative, $731,787 rapid rehousing and $528,250 Jackson Aisle TRA (partial list). 2024 PIT: 7,322 people, 4,173 unsheltered, 9,751 year-round beds. Homekey+: County RFI (due Nov 27, 2024) qualified Volunteers of America, National CORE, California Supportive Housing, American Family Housing and Weingart Center as development partners.
- AMI requirement: People experiencing or at risk of homelessness per HUD and state definitions; CoC permanent housing through Coordinated Entry; HHAP-6 obligation and expenditure deadlines (75% obligated and 50% spent by June 30, 2027). HHAP-5 regional plan governs HHAP-6 uses.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: HHAP-6 budget approved by the CoC Board in 2025 with Board of Supervisors action Jan 13, 2026; HHAP-7 is open to HHAP-6 grantees per the state. The CoC runs the HUD NOFO competition each summer and the Housing Opportunities Committee meets every other month (next listed April 8, 2026 at OCHA). Homekey+ sites move through the County RFI and the Supportive Housing NOFA.)
- Layering notes: HHAP in Orange County pays operating subsidies for permanent and interim housing, rapid rehousing and services; it is an operating partner for PSH, not a capital lender. The region's HHAP is split five ways (County, CoC, Anaheim, Santa Ana, Irvine), so a project inside one of those cities should approach that city's share. County Homekey and Homekey+ sites (Stanton Inn, Tahiti, Casa Paloma and the RFI list) then take OCHA PBVs and MHSA/BHSA loans. Figures from the July 23, 2025 CoC Board presentation (ceo.oc.gov, opened), the Feb 11, 2026 Housing Opportunities Committee slides, HCD HHAP-6 allocations and accountability.ca.gov.
- Source: https://ceo.oc.gov/sites/ceo/files/2025-07/Presentation_Revised%20Final%2025.07.23%20-%20CoC%20Board%20Meeting.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Welfare Exemption (property tax), Orange County Assessor**
- Agency: Orange County Assessor, Claude Parrish, 500 S. Main Street, Orange; Institutional Exemptions unit 714-834-2779; main line 714-834-2727
- Type: tax abatement
- Typical award: Full or partial exemption of property tax on units occupied by households at or below 80% AMI under Revenue and Taxation Code 214(g); worth roughly 1.1% or more of assessed value a year depending on the tax rate area. The Assessor posts current BOE-267 series forms (2026 BOE-267-R is online).
- AMI requirement: Nonprofit or LP with eligible nonprofit managing general partner holding a BOE Organizational Clearance Certificate; units restricted by recorded regulatory agreement or occupied by households at or below 80% AMI; BOE-267 first filing and BOE-267-L, L1 or L2 supplemental affidavits annually by Feb 15 (late claims receive a reduced exemption under Rev. and Tax. Code 270).
- Cycle: rolling (next: 2027-02-15 (annual claim deadline for the 2027-28 roll). File the first claim on acquisition so the construction period is covered.)
- Layering notes: Standard in every tax credit operating budget; underwrite only the restricted units and confirm the managing GP's OCC before closing. The Assessor's institutional exemptions page describes the welfare exemption in general terms only; the exemptions unit phone comes from the Assessor's contact page (both opened). The BOE county contacts page lists the same office.
- Source: https://www.ocassessor.gov/tax-saving-programs/institutional-exemptions
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)


### Placer County

**Affordable Housing and Employee Accommodation Ordinance (County Code Chapter 15.65): 10% inclusionary requirement, in-lieu fee and employee accommodation fee**
- Agency: Placer County Community Development Resource Agency (entitlement review) and Economic Development and Housing Division (fee collection and use); nexus study update by BAE Urban Economics under a February 4, 2025 contract
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Regulatory requirement and the County's only local housing revenue. Adopted October 2020, effective January 1, 2021: private residential projects of more than 100 units must make 10% of units affordable; projects of 8 to 100 units pay an in-lieu fee per habitable square foot of market-rate units (initially $2.00, indexed, reported at $2.73 on the County's program page); projects of 7 or fewer units and projects in infill or transit areas are exempt. An employee accommodation fee at the same per-square-foot rate applies to new non-residential development in the Sierra Nevada and Lake Tahoe areas. Fees fund construction, preservation and acquisition-rehab of affordable housing. Large specific plans (Bickford Ranch, Placer One) have generated the in-lieu balances now lent to Hope Way and proposed for Destination at Placer One.
- AMI requirement: 10% of units at affordable levels defined in the ordinance for projects over 100 units, with the in-lieu alternative below that size; state density bonus law applies to projects providing affordable units. The updated nexus study (draft December 2025) supports higher fees and ordinance amendments scheduled for Board consideration in 2026.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Applies at entitlement (Affordable Housing Employee Accommodation Entitlement Review Form) with fees paid at building permit. Board adoption of the nexus study and fee or ordinance amendments was planned for spring 2026 and was still pending in the records reviewed; confirm the current fee with the Planning Division before underwriting.)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. For a 100% affordable project the ordinance is not a cost; for a mixed-income project over 100 units it is a 10% set-aside, and between 8 and 100 units a fee of roughly $2.73 per square foot (about $2,700 on a 1,000 square foot unit), which is low relative to Bay Area counties. The fee revenue is the pool behind the County's gap loans, so a higher fee from the 2026 nexus update would expand the only local source. The County's exemptions for infill and transit-adjacent projects remove most of western Placer's multifamily sites from the requirement. Density bonus and inclusionary agreements do not trigger prevailing wage.
- Source: https://www.publicceo.com/?p=64372
- Confidence: needs_confirmation (researched 2026-11-06)

**County affordable housing gap loans funded by Affordable Housing in-lieu fees (Hope Way and Destination at Placer One commitments)**
- Agency: Placer County Executive Office, Economic Development and Housing Division (Gloria Stearns, Director; Nikki Streegan, Housing Manager); 145 Fulweiler Avenue #100, Auburn; (530) 886-2642; housing@placer.ca.gov
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Project-by-project Board commitments, no NOFA. On August 25, 2026 the Board approved a Funding Commitment Letter for Hope Way Apartments Phase 1 (Penryn 721, L.P., an affiliate of USA Properties Fund; 132 units of a 240-unit entitlement at 3130 Penryn Road) for a loan of up to $7,260,000 at 3% simple interest over 55 years, funded by affordable housing in-lieu fees (reported as Bickford Ranch in-lieu fees), and a Preliminary Funding Letter for Destination at Placer One (360 units north of Roseville) framing a potential $8,900,000 loan at 3% simple interest for which no funding source has been identified. Earlier County loans include Sabre City (Parkside at Sabre City, 44 homes with ADUs, Housing Trust Placer) and $500,000 in 2025 to a North Lake Tahoe housing trust.
- AMI requirement: Set in each Funding Agreement and Use Deed Restriction: tax credit income tiers (30% to 80% AMI), 55-year term, and the Placer County Affordable Housing Priority Applicant Policy (adopted May 12, 2026, effective July 1, 2026) giving preference to current residents, local workers and past residents of Placer County or the Tahoe Truckee Unified School District area for new deed-restricted units.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No NOFA. Developers request a commitment letter through the Economic Development and Housing Division and the Board acts on each project; Hope Way's Funding Agreement, Deed of Trust and deed restriction follow the commitment letter, and both 2026 projects still must win tax credits. The Board's Ad Hoc Committee for Housing Funding and Fee Implementation is working on new funding sources, and the nexus study update that would raise the in-lieu fee was due to the Board in 2026.)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. Placer County's only gap money is the pool of in-lieu fees collected under its 2020 Affordable Housing and Employee Accommodation Ordinance, and the Board has committed most of it: Hope Way Phase 1 ($7.26 million) exhausted the identified balance, and Destination at Placer One is waiting on a source (a supervisor noted the County has never used General Fund for affordable housing loans). Terms are 3% simple, 55 years, residual receipts style repayment. Expect Board politics: Hope Way needed appeals, a 3-2 vote in December 2025 and a lawsuit settlement cutting it to 132 units with a four-year pause on the rest. The County's 2025-2029 Housing Action Plan targets 1,300 units by mid-2029, and the Prohousing Designation (reapplying fall 2026) gives projects state scoring advantages. Cities (Roseville, Rocklin, Lincoln, Auburn) run their own funds and are Pass 3.
- Source: https://ceqanet.lci.ca.gov/2026090089
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**County-owned land for affordable housing: Dollar Creek Crossing (3205 North Lake Boulevard, Tahoe City)**
- Agency: Placer County Executive Office, Economic Development and Housing, with the Community Development Resource Agency; developer Related California (Related Norcal Development, LLC) under a preliminary agreement
- Type: other
- Typical award: County-owned parcels bought in 2019 across from the 7-Eleven on North Lake Boulevard. After seven years of mixed-use and mixed-income concepts, the Board directed an affordable-only project of at least 80 lower income units in April 2025; the developer's June 2026 financial report says the County would need to contribute about $18,500,000 (land plus gap) for feasibility, and the County has not identified a funding source. The preliminary agreement was set to expire at the end of June 2026; if it lapses the County must issue a new RFP no later than July 2027.
- AMI requirement: Low income (at or below 80% AMI) rental housing, 80 units, with the County's Affordable Housing Priority Applicant Policy for local workers and residents.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Watch for a new RFP by July 2027 if the Related agreement expired; the County's Ad Hoc Committee for Housing Funding and Fee Implementation is tasked with finding the $18.5 million (supervisors have floated a bond or other borrowing). A community open house was held April 2, 2026.)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. Dollar Creek is the County's only land contribution in the Tahoe Basin and shows the limit of its resources: the land is in hand but the County has no fund large enough for an $18.5 million gap, so the deal will depend on state money (MHP, IIG, AHSC, TRPA bonus units) plus TOT programs. A developer answering a future RFP should price the TRPA coverage and allocation process and the County's requirement for workforce preference. County land plus County funds will trigger prevailing wage.
- Source: https://sierrasun.com/news/tahoe-city-affordable-housing-development-meets-funding-challenges
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Housing Trust Placer (nonprofit housing trust seeded and funded by Placer County)**
- Agency: Housing Trust Placer, Inc. (Robyn van Ekelenburg, CEO; robyn@housingtrustplacer.org); County contributions through the Economic Development and Housing Division
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Nonprofit trust formed in 2020 that provides gap financing, acquisition and fundraising; Placer County pledged $575,000 with its 2021 Housing Element and selected the trust to administer its First-Time Homebuyer and Workforce Housing Preservation down payment programs (2025). Projects: Parkside at Sabre City (44 homes each with an ADU, manufactured housing, County and HCD financing, leasing 2025-2026) and the Hope Way site at 3130 Penryn Road (240 units with USA Properties Fund). No published NOFA, loan amounts or terms for developers; financing is arranged deal by deal from public and private sources.
- AMI requirement: Set per project; Sabre City serves low income renters and the trust's homebuyer programs serve first-time and workforce buyers.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No NOFA; approach the CEO directly. The County also approved $500,000 in 2025 for a new North Lake Tahoe housing trust (separate from Housing Trust Placer).)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. Housing Trust Placer is the County's chosen intermediary for homebuyer programs and a co-developer or site holder for County-backed projects rather than a lender with a fund balance, so do not underwrite a trust loan without a conversation. It is useful as a nonprofit partner for the welfare exemption and for assembling County, SMUD, hospital and foundation gap money on western Placer projects. It qualifies as a Local Housing Trust Fund for HCD matching grants only if it meets the LHTF program's dedicated-revenue test, which was not confirmed.
- Source: https://housingtrustplacer.org/
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Launchpad workforce housing incentive, eastern Placer County (Transient Occupancy Tax through the TOT-TBID Dollars at Work program)**
- Agency: Placer County Executive Office, Economic Development and Housing (Timothy Cussen, Housing Specialist); Tahoe City office 775 North Lake Boulevard; funding recommended by the North Tahoe Community Alliance (NTCA) TOT-TBID Dollars at Work program
- Type: grant
- Typical award: $200,000 per new multifamily or single-family unit created (category one), $125,000 per ADU and $50,000 per SRO unit, paid at completion once the workforce deed restriction is recorded. Funding: $1,000,000 inaugural NOFA (April 2025; $600,000 reserved for Steelhead Cottage Court in Kings Beach, 3 tiny-home units, and $125,000 for an accessible ADU) and $3,000,000 of new TOT approved February 3, 2026, for a 2026 NOFA pool of $3,275,000. The February 25 to March 18, 2026 round drew 12 applications (5 multifamily, 6 ADUs, 1 conversion; 55 units; $10,550,000 requested), with a lottery expected to reserve funds from mid-May 2026.
- AMI requirement: Units must be in unincorporated eastern Placer County within the Tahoe Truckee Unified School District boundary (outside avalanche hazard areas) and deed-restricted for occupancy by households with at least one full-time local worker (or equivalent) employed within the TTUSD area; no income limit, the restriction is workforce occupancy. The deed restriction follows the Workforce Housing Preservation Program form with an option to purchase.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: 2026 round closed March 18, 2026; awards by lottery from mid-May 2026. Staff will revise the guidelines with community input (survey August 2026) and return to the Board later in 2026; the next NOFA depends on NTCA recommending TOT for FY 2026-27 and later. Lease to Locals (homeowner incentives, $500,000 of TOT in FY 2025-26, administered by Placemate) continues alongside.)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. Launchpad is a per-unit grant rather than a loan and is sized for small infill projects in Kings Beach, Tahoe City and the North Shore; a 10-unit workforce project could draw $2 million, which is meaningful against TRPA coverage and construction costs in the Basin. It pays only at completion, so it is bridge-financed. The workforce (not income) restriction means it does not fit LIHTC deals directly but can fund unrestricted middle-income units in a mixed-income project. TOT funds are County funds, so prevailing wage status is unclear under Labor Code 1720; confirm with the County. North Lake Tahoe TOT also funds the Workforce Housing Preservation Program and the Dollar Creek Crossing effort.
- Source: https://content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER/2026/05/08/file_attachments/3645300/03A.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA), Placer County formula allocation**
- Agency: Placer County Executive Office, Economic Development and Housing Division
- Type: grant
- Typical award: 2019-2023 five-year formula estimate $2,730,542 (Board resolution with a 302(c)(4) plan; HCD awarded $455,090 for the 2019 allocation on October 21, 2020; the County ran a PLHA NOFA for developers in 2020). HCD's 2024-2028 formula NOFA lists Placer County at $489,950 (2024), $538,105 (2025) and an estimated $3,062,188 over five years. Cities receive their own allocations: Roseville about $1.37 million, Rocklin about $591,000, Lincoln about $585,000 and Auburn about $440,000 over five years.
- AMI requirement: PLHA guidelines: rental housing at or below 60% AMI (up to 80% AMI) with 55-year covenants, or other activities in the County's five-year plan. The 2024-2028 plan activities and any current County PLHA NOFA could not be opened (placer.ca.gov blocked).
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: The County must adopt a new five-year plan for the 2024-2028 allocations under HCD's amended formula NOFA; no 2026 Board action was found in the records reviewed. Ask the Economic Development and Housing Division whether PLHA will be offered through a NOFA or committed to specific projects.)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. At about $500,000 a year, County PLHA is a modest deal-specific source and the County has used a competitive NOFA for it before (2020), so a developer can ask for the next cycle to be run the same way. It can also be stacked under the County's in-lieu fee loan on a project like Hope Way. State funds trigger prevailing wage.

**Project-Based Vouchers and special-purpose vouchers, Placer County Housing Authority (PCHA)**
- Agency: Placer County Housing Authority, operated by Health and Human Services, Human Services Division (Robert Oldham, Director; Alexis Madera, Staff Services Manager); Board of Supervisors sits as the Board of Commissioners
- Type: other
- Typical award: PCHA assists 435 very low and low income households through Housing Choice, Mainstream, VASH, Family Unification and Foster Youth to Independence vouchers; it owns no public housing. PBVs are awarded from the existing inventory through periodic RFQs (RFQ 20387 closed May 30, 2023 for new construction, rehabilitated and existing units) and project agreements: 8 PBVs to The Frances (formerly University Avenue Apartments, 2160 University Avenue, Rocklin, USA Properties Fund, occupancy expected 2029; HAP agreement approved July 28, 2026) and 16 PBVs committed to two Pacific Street partnerships in Rocklin (November 2025). The HCV waiting list is closed.
- AMI requirement: Very low and low income households (at or below 50% AMI at admission); PBV HAP contracts up to 20 years with extensions. PCHA's jurisdiction is all of Placer County except the City of Roseville, which has its own housing authority.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No PBV RFQ open on November 6, 2026; the last competitive round was 2023 and recent awards have gone to projects already in the County's pipeline. Contact PCHA (Human Services Division, Auburn) to request vouchers for a new project; new VASH and Family Unification awards (20 vouchers in 2025) expand the pool slowly.)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. With only 435 vouchers, PCHA can project-base perhaps 8 to 16 units per project, which is the pattern in Rocklin. Treat PBVs as a small PSH set-aside subsidy rather than a project-wide operating source, and plan on a transition reserve. Nine or more PBV units trigger Davis-Bacon; PBVs alone do not trigger state prevailing wage. Roseville Housing Authority (City of Roseville) is covered in Pass 3. Coordinated Entry runs through the Homeless Resource Council of the Sierras (CoC CA-515).

**Roseville, Rocklin/Placer County Continuum of Care (CA-515, Homeless Resource Council of the Sierras) and HHAP, Placer County share**
- Agency: Homeless Resource Council of the Sierras (HRCS, CoC lead and HMIS lead for CA-515 and Nevada County CA-531); Placer County Health and Human Services (County HHAP administrative entity; homeless outreach with the City of Roseville)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HHAP Round 6 allocations (HCD, 2024 count): Placer County $801,045.28 and the Roseville, Rocklin/Placer County CoC $855,530.14. HHAP rounds 1 to 5 brought $7.8 million to the Placer area, $2.0 million of it to the County ($1.8 million obligated, $892,207 spent by mid-2025). The County and City of Roseville co-fund homeless outreach workers ($160,000 through June 2027).

Placer's HHAP is under $1 million a year each to the County and the CoC and is used for subsidies and services, not capital. For a PSH project the value is HRCS Coordinated Entry referrals and possible CoC or HHAP operating support for a few units; pair with PCHA PBVs and County in-lieu fee loans. Roseville, the largest city, has its own housing authority and homelessness programs (Pass 3).

**Tax-exempt multifamily bonds: County TEFRA approvals for CalPFA, CMFA and CSCDA issuances (County not an issuer)**
- Agency: Placer County Board of Supervisors (TEFRA host approval for unincorporated projects); issuers California Public Finance Authority (CalPFA), California Municipal Finance Authority and California Statewide Communities Development Authority
- Type: bond
- Typical award: Project-sized conduit issuances. Placer Creek Affordable Apartments (St. Anton Communities, 168 units at Baseline Road and Westbrook Boulevard, unincorporated Placer, 30% to 70% AMI): CDLAC allocation of $32,320,000 and $3,073,371 of annual federal credits approved April 8, 2025 with CalPFA as issuer; County TEFRA hearing August 19, 2025 for up to $37,000,000; construction August 2025 to September 2026. Earlier County TEFRA approvals covered CMFA and CSCDA issuances for senior and assisted living projects.
- AMI requirement: Federal bond and 4% LIHTC rules: at least 20% of units at 50% AMI or 40% at 60% AMI (or income averaging), 55-year CDLAC regulatory agreement.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Request the TEFRA hearing through the issuer six to eight weeks before the CDLAC application; the County places hearings on regular Board agendas. Hope Way Phase 1 and Destination at Placer One will need TEFRA approvals when they apply for bonds.)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. Neither the County nor the Placer County Housing Authority runs a bond program, so budget issuer fees for CalPFA, CMFA or CSCDA. Placer Creek is the current template for an unincorporated western Placer 4% deal: Lennar-provided site in a specific plan, CalPFA bonds, average income set-aside up to 70% AMI, County affordable housing agreement, no County cash. Bonds alone do not trigger state prevailing wage; County in-lieu fee loans and state funds in the same deal do.
- Source: https://www.treasurer.ca.gov/ctcac/meeting/2025/0408/staff/CA-25-481.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Welfare Exemption (property tax), Placer County Assessor**
- Agency: Placer County Assessor, Matt Maynard; (530) 889-4300; with the State Board of Equalization (Organizational Clearance Certificate)
- Type: tax abatement
- Typical award: Exemption of property taxes on units restricted to lower income households under Revenue and Taxation Code 214(g): full exemption for qualifying units in LIHTC or publicly financed projects and partial exemption for the qualifying share in mixed projects. In Placer County the 1% base rate plus voter-approved debt and direct assessments applies to the exempt value.
- AMI requirement: Nonprofit owner or limited partnership with a nonprofit managing general partner holding a BOE Organizational Clearance Certificate; recorded regulatory agreement (tax credits, bonds, County Use Deed Restriction with income limits, PLHA) restricting rents to lower income households.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: File BOE-267 (first year) or BOE-267-A (annual) with the Assessor by February 15 each year with BOE-267-L, L1, L2 and L-A supplements as applicable; late filings through December 31 receive a partial exemption. The Assessor's web pages were not reachable on November 6, 2026; call the office for filing instructions.)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. Standard underwriting item for Hope Way, Placer Creek and similar LIHTC deals. Note that the County's eastern Placer workforce deed restrictions (Launchpad, WHPP) restrict occupancy to local workers without income limits and do not by themselves qualify a property for the welfare exemption.
- Source: https://www.boe.ca.gov/proptaxes/countycontacts.htm
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Workforce Housing Preservation Program (WHPP), eastern Placer County deed-restriction purchases**
- Agency: Placer County Executive Office, Economic Development and Housing; down payment assistance component administered by Housing Trust Placer
- Type: grant
- Typical award: $4,100,000 allocated since 2021 ($500,000 General Fund and TOT in 2021, $600,000 TOT in 2023, $3,000,000 TOT in April 2024 through the NTCA TOT-TBID program); 17 deed restrictions purchased for $1,852,400 (average $108,965 per property; three in FY 2025-26 for $329,600). $2,247,500 remained through FY 2025-26, with staff seeking to extend it into FY 2026-27. May 12, 2026 amendments opened the program to multifamily properties of more than four units: maximum aggregate disbursement 16% of fair market value with a $75,000 per-unit cap (single-family cap $150,000), local businesses eligible as buyers, and a termination fee that declines over time.
- AMI requirement: Permanent deed restriction requiring occupancy by a household with at least one member of the local workforce (employed within the Tahoe Truckee Unified School District area); no income limit. Applies to existing homes, condos, duplexes and now multifamily rental properties in eastern Placer County.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Applications accepted while funds last; $2,247,500 available as of May 2026, subject to NTCA extension into FY 2026-27. Multifamily eligibility is new as of May 2026, so no multifamily transaction had closed when the guidelines were amended.)
- Layering notes: placer.ca.gov (including DocumentCenter PDFs) sits behind Cloudflare and refused automated access on 2026-11-06; Board items were read from the County's GovDelivery agenda attachments (content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER), the Legistar client 'placerca', state records and news coverage. The 2026 change makes WHPP a small acquisition subsidy for buying existing North Shore apartment buildings and restricting them to local workers: a four-unit building worth $2 million could draw $300,000. It fills the preservation gap the County identified as redevelopment pressure on long-term rentals. The restriction is workforce occupancy rather than income, so it does not combine with LIHTC or state programs that require income limits, but it can pair with Launchpad for added units and with the welfare exemption only if a nonprofit owner also records income restrictions. No construction, so no prevailing wage.
- Source: https://content.govdelivery.com/attachments/CAPLACER/2026/05/08/file_attachments/3645292/02A.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)


### Riverside County

**Behavioral Health Services Act (BHSA) Integrated Plan FY 2026-29, housing interventions**
- Agency: Riverside University Health System, Behavioral Health (RUHS-BH)
- Type: other
- Typical award: BHSA component allocations of $195,334,844 a year; housing interventions set at 23% in year one ($44,927,014) and 27% in years two and three ($52,740,408) after a transfer to other components, with estimated year one housing expenditures of $44,225,982. Plan adopted by the Board June 2, 2026. Uses are tenant-based rental subsidies for about 6,015 people a year (1,015 permanent, 5,000 interim) and operating subsidies for about 725 people in PSH, assisted living and interim settings; no BHSA capital development (the County reports housing funds fully subscribed and points to 399 new interim and recuperative care units at the Mead Valley Wellness Village opening 2027). RUHS-BH housing vendor purchase orders were raised to $15,000,000 a year through FY 2030-31.
- AMI requirement: BHSA-eligible adults with serious behavioral health conditions who are homeless or at risk, at least 50% chronically homeless, Housing First.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Three-year plan effective July 1, 2026 with annual updates each spring; RUHS-BH contracts with housing vendors through purchase orders rather than a developer NOFA.)
- Layering notes: Riverside County's Housing and Workforce Solutions (HWS) department runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA, HHAP, Homekey+ sponsorships and project loans, while the Housing Authority of the County of Riverside (HACR, same Board sitting as Commissioners) supplies project-based vouchers, housing successor funds and land; rivcohws.org and harivco.org block automated access, so records were built from Board of Supervisors files on riversidecountyca.iqm2.com and media.rivcocob.org, RUHS Behavioral Health, the Assessor and the County Housing Element. Unlike several Bay Area counties, Riverside chose tenant-based subsidies and operating support over project-based commitments and zero capital, so BHSA is not a construction source here; developers of PSH should seek RUHS-BH operating subsidy or master lease vendor agreements for behavioral health units and pair them with HACR PBVs. Source is the DHCS Integrated Plan submission (RUHS media 5601); executive brief at ruhealth.org/media/5971; Board adoption file Detail_LegiFile.aspx?ID=30512.
- Source: https://www.ruhealth.org/media/5601
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**CDBG, HOME and ESG, Riverside Urban County (annual NOFA and rolling HOME applications)**
- Agency: Riverside County Housing and Workforce Solutions (HWS), Community and Housing Development Division
- Type: grant
- Typical award: FY 2026-27 One Year Action Plan (adopted July 14, 2026): CDBG $7,764,567 (including $537,816 for Murrieta as a metro city), HOME $2,452,217.03 ($2,102,217.03 allocation plus $350,000 program income; $1,576,663.03 for new construction and substantial rehabilitation, $315,333 CHDO set-aside, $210,221 administration, remainder first-time homebuyer) and ESG $623,369. Recent HOME development loans: 6th Street Seniors Apartments, Coachella (Chelsea Investment) up to $1,575,000 (March 24, 2026); Placita Dolores Huerta, Coachella, increased $300,000 (December 2024).
- AMI requirement: HUD CDBG national objective and HOME rent and income limits with 55-year County covenants on rental projects; ESG for shelters and rapid rehousing.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: 2026-27 cycle: NOFA published September 2025, CDBG and ESG applications September 25 to December 18, 2025 (extended to January 9, 2026), Board hearing April 28, 2026, adoption July 14, 2026. HOME applications are accepted on a rolling basis until funds are committed (RivcoCDBG@rivco.org, (951) 955-5936). The 2027-28 NOFA is expected in late September 2026 with a mid-December deadline.)
- Layering notes: Riverside County's Housing and Workforce Solutions (HWS) department runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA, HHAP, Homekey+ sponsorships and project loans, while the Housing Authority of the County of Riverside (HACR, same Board sitting as Commissioners) supplies project-based vouchers, housing successor funds and land; rivcohws.org and harivco.org block automated access, so records were built from Board of Supervisors files on riversidecountyca.iqm2.com and media.rivcocob.org, RUHS Behavioral Health, the Assessor and the County Housing Element. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area and cooperating cities (cooperation agreements for FY 2027-29 approved July 28, 2026); Riverside, Moreno Valley, Corona, Temecula, Palm Springs, Indio and other large cities are separate entitlements (Pass 3). HOME money is small relative to the county's size (about $1.6 million a year for development) and is used as a $1 million to $1.6 million soft loan alongside HACR PBVs and tax credits; CDBG is district-allocated and rarely funds multifamily. Davis-Bacon applies to HOME projects of 12 or more units. Source is the Board file for the 2026-27 One Year Action Plan (attachment 2026-2027 One Year Action Plan, FileOpen ID 88444).

**Coachella Valley Regional Housing Catalyst Fund (Lift to Rise) with County capital**
- Agency: Lift to Rise (nonprofit fund manager) with County of Riverside contributions through the Board and Supervisorial District 4
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Revolving predevelopment loan fund for affordable housing in the Coachella Valley. The County contributed $2,000,000 of District 4 ARPA funds on May 24, 2022 and added $1,500,000 to Lift to Rise in the FY 2026-27 budget (approved June 9, 2026); Lift to Rise's action plan targets 10,000 affordable homes in ten years with more than 50 regional partners.
- AMI requirement: Affordable housing projects in the Coachella Valley; loan terms set by Lift to Rise.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Apply to Lift to Rise directly; County funds are capital contributions, not a County application process.)
- Layering notes: Riverside County's Housing and Workforce Solutions (HWS) department runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA, HHAP, Homekey+ sponsorships and project loans, while the Housing Authority of the County of Riverside (HACR, same Board sitting as Commissioners) supplies project-based vouchers, housing successor funds and land; rivcohws.org and harivco.org block automated access, so records were built from Board of Supervisors files on riversidecountyca.iqm2.com and media.rivcocob.org, RUHS Behavioral Health, the Assessor and the County Housing Element. The Catalyst Fund is the only revolving predevelopment source in the eastern county and is often the first money into Coachella and Indio tax credit deals before County PLHA, HOME and HACR PBVs; the fund itself and CVAG's housing programs belong in Pass 4 and Pass 3. The FY 2026-27 budget added $5,000,000 of General Fund to HWS for shovel-ready affordable housing and $1,500,000 to Lift to Rise; Supervisor district Unincorporated Communities Initiative funds have also been directed to HWS for housing ($692,239 from District 4, June 24, 2025).

**Countywide inclusionary housing ordinance and industrial linkage fee (Housing Element Program H-12, proposed)**
- Agency: Riverside County Transportation and Land Management Agency, Planning, with HWS
- Type: other
- Typical award: Not adopted. The Board initiated an inclusionary zoning study on May 21, 2024 (Program H-12); the Planning Commission held an Inclusionary Zoning and Warehouse Linkage Fee workshop April 23, 2025 proposing that part of industrial community benefit fees fund affordable housing; the 2025 Housing Element Annual Progress Report (April 14, 2026) lists the ordinance options as still pending, and the FY 2026-27 budget set aside $900,000 for ordinance updates including inclusionary housing.
- AMI requirement: Proposed options include on-site units, off-site units, land dedication or an in-lieu or linkage fee for residential projects over five units and warehouse projects.

- Layering notes: Riverside County's Housing and Workforce Solutions (HWS) department runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA, HHAP, Homekey+ sponsorships and project loans, while the Housing Authority of the County of Riverside (HACR, same Board sitting as Commissioners) supplies project-based vouchers, housing successor funds and land; rivcohws.org and harivco.org block automated access, so records were built from Board of Supervisors files on riversidecountyca.iqm2.com and media.rivcocob.org, RUHS Behavioral Health, the Assessor and the County Housing Element. Recorded so the gap is explicit: the unincorporated county has no inclusionary requirement, no in-lieu fee and no housing trust fund, so County capital depends on federal and state pass-through and General Fund one-offs. An adopted warehouse linkage fee would be the first dedicated local housing revenue. Source is the Board file for the 2025 Housing Element Annual Progress Report.

**Development impact fee waiver for affordable housing (Ordinance 659) and density bonus (Ordinance 348)**
- Agency: Riverside County Transportation and Land Management Agency, Planning and Building and Safety
- Type: fee waiver
- Typical award: The County Housing Element states that all affordable housing projects receive a waiver of all County development impact fees under Ordinance 659 (fees unchanged since 2015 and higher in the eastern Coachella Valley); the County also commits to fee deferrals and fast-track processing for County-assisted affordable projects (Program 1.1d). Density bonus: Ordinance 348 was amended in March 2021 to adopt state density bonus law (Government Code 65915 and 65917.2) with incentives, concessions and waivers; the R-6 Residential Incentive Zone offers case-by-case density above the General Plan for projects with 15% of units at 80% AMI or 25% at 120% AMI. Projects in the unincorporated area are also exempt from most city-level fees.
- AMI requirement: Affordable housing as defined by the DIF ordinance and the Housing Element; density bonus tiers per state law.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Claimed at building permit (DIF waiver) and at entitlement (density bonus). School, regional transportation (TUMF and WRCOG) and special district fees are not County DIFs and are not waived.)
- Layering notes: Riverside County's Housing and Workforce Solutions (HWS) department runs the Urban County HUD programs, PLHA, HHAP, Homekey+ sponsorships and project loans, while the Housing Authority of the County of Riverside (HACR, same Board sitting as Commissioners) supplies project-based vouchers, housing successor funds and land; rivcohws.org and harivco.org block automated access, so records were built from Board of Supervisors files on riversidecountyca.iqm2.com and media.rivcocob.org, RUHS Behavioral Health, the Assessor and the County Housing Element. The blanket DIF waiver is the County's most valuable standing incentive for unincorporated sites (DIFs run several thousand to over ten thousand dollars per unit by area plan); confirm the current Ordinance 659 text and the waiver procedure with Building and Safety because the Housing Element statement is the only source opened this pass and planning.rctlma.org blocks automated access. Prevailing wage is marked unclear because waived fees can be read as public funds under Labor Code 1720(b)(4). Source is the County 2021-2029 Housing Element HCD submittal (Programs 1.1d and 1.2b and the constraints chapter).
- Source: https://planning.rctlma.org/sites/g/files/aldnop416/files/2024-01/Riverside%20County%20-%20HCD%20Submittal.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

- Agency: Housing Authority of the County of Riverside as housing successor to the former County and Coachella redevelopment agencies
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Villa Verde Apartments, Coachella (152 units in two phases, Abode Communities): amended and restated Disposition, Development and Loan Agreement (February 3, 2026) conveys HACR land with a Low and Moderate Income Housing Asset Fund loan of $4,404,640. Rubidoux Gateway Villas, Jurupa Valley (57 family units, National Housing Development Corporation): DDLA with a seller carryback loan equal to the purchase price (September 1, 2026). Camino Terrace, Jurupa Valley (80 units, Wakeland): DDLA and land sale (September 1, 2026). Tres Lagos Apartments Phase III, Wildomar: up to $1,200,000 and tax credit support (July 14, 2026). Housing successor balances at June 30, 2025: County successor (HASA) $460,089 and Coachella successor (CSA) $1,894,430, the latter pending release to Villa Verde in 2026.
- AMI requirement: Housing successor rules (80% AMI and below, 30% extremely low income minimum over five years); DDLAs restrict 49% of non-manager units with HACR covenants, with PBVs on the balance.

HACR behaves as a land bank: it holds former redevelopment parcels in Coachella, Jurupa Valley, Rubidoux and elsewhere and conveys them with a seller loan, PBVs and sometimes a Homekey+ sponsorship, which is how Villa Verde and Camino Terrace were assembled. Successor funds are small and mostly committed.
