### San Diego County

**Affordable housing on County-owned excess land (ground leases through Disposition and Development Agreements)**
- Agency: County of San Diego Department of General Services, Real Estate Services (RFP issuance and DDA), with HCDS (regulatory agreement and IHTF capital); Board of Supervisors approval
- Type: other
- Typical award: 12 County-owned surplus sites are in the pipeline for 100% affordable housing totaling about 1,762 homes (595 completed, 126 under construction, 1,043 upcoming), with $51.8 million of County capital committed to the sites. Nine sites are in the City of San Diego, plus Fallbrook, Ramona and Escondido. Terms are long-term ground leases (99 years at 620 E. Valley Parkway, Escondido, 134 senior units; 3177 Ocean View Boulevard, San Diego, 122 units with BRIDGE Housing and a San Ysidro Health clinic, about $500,000 initial rent then $300 a year escalating 3.5%). Next starts: 6255 Mission Gorge Road (337 units) and 5001 73rd Street (120 units) in early 2027, 4588 Market Street (138) in 2027, Ramona 12th and Main (100) and 5202 University Avenue (90, via SDHC) in 2028.
- AMI requirement: 100% affordable at or below 80% AMI under a County regulatory agreement (55 years or longer); specific populations (seniors, families, PSH) set in each RFP. Developer must comply with the County Working Families Ordinance (prevailing wage, skilled and trained workforce, paid sick leave) on County property.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Site-by-site RFPs posted on the General Services Development Opportunities page when the Board declares a property surplus; no housing RFP was open on November 6, 2026 (the open RFP is a Behavioral Health Wellness Campus). Upcoming: RFP for ground lease and renovation of the four Chula Vista public housing sites after HUD RAD/Section 18 approval, and the Fallbrook North Orange and East Ivy site (acquired June 2025, program TBD). The County and City of San Diego share a goal of 10,000 affordable units on public land by 2030 (6,917 opened, under construction or planned as of March 2026).)
- Layering notes: County land deals come packaged: nominal ground lease, IHTF or other County capital (County sites had their own $12 million IHTF NOFA in July 2026 and get a location preference in every HCDS NOFA), PBVs where HACSD has jurisdiction, and Board support for state applications. In exchange the County requires 100% affordability, Working Families Ordinance labor terms (prevailing wage plus skilled and trained workforce on work over $1 million), ground lease payments to the IHTF, and a County regulatory agreement senior to other restrictions. Mission Gorge (337 units) and Ramona are the largest unincorporated or near-term opportunities; Ramona and Fallbrook sites test rural feasibility. Faith-based land partnerships (three developments, 220 units) are a parallel track.
- Source: https://sdcounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=8704&GUID=2E56B0B2-5171-4711-9A3B-0DAF1260AC97&Options=&Search=&FullText=1
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Behavioral Health Services Act (BHSA) housing interventions and Flexible Housing Pool, County of San Diego Behavioral Health Services**
- Agency: County of San Diego HHSA, Behavioral Health Services (Integrated Plan FY 2026-2029 approved June 9, 2026); Flexible Housing Pool Pilot administered by Brilliant Corners under a single-source contract approved November 18, 2025
- Type: grant
- Typical award: BHSA budget about $329.5 million in FY 2026-27, with the housing component at 27% of the local allocation (roughly $89 million a year by that percentage; the plan does not publish a single housing dollar total). Rental subsidies for about 2,672 people a year: 290 permanent slots (250 Flexible Housing Pool subsidies for ACT clients plus 40 operating subsidies at Starling Place, a Homekey+ site in the City of San Diego) and about 2,382 interim placements from a $23.6 million interim housing budget; 4,305 units a year funded in total. Flex Pool Pilot costs $3.45 to $5.35 million in FY 2026-27 (Behavioral Health Bridge Housing and BHSA). Capital development: none in FY 2026-27.
- AMI requirement: Medi-Cal eligible adults with serious mental illness or substance use disorder who are chronically homeless, homeless or at risk; at least half of housing funds for chronically homeless people; permanent settings include supportive housing, master-leased apartments, shared housing, board and care and recovery residences; Transitional Rent (six months, Medi-Cal managed care) feeds into BHSA subsidies.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No developer NOFA. The plan states the County will not fund capital development with BHSA in year one (to sustain treatment services) and will reassess in future years; operating subsidies are limited to the 40 Homekey+ units. Annual plan updates start FY 2027-28 (draft due to DHCS each March 31), and BHSA will absorb Behavioral Health Bridge Housing programs when that grant ends in FY 2028-29.)
- Layering notes: Unlike Orange or Contra Costa, San Diego County is spending its BHSA housing money on subsidies and a Flex Pool rather than capital, so do not underwrite County BHSA capital for PSH in 2026-27. The realistic asks are: Flex Pool tenant-based subsidies for units set aside for ACT clients (Brilliant Corners master-leasing or direct placements), a Homekey+ style operating subsidy agreement if the County co-applies (it has co-applied with Wakeland in Chula Vista and Casa de Amparo in Vista), and Medi-Cal Transitional Rent through the managed care plans for move-in. Community input asked the County to dedicate the full 25% to capital, so the FY 2027-28 update is the moment to request a capital line. BHSA funds in a project are state funds and trigger prevailing wage.
- Source: https://sdcounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=8563&GUID=EC9C8789-7E53-4306-94B1-1A8D3B0F1733&Options=&Search=&FullText=1
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**CDBG acquisition and rehabilitation NOFA (NOFA 2026-01) and HOME Consortium entitlement, San Diego Urban County**
- Agency: County of San Diego HHSA, Housing and Community Development Services (HCDS), Community Development; community.development@sdcounty.ca.gov
- Type: loan
- Typical award: NOFA 2026-01 (released June 1, 2026, due July 10, 2026, awards November 2026) offered $1.7 million of CDBG for acquisition and/or rehabilitation of existing affordable multifamily units or land acquisition for affordable housing; new construction is not eligible. FY 2027-28 CDBG applications for public facilities, infrastructure and services ran September 21 to October 30, 2026. HOME and HOME-ARP ($11.9 million HOME-ARP allocation) flow through the capital NOFA; the County's loan portfolio holds about $82 million of federal funds across 123 developments.
- AMI requirement: CDBG: households at or below 80% AMI, HUD minimum 5 years but HCDS imposes 55 years. HOME: at or below 60% AMI with 20% Low HOME units, 20-year HUD minimum, 55 years enforced by HCDS. Eligible areas: Urban County (unincorporated plus Coronado, Del Mar, Imperial Beach, Lemon Grove, Poway, Solana Beach) for CDBG and PLHA; HOME Consortium adds Carlsbad, Encinitas, La Mesa, San Marcos, Santee and Vista.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Next acquisition-rehab CDBG NOFA expected around June 2027 if HCDS repeats the 2026 schedule; HOME and HOME-ARP are in NOFA 2026-03 (due November 19, 2026). CDBG public facilities applications open each September for the following fiscal year.)
- Layering notes: Terms match the capital NOFA: 3% simple, 55 years, 50% residual receipts, $720 per unit replacement reserve for rehab, 12-month reservation with no extensions, $4,000 compliance fee. Federal money brings Davis-Bacon, Uniform Relocation Act, NEPA release of funds before any choice-limiting action, lead-based paint and Section 3. Useful for preservation buys of small existing properties in the Urban County cities where IHTF is the only other County option. The larger cities (San Diego, Chula Vista, Oceanside, Escondido, El Cajon, Carlsbad for CDBG) are their own entitlements for Pass 3.
- Source: https://www.sandiegocounty.gov/content/dam/sdc/sdhcd/new-docs/CD/01.NOFA_2026-01_CDBG-FINAL.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**HCDS Affordable Housing NOFA (NOFA 2026-03): IHTF, HOME, HOME-ARP, CDBG and PLHA capital loans**
- Agency: County of San Diego Health and Human Services Agency, Housing and Community Development Services (HCDS), Community Development; 3989 Ruffin Road, San Diego 92123; community.development@sdcounty.ca.gov; (858) 694-4885; NOFA contact Felipe Murillo (858) 694-4807
- Type: loan
- Typical award: NOFA 2026-03 (released October 1, 2026, due November 19, 2026 at 2:00 pm, awards March 2027) offers about $16.0 million: $9.0M Innovative Housing Trust Fund, $2.5M HOME, $1.5M HOME-ARP, $1.8M CDBG, $1.2M PLHA. HCDS allocates sources at its discretion across awarded projects. Recent IHTF loans have ranged from $400,000 to $7.25 million per project (median about $3.5 million); HCDS's portfolio averages about $1.4 million of County-administered money per development. The prior round (NOFA 2024-01, due December 20, 2024) offered $6.5M HOME/HOME-ARP/CHDO, $3.5M IHTF, $1.5M PLHA plus 100 PBVs and 25 VASH PBVs.
- AMI requirement: 55-year minimum affordability for every award regardless of source. IHTF at or below 80% AMI regionwide; HOME and PLHA at or below 60% AMI (20% of HOME units at Low HOME rents); HOME-ARP at or below 30% AMI for people experiencing or at risk of homelessness; CDBG at or below 80% AMI for acquisition or rehabilitation only. Preferences: unincorporated area or County-owned land, projects applying to the 2027 second CDLAC/TCAC round, green building (LEED Silver, 50% onsite renewable), homeless units with secured services funding (HOME-ARP).
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: NOFA 2026-03 applications due November 19, 2026 at 2:00 pm (USB plus electronic copy); virtual industry day was October 15, 2026; awards March 2027. HCDS has run at least one capital NOFA each year since 2018 and plans to award its newest $22 million IHTF allocation through NOFAs in FY 2026-27, so expect another round in 2027.)
- Layering notes: This is the main county gap source for the whole San Diego region, including the City of San Diego for IHTF money (HOME, HOME-ARP, CDBG and PLHA are limited to the Urban County and HOME Consortium cities listed in the NOFA). Loan terms: 3% simple interest, 55-year term, 50% residual receipts shared proportionally with other public lenders, GP loans may not be repaid ahead of the County, TCAC developer fee limits, replacement reserves $375 per unit (new construction), $500 per PSH unit, $720 per unit (rehab), $4,000 initial compliance monitoring fee. All other local funds must be committed before applying and senior lenders must subordinate to the regulatory agreement. Funds are reserved for 24 months and the County will not split or combine awards if a deal converts between 9% and 4%. Scoring (165 points) weights geographic location 30, financing gap 20, experience 20, target population 15, services 15, overall 15. Federal sources carry Davis-Bacon, NEPA, Section 3, BABA (HOME) and relocation rules; state prevailing wage applies to County loans. PSH or PBV projects must fund a one-year transition reserve. No Place Like Home was offered through earlier County NOFAs and is exhausted.
- Source: https://www.sandiegocounty.gov/content/dam/sdc/sdhcd/new-docs/CD/01%20NOFA_2026-03_Capital_IHTF_HOME_FINAL.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Inclusionary Housing Ordinance, In-Lieu Fee and expanded density bonus, unincorporated San Diego County (Zoning Ordinance Sections 6341 and 7430)**
- Agency: County of San Diego Planning and Development Services (ordinance and affordable housing plans) with HCDS (in-lieu fee trust fund and affordable housing agreements)
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Adopted June 24, 2026 (Ordinance 11009), effective October 22, 2026; projects with a complete or SB 330 preliminary application submitted by October 21, 2026 are exempt. Applies to residential and mixed-use projects of 10 or more primary units in the unincorporated area. Incentives: density bonus doubled relative to state law up to a 50% maximum (10% low income units earns 40% instead of 20%) plus two extra concessions, and 30-day priority discretionary review for projects providing 50% more low income units than required. In-lieu fees: $18.69 per interior square foot (rental, 95% market share assumed, effective $17.76), $21.37 (for-sale, effective $19.23), $41.82 (General Plan Amendment projects, effective $33.46), paid at building permit and deposited to an HCDS trust fund for affordable housing anywhere in the county including cities. Staff estimate 15 to 60 deed-restricted units a year.
- AMI requirement: For-rent: 5% extremely low (30% AMI) or 5% very low (50% AMI) or 10% low (80% AMI). For-sale: 5% very low or 10% low or 15% moderate (120% AMI). General Plan Amendment projects: 20% low. Units calculated on base density before bonus, rounded up at 0.5. Alternative compliance in Board priority order: land donation, affordable for-sale ADUs (up to 50% of requirement or 5 units), in-lieu fee for 50%, in-lieu fee for 100%, off-site units in the same community plan area, a village, a TCAC High or Highest Resource area or a VMT-efficient area (cities allowed). 100% affordable projects are exempt but record an affordable housing agreement. Market-rate permits are held until affordable units are permitted (50% rule) and finaled (75% rule).
- Cycle: rolling (next: In effect for applications submitted on or after October 22, 2026. Senior Housing and Small Lot zoning updates and Housing Unlocked zoning amendments are scheduled for the Board in fall 2026 and 2027; a programmatic EIR for low-VMT housing streamlining comes in 2027.)
- Layering notes: For a 100% affordable deal the ordinance is a zero-cost exemption plus access to the doubled density bonus if the project also meets a set-aside; for mixed-income workforce deals in the unincorporated area it adds a 5% to 10% affordable obligation or roughly $18 to $19 per square foot in fees. The in-lieu fee revenue becomes new County gap money administered by HCDS (the Board asked staff for a dedicated IHTF revenue source and this is the first one). Land donation sites must be suitable for multifamily at the displaced density with utilities, outside slopes, floodplains, farmland and habitat; donations over $1 million go to the Board. Density bonus and inclusionary agreements do not trigger prevailing wage; the Working Families Ordinance applies only on County-owned land. 2026 San Diego County AMI is $130,900.
- Source: https://www.sandiegocounty.gov/content/dam/sdc/pds/advance/HousingStudy/San%20Diego%20County%20Inclusionary%20Housing%20Ordinance%20Applicant%20Guide_10_22_26.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Innovative Housing Trust Fund (IHTF), County of San Diego**
- Agency: County of San Diego HHSA, Housing and Community Development Services (HCDS); established by the Board of Supervisors October 10, 2017 (Administrative Code Article XV, Sections 230.8 and 232.5)
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Board allocations total over $129 million through seven allocations since 2017 (General Fund one-time money, plus redirected grant balances such as $2 million of Capital ESG in June 2025). $112 million has been awarded to 36 developments with 3,054 units in 22 communities, leveraging $1.6 billion. The newest $22 million allocation is being awarded through NOFAs in FY 2026-27: a $12 million IHTF NOFA for County-owned sites ran July 6 to August 10, 2026, and $9 million is in NOFA 2026-03 (due November 19, 2026). Loans to date range from $200,000 (Vista Lane) to $7.25 million (Terrasini, 95 units); most are $3 million to $7 million.
- AMI requirement: Households at or below 80% AMI (extremely low, very low and low income). NOFA 2024-01 required 99-year affordability for IHTF-funded units; NOFA 2026-03 states a 55-year minimum for all sources. Eligible anywhere in San Diego County, including all 18 cities.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Through NOFA 2026-03, due November 19, 2026. The Board's October 7, 2026 five-year report directs staff to identify a dedicated ongoing revenue source for the IHTF; no ballot measure or fee is adopted yet, so future rounds depend on one-time Board allocations.)
- Layering notes: IHTF is the only County money usable inside the City of San Diego and the larger cities, which makes it the typical County piece of a City of San Diego deal alongside SDHC funds and Housing Authority of the City of San Diego bonds. It is a 3% simple, 55-year residual receipts loan under HCDS standard terms. HCDS also deposits ground lease revenue from County land deals (for example about $500,000 of initial rent from 3177 Ocean View Boulevard) and will deposit inclusionary in-lieu fees from the unincorporated ordinance into HCDS trust funds, so the fund will grow modestly from those streams. County-owned sites get a scoring preference and had their own $12 million round in summer 2026. Expect state prevailing wage on any IHTF-funded construction.
- Source: https://sdcounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=8806&GUID=5FC31D6F-DC1F-407F-A2D9-2AA8C5F0F812&Options=&Search=&FullText=1
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA), County of San Diego formula allocation**
- Agency: County of San Diego HHSA, Housing and Community Development Services (HCDS)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HCD's 2024-2028 formula NOFA lists San Diego County at $1,389,802 (2024), $1,488,682 (2025) and an estimated $8,686,263 over five years (the City of San Diego is separate at about $4.1 to $4.4 million a year). HCDS puts PLHA into its capital NOFAs ($1.2 million in NOFA 2026-03, $1.5 million in NOFA 2024-01) and into a moderate-income down payment assistance program.
- AMI requirement: Rental housing at or below 60% AMI with 55-year affordability under HCDS's NOFA; PLHA guidelines allow up to 80% AMI rental and 120% to 150% AMI ownership. Eligible only in the Urban County (unincorporated plus Coronado, Del Mar, Imperial Beach, Lemon Grove, Poway, Solana Beach).
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Through NOFA 2026-03, due November 19, 2026. HCDS is preparing the County's new five-year PLHA plan and application for the 2024-2028 allocations (Board action expected in the coming months), which sets how much goes to development versus down payment assistance.)
- Layering notes: Small relative to the IHTF but it is the one County source that can be used for moderate-income ownership. For rental projects it rides along with IHTF and HOME in the same application, same 3% 55-year loan terms. State funds trigger prevailing wage. Cities with their own PLHA (San Diego, Chula Vista, Oceanside, Escondido and others) are Pass 3.
- Source: https://www.hcd.ca.gov/sites/default/files/docs/grants-and-funding/plha/2024-2028-plha-formula-allocation-nofa.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Project-Based Vouchers and HUD-VASH PBVs, Housing Authority of the County of San Diego (HACSD)**
- Agency: Housing Authority of the County of San Diego, administered by HCDS Rental Assistance Division (Board of Supervisors sits as the Board of Commissioners); 3989 Ruffin Road, San Diego
- Type: other
- Typical award: Since the Board's September 1, 2021 direction to expand PBVs, HACSD has awarded 768 PBVs (HCV PBVs plus VASH PBVs); 479 PBV units are operating and 327 more open within one to four years. The FY 2026 Agency Plan lists 387 PBV units in 13 developments operating, 119 units in four developments opening in 2026 (Union Tower 24 VASH, Villa Serena II 15, Alvarado Senior Village 53, Palomar Point 27) and 333 units in eight developments opening 2027 or later. PBVs are awarded through HCDS NOFAs (NOFA 2024-01 offered 100 PBVs and 25 VASH PBVs; 65 additional VASH vouchers were secured in 2025). HACSD assists over 11,000 households and pays about $21 million a month to about 4,000 landlords.
- AMI requirement: PBV units at or below 50% AMI (30% to 50% AMI homeless or special needs populations in recent awards); VASH PBVs for veterans up to 80% AMI. Initial HAP contract of 20 years renewable for up to 20 more at HACSD's discretion. HACSD jurisdiction: unincorporated area plus Chula Vista, Coronado, Del Mar, El Cajon, Escondido, Imperial Beach, La Mesa, Lemon Grove, Poway, San Marcos, Santee, Solana Beach and Vista (not the cities of San Diego, Carlsbad, Encinitas, National City or Oceanside).
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: No PBVs are offered in NOFA 2026-03 (due November 19, 2026). The HCV program closed to new applicants February 20, 2026 because of federal funding levels, so a PBV round depends on HUD appropriations; HACSD's 121 public housing units in Chula Vista are converting to PBVs through RAD/Section 18 with a developer RFP for ground lease and renovation expected after HUD approval (18-month process started November 2025).)
- Layering notes: PBVs are the County's operating subsidy for PSH and are usually requested in the same HCDS NOFA as the capital loan, with Coordinated Entry referrals through the Regional Task Force on Homelessness. HCDS requires a transition reserve sufficient to hold rents for one year if PBV assistance is not renewed. Nine or more PBVs in a project trigger Davis-Bacon under HCDS rules; PBVs alone do not trigger state prevailing wage. Projects inside the City of San Diego use San Diego Housing Commission vouchers instead (Pass 3). Watch the Chula Vista public housing repositioning: four sites (Dorothy Street, L Street, Melrose, Town Center Manor, 121 units) will be offered to an affordable developer under a ground lease with PBVs attached.
- Source: https://www.sandiegocounty.gov/content/dam/sdc/sdhcd/new-docs/2026_Annual_Agency_Plan-Final.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**San Diego City and County Continuum of Care (CA-601, Regional Task Force on Homelessness) and HHAP, County of San Diego share**
- Agency: Regional Task Force on Homelessness (RTFH, CoC lead and HMIS lead); County of San Diego HHSA, HCDS Office of Homeless Solutions (County HHAP administrative entity); City of San Diego (large city allocation)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HHAP Round 6 (FY 2026-27 through 2028-29): County $12.1 million (accepted December 9, 2025), RTFH CoC $12.9 million, City of San Diego $25.8 million, from a joint regional application filed August 29, 2025. County HHAP rounds 1 to 6 total about $64 million ($9.99M, $4.57M, $12.79M, $10.5M, $14.1M, $12.1M); the region received $220.6 million in rounds 1 to 5 plus $46.2 million of Encampment Resolution Funds. County HHAP pays for Housing Our Youth, the Local Rental Subsidy Program ($6.6 million in Round 5), shallow rent subsidies, housing stability case management and harm reduction contracts, not capital. 2025 point-in-time count: 9,905 people, 5,714 unsheltered.
- AMI requirement: People experiencing or at risk of homelessness; HHAP-6 requires a regionally coordinated action plan, 10% youth set-aside, and allows Category 1 permanent housing uses including housing development and capital funding, rental assistance and operating support.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: RTFH's 2026 HHAP application period is open (see rtfhsd.org Grants and Contracts) alongside the FY 2026 HUD CoC NOFO; the County's HHAP-6 is already programmed to existing contracts. HHAP Round 7 depends on the state budget.)
- Layering notes: County HHAP is operating money, not a capital source, and the County's BHSA plan says it will spend all of it on existing youth, regional housing assistance and local rental subsidy programs as ARPA expires. A PSH developer should look to RTFH for CoC PSH or Rapid Rehousing operating funds and to the City of San Diego's much larger HHAP allocation for capital or operating subsidies inside the city (Pass 3). Coordinated Entry through RTFH is the referral source required by HCDS for homeless units in County-funded projects, and RTFH's CES is the official front door for BHSA housing interventions tied to homelessness.
- Source: https://sdcounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=8186&GUID=BA2487AB-80B3-4A8B-88EB-EF4A4D6D05FC&Options=&Search=&FullText=1
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Tax-exempt multifamily bonds: County TEFRA approvals for CMFA, CSCDA and other JPA issuances (County not an issuer)**
- Agency: County of San Diego Board of Supervisors (TEFRA host approval through the Finance and General Government Group, Debt Advisory Committee concurrence); issuers California Municipal Finance Authority, California Statewide Communities Development Authority, California Public Finance Authority, California Enterprise Development Authority
- Type: bond
- Typical award: Project-sized conduit issuances approved as consent items roughly monthly. 2026 approvals include CMFA bonds for 2581 Commercial Street Apartments (70 units, up to $13 million, March 24 and August 18, 2026), four CMFA issuances on March 24, 2026 (up to $20M, $13M, $40M and $72M), Island Family Housing (CSCDA, August 18, 2026), Fallbrook Trails, Promenade and Seaward Affordable Apartments (CMFA, September 2026), Vista Lane Family Apartments reissuance (165 units, up to $55 million, December 9, 2025) and Fallbrook Senior Apartments (up to $26 million, November 4, 2025). The County collects about $991 per approval to recover staff costs and takes on no debt.
- AMI requirement: Federal bond and 4% LIHTC rules: at least 20% of units at 50% AMI or 40% at 60% AMI (or income averaging), 55-year CDLAC regulatory agreement.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Request the County TEFRA approval through the issuer (CMFA or CSCDA handle the notice and hearing); allow six to eight weeks before the CDLAC application deadline. Approvals appear on regular Board agendas; the Board also reapproves when terms change (reissuance on conversion to permanent, address corrections).)
- Layering notes: Neither the County nor the Housing Authority of the County of San Diego issues multifamily bonds; CMFA is the usual issuer, with County Board approval for projects in the unincorporated area and, in practice, for several City of San Diego addresses as well. Inside the City of San Diego the Housing Authority of the City of San Diego (through SDHC) is an active issuer and will be covered in Pass 3. Bonds alone do not trigger state prevailing wage, but County IHTF, HOME or PLHA in the same deal does. Pair the TEFRA timeline with the HCDS NOFA award (March 2027) and the 2027 second CDLAC/TCAC round that the NOFA rewards.
- Source: https://sdcounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=8683&GUID=197FFBBE-5A88-4EB6-A06C-A6255DD07117&Options=&Search=&FullText=1
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Welfare Exemption (property tax), San Diego County Assessor**
- Agency: San Diego County Assessor/Recorder/County Clerk, Jordan Z. Marks; Institutional Exemptions unit, 1600 Pacific Highway, Suite 103, San Diego, CA 92101; (619) 531-5772; with the State Board of Equalization (Organizational Clearance Certificate)
- Type: tax abatement
- Typical award: Exemption of property taxes on units restricted to lower income households under Revenue and Taxation Code 214(g): full exemption for qualifying units in LIHTC or publicly financed projects and partial exemption for the qualifying share in mixed projects. In San Diego County the 1% base rate plus voter-approved debt applies to the exempt value, typically worth 1.1% to 1.25% of assessed value a year.
- AMI requirement: Nonprofit owner or limited partnership with a nonprofit managing general partner holding a BOE Organizational Clearance Certificate; recorded regulatory agreement (tax credits, bonds, HOME, PLHA, IHTF or County regulatory agreement) restricting rents to lower income households; BOE-267-L2 tenant data for projects without government financing or tax credits.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Annual filing due February 15 each year (the Assessor's current instruction sheet, revised December 2025, states February 15, 2026); first-year claims on BOE-267 with BOE-267-L, L1, L2 and L-A supplements as applicable; late filings through December 31 receive a partial exemption.)
- Layering notes: Standard underwriting item for every LIHTC or County-financed deal in the region. The County's 55-year regulatory agreement and the inclusionary affordable housing agreements qualify as recorded restrictions. File the Organizational Clearance Certificate copy with the first claim and use the San Diego forms posted at sdarcc.gov. Mixed-income inclusionary projects owned by for-profits do not qualify and should instead seek a restricted assessed value under Revenue and Taxation Code 402.1.
- Source: https://www.sdarcc.gov/content/dam/arcc/assessor/forms/property-tax-exemption-and-exclusion-forms/267WELFIRST.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)


### San Joaquin County

**Affordable Housing Density Bonus, Development Title Chapter 9-401 (unincorporated county)**
- Agency: San Joaquin County Community Development Department
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: State-law density bonus codified in Chapter 9-401 (2026-10 code edition): 20% base bonus for 10% low-income, 5% very low-income or senior projects; 5% for 10% moderate-income for-sale units; 20% for 10% very low-income units for veterans, transition-age youth or homeless households; 35% for qualifying student housing; sliding scale up to 50% (2.5% per 1% very low-income above 5%, 1.5% per 1% low-income above 10%); land donation bonus 15% to 35%; floor area ratio bonus in the R-H zone for infill near transit with 20% of units at or below 50% AMI; 100% lower-income projects per Government Code 65915. One to four incentives or concessions by affordability tier plus parking reductions; the County may exceed these amounts (9-401.060).
- AMI requirement: 55-year rental affordability (longer if required by financing); for-sale units sold to qualifying buyers with equity sharing; affordable units integrated with and indistinguishable from market-rate units; replacement of existing affordable units required.
- Cycle: rolling (next: By application; pre-application response within 90 days; streamlined ministerial approval under 9-401.120 for eligible infill projects with at least 10% of units at or below 80% AMI (60 or 90 day review, deemed approved if missed).)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). The unincorporated county has no inclusionary ordinance and no in-lieu fee, so the density bonus and concessions are the only land-use subsidy; the 2023-2031 Housing Element (HCD submittal revised January 2025, adopted and revised June 30, 2026 to transfer RHNA to the new City of Mountain House, 8,808 to 5,113 units) sets a quantified objective of 100 lower-income units through the bonus and commits to fee deferrals for affordable subdivisions. The ministerial pathway in 9-401.120 carries state prevailing wage and skilled workforce requirements for larger projects under Government Code 65913.4, so model labor costs before electing it. Most multifamily sites in the unincorporated county (French Camp, Garden Acres, Lodi and Stockton fringe) are constrained by water and sewer service, which the Housing Element names as the largest constraint. Source is the published Development Title chapter on gocodebook.com (County code host); confirm current text with Community Development at (209) 468-3144.
- Source: https://gocodebook.com/library/us/ca/san-joaquin-county/development-title/series-400-additional-use-and-development-regulations/chapter-9-401-affordable-housing-density-bonus
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Behavioral Health Services Act (BHSA) Integrated Plan FY 2026-29, housing interventions**
- Agency: San Joaquin County Behavioral Health Services (Health Care Services Agency)
- Type: other
- Typical award: Three-year plan totaling $295,750,706 approved June 2, 2026 (5-0). The County requested a transfer of 7% from the housing component to Behavioral Health Services and Supports, leaving housing interventions at 23% of BHSA funds. Estimated housing intervention expenditures: $18,790,266 in year one and $42,013,536 in year two; the continuum table shows housing services of $37.0 million, $41.6 million and $37.8 million across the three years. Planned uses: project-based rental subsidies for about 150 people a year (64 permanent, 86 interim; 350 units), operating subsidies for about 116 people (228 units, property management staffing for SRO and transitional-to-permanent apartments), landlord outreach and mitigation for about 100, and two capital projects (a 50-unit tiny home project and the 33-unit Edison House, both braided with other funds, unit availability June 30, 2027, BHSA share capped at $450,000 per unit).
- AMI requirement: Individuals with serious behavioral health conditions who are homeless or at risk; at least 50% of housing intervention funds for chronically homeless households; PSH setting types and Housing First.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Plan effective July 1, 2026 through June 30, 2029 with annual updates each June. Behavioral Health Services completed a request for qualifications for housing developers in 2025-26 (RFQ 25107) and will work with the pre-qualified pool: Central Valley Low Income Housing Corporation, Delta Community Developers Corporation, Domus Development LLC, Mahogany and Oak Real Estate, Mutual Housing California, RHCB Development LP, Self-Help Enterprises and Visionary Home Builders of California.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). BHSA is the County's largest discretionary housing stream and the realistic source of project-based operating subsidy for PSH units in the unincorporated county and Stockton; capital is limited to the two named projects, so new deals should pitch rental or operating subsidies for behavioral health set-aside units rather than construction money. Behavioral Health Services also carries Homekey+ (Bridge to Home, $15,900,896 agreement with CVLIHC approved January 13, 2026) and Behavioral Health Bridge Housing Round 3 contracts, and is building the SJ BeWell campus with a $38.2 million BHCIP Round 2 award (March 2026). Pre-qualification through the BHS RFQ is the entry point for developers. The Board file (source) links the board order, the 490-page DHCS Integrated Plan submission (FileOpen.aspx?Type=4&ID=45761) and the June 2, 2026 presentation.
- Source: https://sanjoaquincountyca.iqm2.com/Citizens/Detail_LegiFile.aspx?ID=13401
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**CDBG and HOME, San Joaquin Urban County (Neighborhood Preservation Division NOFA)**
- Agency: San Joaquin County Human Services Agency, Neighborhood Preservation Division
- Type: grant
- Typical award: FY 2026-27 Urban County entitlements requested by jurisdiction: CDBG $2,948,080 (County share $1,740,858; Escalon $75,000; Lathrop $97,358; Manteca $420,911; Ripon $92,270; Tracy $521,683), HOME $973,290 (County share $663,627) and ESG $258,552, plus about $500,000 CDBG and $500,000 HOME program income. County HOME rental awards in 2026-27: DCDC Senior Housing at Salas Park (Lodi Commons) $230,024 entitlement plus $500,000 program income; STAND solar for permanent supportive shared housing $190,281; CHDO set-aside $145,993. Historic averages per the division: CDBG about $2.4 million a year, HOME about $700,000 a year, ESG about $200,000 a year.
- AMI requirement: HUD CDBG national objective (51% low and moderate income benefit) and HOME rent and income limits with HOME affordability periods; at least 15% of HOME to CHDOs. Lodi Commons (54 restricted senior units plus manager) is the current HOME-assisted rental example.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: 2026-27 cycle: NOFA posted December 8, 2025, applications due January 23, 2026 at 4:00 pm, virtual workshop December 17, 2025, Board public hearing May 12, 2026. The 2027-28 NOFA is expected in December 2026 with a late January 2027 deadline; confirm with Neighborhood Preservation at (209) 468-3175 or neighborhood@sjgov.org.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). The Urban County covers unincorporated San Joaquin County plus Escalon, Lathrop, Manteca, Ripon and Tracy (qualification package for FY 2027-28 through 2029-30 authorized August 4, 2026); Stockton and Lodi are separate HUD entitlements with their own NOFAs (Pass 3). Roughly half of CDBG and HOME passes through to the five cities, so County HOME available for rental development is typically under $1 million a year including program income; the 2026-27 draft Action Plan says requests far exceeded funds. HOME is used as a small soft loan behind 4% and 9% tax credit deals. Lodi Commons (Housing Authority, 55 units, TDC $28,463,882) stacks $3,031,559 of County HOME from three fiscal years, $2,000,000 County HOME-ARP, $1,000,000 County HHAP, $5,400,000 HACSJ funds, $665,000 City of Lodi CDBG, $2,000,000 Health Net, $3,000,000 San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund, $500,000 HUD CPF and about $10.7 million LIHTC equity, with 32 PBVs and 22 ACC units (FONSI dated November 4, 2026). Davis-Bacon applies to HOME-assisted projects of 12 or more units and to CDBG-assisted projects of 8 or more units; state prevailing wage applies to public subsidy. The Neighborhood Preservation pages moved from sjgov.org to sjchsa.org in 2026; the 2025-2029 Consolidated Plan and 2026-2027 Annual Action Plan are posted there.
- Source: https://www.sjchsa.org/Portals/0/resources/neighborhood-preservation/home/2026-2027-Public-Notice-Notice-of-Funding-Availability-(NOFA)-English.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**County General Fund one-time affordable housing contributions (no standing County housing trust fund)**
- Agency: San Joaquin County Board of Supervisors
- Type: grant
- Typical award: One-time allocations by Board action rather than a program: $1,500,000 approved August 8, 2023 to Visionary Home Builders for a 94-unit senior and family development in Downtown Stockton (TDC $60,518,336, leveraging about $34 million in state tax credits with HCD Infill Infrastructure Grant, City of Stockton HOME and a City impact fee waiver). The County has no housing trust fund, no housing bond and no dedicated housing revenue; the 2023-2031 Housing Element and the SJCOG feasibility study point to the regional fund as the vehicle instead.
- AMI requirement: Set project by project; the 2023 award prioritized occupancy for people living or working in the County.
- Cycle: closed/inactive (next: No application process. Requests are taken to the Board through Human Services Agency or Behavioral Health Services sponsorship.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). Recorded so the gap is explicit: County General Fund money for multifamily has been rare and discretionary, and the County's 2026 posture is to route capital through the SJCOG Regional Housing Fund (which the County helped capitalize through Measure K) and operating dollars through BHSA, HHAP and PLHA. Mountain House, incorporated in 2024, holds an $8.19 million affordable housing fund from its community services district era (Housing Element), now a city program for Pass 3.
- Source: https://www.sjgov.org/department/bos/board-news/board-news-detail/2023/08/09/san-joaquin-county-approves-1.5-million-to-help-build-94-unit-affordable-housing-development
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**County-owned and surplus land for affordable housing**
- Agency: San Joaquin County General Services (real property), with Behavioral Health Services and Veterans Services for sponsored projects
- Type: other
- Typical award: No standing County land program. Known actions: a Surplus Land Act notice of availability for about four acres of County-owned land adjacent to Gianone Park in Stockton for deed-restricted affordable housing by sale or long-term ground lease (posted November 20, 2025, offers due January 30, 2026, Board approval required); Victory Gardens, 49 veteran PSH units built on 2.5 acres of County land in French Camp; a proposed campus-style veterans housing village in French Camp near San Joaquin General Hospital pursued with a Homekey+ application in 2026.
- AMI requirement: Surplus Land Act priority for affordable housing developers with at least 25% of units at or below 80% AMI (Government Code 54222.5) or deeper restrictions negotiated with the County.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Gianone Park offering closed January 30, 2026; outcome not posted. Watch the County Purchasing bid page (sjgov.org/department/pss/bids, Public Purchase) and HCD's surplus land notices map for future notices.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). County land has been the basis for the county's veteran PSH projects and is the most likely form of County contribution to a new deal because the County has no capital fund. Marked needs_confirmation because the Gianone Park notice was read on a third-party procurement aggregator (source) and the County's own bid posting (sjgov.org/gems bidView bidId=206984) returned 404 on November 7, 2026; confirm with County General Services.

**Development impact fee deferral for affordable housing (Housing Element program, unincorporated county)**
- Agency: San Joaquin County Community Development Department and Public Works
- Type: fee waiver
- Typical award: Typical planning and development impact fees for a multifamily unit in the unincorporated county were about $20,000 to $20,500 per unit in 2023 (County fees of roughly $9,200 to $9,650 plus County facilities $1,564, regional transportation $2,664, habitat mitigation $1,929, school $4,041 and fire $620). The 2023-2031 Housing Element commits the County to process fee deferrals for projects affordable to lower-income households and to study fee waivers or reductions for affordable projects; no fee waiver fund or standing deferral ordinance was found.
- AMI requirement: Projects affordable to lower-income households as defined in the Housing Element programs; terms set case by case.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Request with the entitlement application; the Housing Element says the County will implement a deferral or reduction policy within three months of finding it feasible.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). Impact fees in the unincorporated county are low compared with Bay Area counties, so deferral is worth less than $20,000 per unit of carry; the larger cost issue is extending water and sewer to sites. Habitat mitigation (SJMSCP) and regional transportation fees are set by other agencies and are not waived by the County. Marked needs_confirmation because the Housing Element (opened) describes the policy as a commitment to study and implement, and no adopted deferral ordinance or fee schedule page was opened this pass; confirm with Community Development.
- Source: https://www.sjgov.org/Commdev/cgi-bin/cdyn.exe/file/Planning/Draft%20Documents%20for%20CDD%20Projects/2023-2031%20SJC%20Housing%20Element%20HCD%20Submittal%20Revised%20January%202025.pdf
- Confidence: needs_confirmation (researched 2026-11-07)

**HACSJ public housing repositioning and co-development (Delta Community Developers Corp.)**
- Agency: Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin and Delta Community Developers Corp. (DCDC), its nonprofit instrumentality
- Type: other
- Typical award: HACSJ is repositioning its 910-unit public housing portfolio through RAD, Section 18 and mixed-finance deals and is itself the county's most active supportive housing developer. Pipeline from the FY 2026-27 Annual Plan and development page: Tracy Apartments Phases I and II (55 units each, replacing 17 demolished Tracy Homes units; sources include Health Plan of San Joaquin, HOME, Capital Fund loan, Freddie Mac permanent loan, U.S. Bank, LIHTC); Lodi Commons and Danny Drive (110 senior units each in two phases); Edison House (33 PSH units, Homekey+, Stockton PIP, HHAP); Grant Street Apartments (24 behavioral health units, MHSA, HHAP, ARPA); Sierra Vista Phase III (106 units, Section 18 disposition of 26 units in process, up to 80 approved vacancies); Conway Homes phased redevelopment. Sierra Vista Phase I was co-developed with CCHC, and HACSJ has sought a development partner for Sonora Square.
- AMI requirement: Public housing and PBV income limits with long-term use restrictions under RAD or Section 18 conversion; LIHTC restrictions on mixed-finance phases.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Partner solicitations are issued project by project on hacsj.org procurement; none open as of November 7, 2026.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). For outside developers HACSJ matters in three ways: as a PBV source, as a co-developer or ground lessor on repositioned public housing sites in Stockton, Tracy and Lodi, and as a competitor for the same County HOME, HHAP, Health Net and Regional Housing Fund dollars (Lodi Commons took $3 million of the $11.85 million Round 1 fund). Davis-Bacon and state prevailing wage apply to mixed-finance public housing work. HACSJ also operates farm labor housing in Lodi, French Camp and Thornton. Source is the HACSJ development page, supplemented by the 2026-27 Annual Plan and the 2025-2029 5-Year Plan (hacsj.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/10.1.25-5-Year-Plan.pdf).
- Source: https://hacsj.org/development/
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**HHAP Round 6 local NOFA and Continuum of Care CA-511 (County and SJCoC allocations)**
- Agency: San Joaquin County Human Services Agency, Neighborhood Preservation Division, with the San Joaquin Continuum of Care
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HHAP-6 allocations: County of San Joaquin $5,399,638.58 and San Joaquin Continuum of Care $5,766,906.84 (City of Stockton receives its own share; the regional HHAP-6 plan totals $22,698,332.21). Regional HHAP-6 budget: permanent housing 18% (rapid rehousing and services, $2,042,849.90 each for the County and CoC lines), homeless prevention $4.1 million, interim housing $10.4 million, youth set-aside $2.27 million, administration $1.8 million, capital $0. HHAP-5 local awards: 30 agreements totaling $6,430,416.36 approved March 3, 2026 for March 3, 2026 through June 30, 2028, plus $1,000,000 HHAP-5 to the SJ BeWell Tiny Home Village.
- AMI requirement: Housing First compliant projects serving people experiencing homelessness; HHAP-6 priorities are sustaining existing permanent and interim housing investments. Applicants must be 501(c)(3) nonprofits, tribes or public agencies.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: HHAP-6 local NOFA released October 19, 2026; applications due 2:00 pm Friday, November 20, 2026 by email to fiscalneighborhood@sjgov.org or in person at 400 E. Main Street, Stockton.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). HHAP has been used as a small capital and operating piece in County-area PSH (for example $1,000,000 in Lodi Commons and $1,000,000 HHAP-5 for the BeWell tiny home village), but the HHAP-6 regional plan budgets $0 for capital and shifts money to sustaining operations, so developers should treat HHAP as an operating or services source for existing PSH rather than a construction source. The 2025 Regional Homeless Action Plan reports 240 PSH units built in the past five years and 200 plus in the pipeline, and a shelter inventory growing from 1,346 to 1,934 beds by the end of 2026. HHAP rounds 1 through 6 brought roughly $82.7 million to the region. HUD CoC Program funds flow through the SJCoC annual competition (2024 announcement posted on sjchsa.org); Stockton, the County and the CoC file a joint HHAP action plan.

**Health Net and Health Plan of San Joaquin housing investments (Medi-Cal plan capital grants)**
- Agency: Health Net (Centene) and Health Plan of San Joaquin, in partnership with HACSJ and the San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund
- Type: grant
- Typical award: Health Net granted $3,000,000 to the San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund (2026, $2,850,000 net available for loans) and $2,000,000 directly to Lodi Commons; Health Plan of San Joaquin is listed as a source for Tracy Apartments Phase I. Awards have been $2 million to $3 million per commitment.

- Cycle: rolling (next: No public application; commitments negotiated with the plans and routed through the Regional Housing Fund or directly to HACSJ projects.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). Managed care plan money is the newest soft source in the county and has shown up in every 2026 capital stack (Lodi Commons, Tracy Apartments, the Regional Housing Fund). It is grant money and does not by itself trigger prevailing wage, but it is always paired with public funds that do. Recorded at county level because the plans are county Medi-Cal plans and the money flows through countywide vehicles; figures are from the SJCOG March 11, 2026 announcement (source), the June 18, 2026 fund packet and the Lodi Commons FONSI.
- Source: https://sjcog.org/CivicAlerts.asp?AID=688
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Homekey+ and Behavioral Health Bridge Housing, County-sponsored projects**
- Agency: San Joaquin County Behavioral Health Services (Homekey+, BHBH) and Veterans Services Office
- Type: grant
- Typical award: Bridge to Home (Central Valley Low Income Housing Corporation): Homekey+ agreement for $15,900,896, January 13, 2026 through March 31, 2027, with a $12,785,399 appropriation in the FY 2025-26 Behavioral Health budget. Behavioral Health Bridge Housing Round 3: fourth amendment with CVLIHC (August 1, 2024 to June 30, 2027), $281,000 to the City of Manteca (March 1, 2026 to June 30, 2036) and $1,500,000 to Mahogany and Oak Consultancy (June 2, 2026 to June 30, 2027). HHAP-5 $1,000,000 for the SJ BeWell Tiny Home Village. HACSJ's Edison House (33 PSH units, 14 for veterans) is a state Homekey+ awardee. News reports describe a February 2026 Board consent action authorizing Veterans Services to apply for up to $38,500,000 of Homekey+ for a campus-style veterans housing village in French Camp near San Joaquin General Hospital; that item was not located in the Board files opened this pass.
- AMI requirement: Homekey+ and BHBH serve people experiencing homelessness with behavioral health conditions and veterans; PSH with 55-year restrictions for Homekey+ capital.
- Cycle: rolling (next: State Homekey+ applications are by state NOFA; the County sponsors applications case by case with Behavioral Health Services or Veterans Services as co-applicant. No County solicitation is open.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). The County's role is co-applicant and contract manager rather than lender; developers bring the site and the County brings match, operating funds (BHSA, BHBH, HHAP) and services. CVLIHC is the County's primary operator and Mahogany and Oak and HACSJ are the other repeat partners. Confirm the French Camp veterans village application status with the Veterans Services Office; the figure comes from a news summary of the February 2026 agenda rather than an opened Board file.
- Source: https://sanjoaquincountyca.iqm2.com/Citizens/Detail_LegiFile.aspx?ID=12929
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA), County formula allocation**
- Agency: San Joaquin County Human Services Agency, Neighborhood Preservation Division
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HCD formula allocation to the County: $919,667 (2024 allocation year), $985,099 (2025), estimated $5,747,919 for the 2024-2028 five-year plan; for 2019-2023 the County was eligible for an estimated $7,571,813 and had received $5,587,686 by early 2026. The County directs its PLHA to Activity 6 (emergency shelter and navigation center operations). 2026 NOFA: $1,884,922 from the 2022 and 2023 allocations; seven grant agreements approved June 30, 2026 (City of Manteca $206,324, City of Tracy $206,324, Gospel Center Rescue Mission $206,324, St. Mary's Community Services $206,324, Haven of Peace $400,000, HOPE Ministries $280,000, McHenry House of Tracy $379,626) running through June 30, 2030.
- AMI requirement: PLHA Activity 6 serves persons experiencing or at risk of homelessness; applicants must show site control for a navigation center. County retains 5% for administration.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Last County PLHA NOFA opened January 16, 2026 and closed February 20, 2026 at 4:00 pm; next cycle not announced. Contact fiscalneighborhood@sjgov.org.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). The County's PLHA five-year plan does not fund multifamily capital, so PLHA is not a County gap source for tax credit deals here; it sustains shelter operations that otherwise compete with HHAP. Stockton, Lodi, Tracy and Manteca receive their own PLHA formula allocations and may use them differently (Pass 3). County allocation figures are from the HCD 2024-2028 PLHA NOFA allocation tables; the 2019-2023 figures and the Activity 6 plan are from the County PLHA public notice (source).
- Source: https://www.sjchsa.org/Portals/0/resources/neighborhood-preservation/home/PLHA---PUBLIC-NOTICE.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Project-Based Vouchers, Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ)**
- Agency: Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (PHA code CA024), 2575 Grand Canal Boulevard, Suite 100, Stockton
- Type: other
- Typical award: HACSJ administers 5,380 Housing Choice Vouchers and 910 public housing units (FY 2026-27 Annual Plan). PBVs are awarded through Requests for Proposals for existing, new construction or substantially rehabilitated housing serving persons with disabilities, veterans and families; a past RFP offered up to 130 PBVs for existing units. FY 2026-27 project-basing: Tracy Apartments Phase I 32 PBVs plus 22 RAD and tenant protection vouchers (54 units), Lodi Commons 32 PBVs plus 22 ACC units (estimated PBV subsidy $152,172 a year), Edison House 19 PBVs plus 14 HUD-VASH (33 units), Grant Street Apartments 24 PBVs.
- AMI requirement: HUD PBV rules: households at or below 50% AMI at admission with 75% at or below 30% AMI; 15 to 20 year HAP contracts; HACSJ is not a Moving to Work agency so the 20% program cap and PBV competitive selection rules apply (exception for projects selected through a prior competitive public process).
- Cycle: rolling (next: No open PBV RFP found on hacsj.org procurement as of November 7, 2026; HACSJ reports it cannot apply for additional vouchers because of funding shortfalls, so new PBVs come from existing voucher authority. Monitor hacsj.org/resources/procurement and Public Purchase.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). PBVs are the main operating subsidy for PSH and senior deals in the county and underwrite debt on Measure-K and Regional Housing Fund projects; HACSJ attaches PBVs to its own Delta Community Developers Corp. projects and to partner projects. Davis-Bacon applies to PBV new construction and rehabilitation of nine or more units. HACSJ also runs HUD-VASH, Family Unification and Emergency Housing Vouchers (EHV sunset noted in 2026) and launched CASH SJC in August 2026, a $4.75 million CDSS-funded direct cash program for about 250 seniors on its waitlists. The 2026-27 Annual Plan (effective October 1, 2026) is the source; PBV program description is on hacsj.org/portfolio-item/project-based-voucher.
- Source: https://hacsj.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/26-27-Annual-Plan.pdf
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund (SJRHF) gap loans, SJCOG**
- Agency: San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund Inc. (501(c)(3) subsidiary of the San Joaquin Council of Governments), fund manager Harris and Associates
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Round 1 (June 2026) committed $11,850,000 in gap loans to four projects: Airport Way Apartments, Manteca (TPC Housing) $2,850,000; The Junction, Tracy (CRP Affordable Housing and Community Development) $3,000,000; Lodi Commons at Salas Park, Lodi (San Joaquin Housing Authority) $3,000,000; The Don Shalvey Apartments, Stockton (Visionary Home Builders) $3,000,000. Alternates: Countrywood Cottages, Escalon; Casa De Mariposa and The View at Channel, Stockton. $750,000 held in reserve. Capitalization net of administration: $4,750,000 HCD Local Housing Trust Fund grant, $5,000,000 SJCOG Measure K smart growth local match, $2,850,000 Health Net grant ($12.6 million total; $13 million plus gross including $75,000 from the San Joaquin Community Foundation). Enterprise reports the round supports 421 affordable homes.
- AMI requirement: 55-year regulatory agreement minimum; LHTF dollars prioritize extremely low and very low income units in new construction and permanent rental housing; Measure K match requires infill and smart growth locations; Health Net dollars are flexible but prioritize low-income units. Preference for sites near transit and services.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Round 1 closed; funding commitment letters carry a one-year term with a one-year extension option tied to tax credit outcomes. No Round 2 date is set; SJCOG is seeking new capital and the Advisory Committee meets quarterly (March 4, June 3, September 2 and December 2, 2027). An ADU pilot of about $300,000 in REAP 2.0 funds is in design. Contact Joann Martinez, SJCOG, (209) 235-0582.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). This is the only countywide gap lender and the first new local soft money in the county in years; awards of $2.85 million to $3 million were sized to close 4% bond and LIHTC deals already in the pipeline, and funds disburse only after all other financing is secured. Loan terms: 3% simple interest or the Freddie Mac 30-year rate, 55-year term extendable 25 years, annual payments from 50% of residual receipts shared pro rata with other public lenders, 1% origination fee, debt service coverage of 1.15 or better in year one (TCAC minimum operating expenses per Title 4 Section 10327 unless justified). Public money from the fund is treated as a public subsidy for prevailing wage. The SJRHF board is the SJCOG board (Escalon, Lathrop, Lodi, Manteca, Mountain House, Ripon, San Joaquin County, Stockton, Tracy). Source is the June 18, 2026 Advisory Committee agenda packet with the award table and underwriting summaries; guidelines are in the April 13, 2026 packet (sjcog.org/AgendaCenter/ViewFile/Agenda/_04132026-1710).
- Source: https://www.sjcog.org/AgendaCenter/ViewFile/Agenda/_06182026-1726
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Welfare Exemption (property tax), San Joaquin County Assessor**
- Agency: San Joaquin County Assessor-Recorder-County Clerk, Office of the Assessor, 44 North San Joaquin Street, Second Floor Suite 230, Stockton, (209) 468-2630
- Type: tax abatement
- Typical award: Full exemption of the ad valorem property tax on qualifying low-income rental housing (about 1% of assessed value plus voter-approved debt each year), applied by unit share for LIHTC and other restricted projects under Revenue and Taxation Code 214(g).
- AMI requirement: Property owned and operated by a qualifying nonprofit or a limited partnership with a nonprofit managing general partner holding a BOE Organizational Clearance Certificate; units restricted to lower-income households by regulatory agreement or tax credits; BOE-267 series claim forms filed with the Assessor.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: February 15, 2027 for the 2027-28 roll; claims filed February 16 through December 31 receive a partial exemption; late deadlines roll to the next business day.)
- Layering notes: San Joaquin County has no county housing trust fund, no county housing bond and no inclusionary ordinance; the County's own capital for multifamily is limited to HUD pass-through funds administered by the Human Services Agency Neighborhood Preservation Division (sjchsa.org), while the regional gap lender is the SJCOG-sponsored San Joaquin Regional Housing Fund and the main voucher and development partner is the Housing Authority of the County of San Joaquin (HACSJ). Standard California process: the Assessor page lists rental housing for lower-income households and HUD-financed elderly or disabled housing among eligible uses and requires a new claim every year. Budget the first-year supplemental and the partial-exemption rule when closing late in the year. Assessor name is on the office leadership page, not the exemption page.
- Source: https://www.sjgov.org/department/assessor/tax-savings-exemptions/welfare-exemption
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)


### San Luis Obispo County

**Behavioral Health Services Act (BHSA) Housing Interventions and capital development RFP, 2026-2029 Integrated Plan**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Behavioral Health Department
- Type: grant
- Typical award: Housing Interventions budget $6,930,885 (FY 2026/27), $6,792,267 (FY 2027/28), $6,656,422 (FY 2028/29), 30% of BHSA. Funds about 107 PSH beds, 86 assisted living and board and care beds, 10 time-limited beds, FSP housing, landlord mitigation and participant assistance. Capital development: yes, one project in Year 1 via an RFP for a minimum of two units at up to $450,000 of BHSA per unit, braided with other funds, available by June 30, 2027, with by-right provisions required. Behavioral Health Bridge Housing beds start Year 2.
- AMI requirement: BHSA-eligible adults with serious mental illness or substance use disorder experiencing or at risk of homelessness; half of housing funds for chronic homelessness.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Capital development RFP expected in FY 2026/27 (units by June 30, 2027). Plan submitted to DHCS March 2026, final June 30, 2026; annual updates follow.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. The capital set-aside is tiny (two units), so BHSA matters mainly as an operating and rental subsidy source for PSH units in a larger project. Transitions-Mental Health Association is the County's main housing partner. Contact Behavioral Health about committing operating subsidies to units in a Homekey+ or LIHTC project.
- Source: https://www.slocounty.ca.gov/departments/slo-health/behavioral-health/behavioral-health-projects/integrated-plan-behavioral-health-services-act/integrated-plan-documents/slobhd-integrated-plan-and-bos-certification
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Department of Social Services, Homeless Services Division
- Type: grant

Separately the County is advancing Welcome Home Village (40 permanent supportive housing units and 14 interim shelter units, anticipated opening spring 2026) with HHAP and County funds. The County's FY 2025/26 contracts for services, housing and facilities totaled $13,646,539.60. Earlier Homekey: City of San Luis Obispo Calle Joaquin (76 units, $19.4 million Homekey plus $2.6 million County SB 1090).
- AMI requirement: Permanent supportive housing for people experiencing homelessness with behavioral health needs or veterans.

- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). **Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) Project-Based Vouchers and development partnerships**
- Agency: Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo and San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation (affiliate)
- Type: other
- Typical award: HASLO administers more than 2,000 Housing Choice Vouchers countywide and converted its public housing to tax credit and PBV units under RAD; there is no public housing in the county. Its nonprofit affiliate develops affordable housing countywide (Monterey Family Apartments 50 units, Balay Ko on Monterey 56 senior units, Arroyo Terrace 63 units, a 34-unit mixed-use project with Transitions-Mental Health Association). The CoC and HASLO run homeless admission preferences (up to 12 mainstream vouchers) and apply jointly for FUP and Emergency Housing Vouchers.
- AMI requirement: Households at or below 50% AMI at admission (75% at or below 30% AMI); PBV units meet Housing Quality Standards.
- Cycle: closed/inactive (next: No PBV owner RFP posted on haslo.org as of November 8, 2026; HASLO attaches PBVs mainly to its own and partner projects. Paso Robles Housing Authority serves that city.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. - Agency: San Luis Obispo County Department of Planning and Building
- Type: other

**Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA) loans under the County 5-Year Plan**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Department of Social Services, Homeless Services Division
- Type: loan
- Typical award: $149,238.80 of 2021 PLHA offered in the 2027 Action Plan NOFA under Plan Activity 2 (predevelopment, development, acquisition, rehabilitation and preservation of affordable rental and ownership housing including ADUs for workforce households up to 120% AMI). The NOFA states multifamily rental projects are not eligible under Activity 2 in this round.
