**Prohousing Designation (HCD, October 2025)**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Department of Planning and Building
- Type: other
- Typical award: HCD awarded the County a Prohousing Designation in October 2025 for lowering permit barriers for affordable housing, incentivizing density through land use changes (Action 1 multifamily update) and creating the Regional Housing Fund. Designation gives projects in the unincorporated county priority or bonus points in state programs including the Prohousing Incentive Program, AHSC, Caltrans planning grants and others.
- AMI requirement: Not applicable to the designation.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Designation is active; the County may apply for Prohousing Incentive Program funds in HCD's next round.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. Cite the designation in AHSC, Multifamily Housing Program and Infill Infrastructure Grant applications for unincorporated sites. Pair with Regional Housing Incentive density and the Housing Trust Fund.
- Source: https://www.slocounty.ca.gov/departments/administrative-office/featured-news/2025/state-designates-slo-county-as-prohousing%E2%80%9D-supervisors-adopt-new-housing-policies-to-boost-housing
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Regional Housing Fund and Regional Housing Grant (County Code Title 31, adopted October 21, 2025)**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Department of Social Services (grant) with Department of Planning and Building (fee collection)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: New fund capitalized by Regional Housing Incentive in-lieu fees ($30,000 per Incentive Point initially, range $30,000 to $50,000, ENR-indexed from July 1, 2026 and adjusted 5% up or down from July 1, 2029 based on program use), plus any General Fund, grants or private contributions. 75% goes to the Guaranteed Affordable Housing Account for the Regional Housing Grant and 25% to a Public Transportation Support Account. The County said the program is meant to fill the funding gap for roughly 280 already-approved affordable units. No awards yet and no balance published.
- AMI requirement: New guaranteed affordable units for very low, low or moderate income households (rental or ownership) in unincorporated urban areas consistent with Housing Element Policy HE 1.01; covenants per Section 22.12.030.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Annual awards recommended by Social Services to the Board; the first Regional Housing Grant round depends on fee receipts and had not been announced as of November 8, 2026. Watch the Action Plan NOFA each September.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. This is the County's replacement for inclusionary revenue, but it is voluntary and fee-driven, so capitalization will be slow. Developers with entitled but unfunded unincorporated projects (the 280-unit backlog) should register interest with HSD so they are in line for the first round. Likely to be administered through the same Neighborly NOFA as HOME and Title 29.
- Source: https://www.slocounty.ca.gov/departments/planning-building/forms-documents/housing-forms-and-documents/2020-2028-housing-element-documents/action-2-incentive/action-02-t31-regional-housing-fund
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Regional Housing Incentive Program (Title 22 Chapter 22.12.010, adopted October 21, 2025)**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Department of Planning and Building
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Voluntary program for multifamily projects wholly inside an Urban Reserve Line. Projects accrue Incentive Points (two per on-site very low income unit, or by paying the Title 31 in-lieu fee of $30,000 per point through June 30, 2026, indexed to ENR construction costs after) and spend them on standards relief and density increases that count as base density before the state density bonus. Very low income units funded with government grants or tax credits do not earn points unless the project is 100% affordable (very low, low and moderate). Phase 2 (Actions 7 and 8, introduced November 4, 2025, scoped April 21, 2026) would add ministerial permitting for participating projects.
- AMI requirement: On-site units restricted to very low income under Section 22.12.030 Housing Affordability Standards (long-term covenants); in-lieu option carries no on-site units.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Not applicable; requested with the land use permit. Inland ordinance effective 30 days after October 21, 2025 adoption; Coastal Zone version pending Coastal Commission certification.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. For a 100% affordable LIHTC project the program adds density on top of the state bonus (density increases apply to base density first). Companion Action 1 (September 23, 2025) rewrote countywide multifamily standards and dropped minimum density requirements. Confirm point values and incentive table with Planning and Building; the adopted table is not reproduced in the text copy of the ordinance.
- Source: https://www.slocounty.ca.gov/departments/planning-building/forms-documents/housing-forms-and-documents/2020-2028-housing-element-documents/action-2-incentive/action-02-t22-incentive-program-(clean)
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**SB 1090 Diablo Canyon settlement funds for affordable housing**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Board of Supervisors with Department of Social Services, Homeless Services Division
- Type: grant
- Typical award: SB 1090 (2018) directed Diablo Canyon closure mitigation funds to the County with a stipulation to support affordable housing in the region. Awards by Board action: $1.3 million to Cambria Pines Apartments (Peoples' Self-Help Housing, 33 units, $28 million total) approved June 17, 2025; $2.6 million to the City of San Luis Obispo's Calle Joaquin Homekey motel conversion (76 units) in 2024. The County's 2025 annual report also cites a $2.5 million CDBG-DR mitigation award.
- AMI requirement: Set by project; used for affordable and supportive housing in communities affected by the plant closure.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No NOFA; awards are negotiated with HSD and approved by the Board. Remaining SB 1090 housing balance not published.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. SB 1090 is the County's only large discretionary gap source and has gone to shovel-ready projects with TCAC awards or Homekey grants. **State Density Bonus and Other Incentives (Title 22 Section 22.12.020) and 2025 multifamily standards update**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Department of Planning and Building
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Implements Government Code 65915: density bonus, concessions and reduced parking for projects with very low, lower or moderate income units, units for transitional foster youth, disabled veterans or homeless persons at very low income rents, senior housing and mixed use, above the maximum density set by Planning Area Standards. Action 1 (September 23, 2025) updated countywide multifamily regulations in Titles 22 and 23 (inland effective October 23, 2025; coastal pending Coastal Commission). Action 4 and 5 (June 3, 2025) updated ADU, urban dwelling and urban lot split rules.
- AMI requirement: Deed restrictions per project approval under Section 22.12.030 (55 years rental under state law).
- Cycle: rolling (next: Not applicable; applied with the land use permit.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. Stack the state bonus on top of Regional Housing Incentive density (which is treated as base density). The County's Prohousing Designation (October 2025) and the Housing Element implementation framework favor sites within Urban Reserve Lines along Highway 101 and in Nipomo, Templeton, Los Osos and Cambria.

**Title 29 Affordable Housing Fund (residual inclusionary in-lieu and housing impact fees)**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Department of Social Services, Homeless Services Division (fund created under County Code Title 29)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: Fund balance $558,321.03 at July 1, 2025; $108,775.83 drawn and $205,734 committed to prior projects in FY 2025/26; $154,616.15 offered in the 2027 Action Plan NOFA ($56,199.01 Open to All, $13,251.59 Central County, $124,427.55 South County; North County and North Coast exhausted). Recent allocations: Rolling Hills III $89,978, Monterey Family Apartments $69,448, Cambria Pines $46,308, Arroyo Terrace $254,972, Balay Ko $228,641. 119 Title 29-assisted units complete in FY 2026/27.
- AMI requirement: Households at or below 80% AMI under County Affordable Housing Standards; 55-year rental and 45-year ownership covenants. Awards are grants, except TCAC projects must take a loan (typically 3% simple interest). Funds must be spent in the market area where fees were collected.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Included in the 2027 Action Plan NOFA (closed October 30, 2026). Remaining balances are small and will be exhausted within one or two cycles; no new revenue since the June 7, 2022 repeal.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. Title 29 is a shrinking residual pot restricted by market area (South County has the largest remaining balance). Use it as a small TCAC local contribution alongside HOME and PLHA. The County's July 2026 risk analysis holds back $110,148 for possible permit refunds and $39,262 for Open to All refunds.
- Source: https://www.slocounty.ca.gov/departments/social-services/forms-documents/homeless-services/homepage/2026/title-29-report-for-fy-2025-26

**Urban County Action Plan NOFA: HOME and CDBG affordable housing development loans**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Department of Social Services, Homeless Services Division
- Type: loan
- Typical award: 2027 Action Plan NOFA (issued September 28, 2026, applications due October 30, 2026): HOME estimated $690,900 available for projects (allocation $767,667), CDBG $1,418,895 across the Urban County (County share $368,732 for projects; cities $30,236 to $371,200 each). 2026 Action Plan (approved April 7, 2026) put $499,969 of HOME into one affordable rental project and CDBG into rehab, shelters and a recuperative care facility. Recent HOME and Title 29 layered projects: Rolling Hills III (Templeton, 28 units, Peoples' Self-Help Housing), Cambria Pines (33 units, $373,418 HOME/PLHA/Title 29 in 2025), Monterey Family Apartments (50 units), Balay Ko on Monterey (56 senior units) and Arroyo Terrace (63 units), the last three by HASLO's nonprofit.
- AMI requirement: HOME: 90% of households at or below 60% AMI, 20% of units at 50% AMI in projects of five or more HOME units; affordability 20 years for new construction (longer under County agreements); 25% HOME match and 15% CHDO set-aside. CDBG: 51% low and moderate income.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: 2027 Action Plan applications closed October 30, 2026; draft housing recommendations hearing February 2027; Board of Supervisors approval April 2027; FY 2027/28 awards begin July 1, 2027. The 2028 Action Plan NOFA is expected in September 2027.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. Scoring (100 points) weights scope and expertise (40), funding readiness (15), cost effectiveness and unit count (20), entitlement readiness (10), site standards, vulnerable population targeting and alignment with the Countywide Plan to Address Homelessness (15). HOME awards are small ($400,000 to $900,000) and are typically layered with Title 29, PLHA, SB 1090 and Housing Trust Fund loans under TCAC. Apply through Neighborly; attend the October information session.
- Source: https://www.slocounty.ca.gov/departments/social-services/forms-documents/homeless-services/funding-availability/2027/2027-action-plan-nofa
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Welfare exemption from property tax for nonprofit-owned affordable housing**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Assessor, 1055 Monterey Street, San Luis Obispo, 805-781-5000 (County main line)
- Type: tax abatement
- Typical award: Exempts the affordable share of assessed value for qualifying nonprofit or LP-with-nonprofit-GP owners under Revenue and Taxation Code 214(g); worth roughly 1.0% of assessed value plus voter-approved rates each year. The Assessor's exemptions page posts BOE-267, 267-A, 267-H, 267-L, 267-L1, 267-L3, 267-L4, 267-L-A, 267-O, 267-R, 231-AH, 236 and 236-A.
- AMI requirement: Units restricted to lower income households (and up to 140% AMI continuing residents under 267-L3/L4 rules) with a recorded regulatory agreement; BOE Organizational Clearance Certificate required.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: File BOE-267 (first year) or BOE-267-A with the applicable 267-L supplement by February 15 each year.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. Standard statewide exemption; the Assessor posts all low income housing forms online. Underwrite as an operating saving on every LIHTC and PSH project.

### San Mateo County

**Behavioral Health Services Act (BHSA) housing interventions and MHSA/BHSA capital through the Affordable Housing Fund**
- Agency: San Mateo County Health, Behavioral Health and Recovery Services (BHRS), with the Department of Housing administering capital awards
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Three-Year Integrated Plan FY 2026-27 to FY 2028-29 approved by the Board June 9, 2026 (Behavioral Health Commission hearing closed March 5, 2026). Housing Interventions expenditures $24,427,542 (FY 2026-27), $24,374,720 (FY 2027-28), $24,374,720 (FY 2028-29); capital development $6,152,822 then $6,100,000 a year (24% of the component, near the 25% cap) delivered through the DOH Affordable Housing Fund NOFAs, where the AHF 14 round carried $5,000,000 of MHSA/BHSA capital at up to $200,000 per restricted unit; the plan lists nine capital projects and a 65-unit PSH pipeline at up to $450,000 per unit. Operating subsidies $3,540,627 plus $2,182,327, bundled rental and operating subsidies about $1,160,000 to $1,460,000, rental subsidies $1,000,000; $12,900,025 a year (51%) dedicated to chronically homeless people; 64 people a year in operating-subsidized units. San Mateo joined the state Flex Pool Technical Assistance Academy.
- AMI requirement: MHSA/BHSA units for BHRS-referred adults with serious mental illness or substance use disorder who are homeless or at risk: 30% AMI with PBVs or 20% AMI without, Housing First, on-site supportive services; 55-year County regulatory agreement through the AHF loan.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Apply through the AHF NOFA (next round expected fall 2027) and meet with BHRS before applying; the plan runs July 1, 2026 to June 30, 2029 with annual updates. Ask BHRS about operating subsidies and transitional rent for MHSA/BHSA units, which the AHF NOFA counts toward its homelessness preference requirement.)
- Layering notes: San Mateo keeps BHSA capital at the statutory maximum and routes it through the same AHF loan documents as Measure K, so a PSH sponsor can get Measure K plus about $200,000 per BHSA unit in one award with BHRS operating subsidy behind it. State funds trigger prevailing wage. Plan read from the Board attachment on Legistar (final plan PDF, opened); the smchealth.org copies returned 404.
- Source: https://sanmateocounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=94826&GUID=9A552B75-9564-4B1D-A51C-4144E6EBF1C3&Options=&Search=&FullText=1
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Daly City/San Mateo County Continuum of Care (CA-512) and HHAP**
- Agency: San Mateo County Human Services Agency, Center on Homelessness (CoC lead agency and HHAP administrative entity for the County and CoC)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HHAP joint County and CoC allocations: Round 1 $3,431,246 (Feb 2020), Round 2 $1,597,106, Round 3 $4,375,129, Round 4 $4,658,201, Round 5 $5,237,946 (County $2,564,722, CoC $2,673,224), Round 6 $5,026,361 (County $2,430,522, CoC $2,595,839; accepted June 10, 2025, awarded March 2, 2026, usable through June 30, 2029), about $24,300,000 in all. State accountability site: $19,300,000 HHAP rounds 1 to 5 to the County ($13,800,000 obligated, $11,800,000 spent by mid-2025); Encampment Resolution Funding $16,000,000 in the area, $14,100,000 to the County. HHAP-6 supports operations of the 240-unit Navigation Center in Redwood City. HUD CoC program FY 2024 awards to CA-512: $817,332 (Shelter Plus Care sponsor-based $368,857, Vendome $303,133, Redwood Family House $145,342). State ESG CoC share $188,054 (2024). 2024 PIT: 2,130 people, 1,145 unsheltered, 3,454 year-round beds. Homekey: Casa Esperanza, Redwood City (former Comfort Inn, 51 PSH units, $15,978,232 Homekey plus up to $7,697,170 ARPA match, operated by Episcopal Community Services since 2025 under a 55-year ground lease with County Housing Voucher Program operating subsidies) and a Ramada conversion in South San Francisco with ECS; Homekey+ co-applicant RFP (Jan 15 to Feb 5, 2025) to pair developer teams and sites with County match for 2025 applications.
- AMI requirement: People experiencing or at risk of homelessness per HUD and state definitions; CoC permanent housing through Coordinated Entry; HHAP-6 must obligate 75% and spend 50% by June 30, 2027.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: HHAP-7 is available to HHAP-6 grantees; the CoC runs the HUD NOFO each summer. For capital, the County route is the Homekey+ co-applicant process through the Department of Housing and the AHF NOFA's homelessness preference units; HHAP itself funds shelter and services.)
- Layering notes: A small CoC (about $800,000 a year in HUD renewals) and modest HHAP, so homeless capital comes from Measure K and Homekey rather than CoC money; the County uses its own Housing Voucher Program and HACSM PBVs for operating subsidy at Homekey sites. HHAP figures from the County HSA HHAP page and Board item 25-554 (opened), HCD allocation lists and accountability.ca.gov; Homekey details from Board item 25-249 and the Homekey+ RFP page.
- Source: https://www.smcgov.org/hsa/homeless-housing-assistance-and-prevention-hhap-program-0
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Development fee waiver for affordable housing (no countywide program)**
- Agency: San Mateo County Planning and Building Department
- Type: fee waiver
- Typical award: None adopted. The County charges affordable housing impact fees and planning and building fees on unincorporated projects; no waiver or deferral program for affordable housing was found. The AHF NOFA scores local contributions such as land, fee waivers or inclusionary contributions from host cities as leverage toward its $1,000,000 or $20,000 per unit third-party commitment threshold.
- AMI requirement: Not applicable.
- Cycle: closed/inactive (next: Not applicable. Ask the host city for fee waivers; in the unincorporated area budget full County fees.)
- Layering notes: Negative finding. Labor Code 1720(b)(4) counts waived fees as public funds for prevailing wage purposes. Planning fees page is linked from the County inclusionary ordinance page (opened).
- Source: https://smcgov.org/planning/inclusionary-affordable-housing-ordinance
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**HACSM Moving to Work funds for affordable housing development (through the Affordable Housing Fund)**
- Agency: Housing Authority of the County of San Mateo (MTW Activity 2011-16 and development activity) with the Department of Housing
- Type: loan
- Typical award: HUD has approved HACSM's use of unspent HAP reserves (MTW funds) for affordable housing development in steps: $4,000,000 originally, $8,000,000 (FY 2013), $10,000,000 (FY 2016), $25,000,000 (FY 2020), $35,000,000 (FY 2024) and $65,000,000 (FY 2025). The FY 2027 MTW Plan reports $65,000,000 of MTW funds approved for the Affordable Housing Fund, including $7,900,000 for Ridge at Ralston in Belmont and $8,600,000 for 493 Eastmoor in Daly City.
- AMI requirement: Low, very low and extremely low income rental housing in San Mateo County; HACSM loans are typically zero interest with principal repaid from residual receipts under the AHF loan documents, 55-year covenants.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: Allocated through the AHF NOFA when HACSM has reserves to contribute; next AHF round expected fall 2027. Ask DOH whether HACSM funds are in the pool before applying.)
- Layering notes: Unusual source: HUD lets this MTW agency lend its unspent voucher money as capital, and the County has pushed the ceiling to $65,000,000. It arrives inside an AHF award as a zero-interest residual receipts loan, which helps cash flow versus the County's 3% Measure K loan. Federal HAP money carries Davis-Bacon and Section 3; state prevailing wage applies through the County loan. Read from the FY 2027 MTW Annual Plan PDF (opened).
- Source: https://www.smcgov.org/media/161447/download
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Housing Endowment and Regional Trust of San Mateo County (HEART) developer loans**
- Agency: HEART of San Mateo County, joint powers authority of the County and 20 cities and towns; 2905 S. El Camino Real, San Mateo; Armando Sanchez 650-204-5640
- Type: loan
- Typical award: HEART reports $57,000,000 invested in affordable housing development. Two products: QuickStart (up to five years, predevelopment, acquisition, construction or rehab, principal deferred to maturity) and SemiPerm (five to 15 years including extensions, interest-only or amortized on a 30-year schedule with balloon, for acquisition, predevelopment, refinancing and rehab). Rates are described as favorable and competitive and quoted on request; origination fee 2% of loans up to $2,000,000 and 1.5% above, $3,000 application fee, borrower pays lender legal and third-party costs; loan size depends on project financials and repayment source. Recent County relationships: a $2,400,000 agreement (June 16, 2026) for HEART to run the County's mobile home replacement loan program, and a $450,000 HEART loan in HEART-SV's plan to take over Visitacion Gardens in Brisbane (May 2026). Funded projects include Montara at Bay Meadows, Ocean View, Peninsula Station, Trestle Glen, Hillcrest Senior, Serenity Senior, Fair Oaks Commons, The Village at the Crossing and 2000 South Delaware.
- AMI requirement: Up to 120% AMI (workforce) with a 55-year affordability covenant on a number of units commensurate with HEART's share, deferring to other funders' covenants when sufficient; projects must be in a member jurisdiction (the County and 20 cities and towns: Atherton, Belmont, Brisbane, Burlingame, Colma, Daly City, East Palo Alto, Foster City, Half Moon Bay, Hillsborough, Menlo Park, Millbrae, Pacifica, Portola Valley, Redwood City, San Bruno, San Carlos, San Mateo, South San Francisco, Woodside and the County) with an adopted or under-review housing element.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Rolling; contact HEART for current rates and available funds. The 2026 Board report notes HEART is restructuring programs (first-time homebuyer loans ended Jan 28, 2026). HEART also runs an ADU design center and received state LHTF matching grants in past rounds.)
- Layering notes: HEART is bridge and acquisition money, not permanent gap financing: use QuickStart to tie up a site or carry predevelopment until an AHF award and tax credits close, then take out the loan. Member dues are population based and the County administers HEART, so it reads as a county-level source even though cities co-own it. Public agency funds, so assume state prevailing wage applies to construction financed with HEART loans. Term sheets (May 24, 2023) and member list opened on heartofsmc.org.
- Source: https://www.heartofsmc.org/programs/for-developers/
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Inclusionary Affordable Housing Ordinance (2004) with 2026 rezoning amendments, County affordable housing impact fees, and state density bonus**
- Agency: San Mateo County Planning and Building Department, 455 County Center, Redwood City; impact fees deposited to the Department of Housing's Housing Trust Fund
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Multifamily projects of five or more units in the unincorporated area must make 20% of units affordable, typically to lower income households (a rental project of five to nine units may provide its one required unit at moderate income; ownership projects may provide half at moderate income), with in-lieu fees, off-site units, land dedication or rehabilitation of existing affordable units allowed at the County's sole discretion. Ordinance 4927 (adopted Oct 6, 2026) removes those alternatives and the moderate-income option in the new R3-MD, PC-HD and TS-MU districts rezoned April 21, 2026 in Colma, Broadmoor and the Harbor Industrial area: 20% on site at low, very low or extremely low income. Affordable housing impact fees on residential and non-residential development were adopted in 2016 from a multi-jurisdictional nexus study and fund the AHF ($100,000 offered in each of AHF 13 and 14 for unincorporated projects); a new Multi-Jurisdictional Inclusionary Housing and Non-Residential Linkage Fee Study (Strategic Economics, $619,135, Dec 2025 to June 2027, County share $51,681) will reset fee levels for the County and ten cities. Density bonus follows Government Code 65915; the County enforces state law and has no local bonus beyond it.
- AMI requirement: Inclusionary units at lower income rents or prices for the ordinance term (long-term covenants recorded at entitlement); state density bonus units 55 years for rental.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Not applicable; applies at entitlement. Expect revised impact fee levels after the nexus study concludes in mid-2027. Unincorporated areas include North Fair Oaks, Colma, Broadmoor, Harbor Industrial, the Midcoast and Emerald Hills.)
- Layering notes: North Fair Oaks and the Colma and Broadmoor rezoning areas are where County inclusionary rules bite; 100% affordable projects satisfy them automatically and take the state density bonus. In-lieu fees and impact fees are small (about $100,000 a year reaches the AHF) because unincorporated development volume is low. Density bonus alone does not trigger prevailing wage. Sources: County inclusionary ordinance page (the ordinance PDF is image-only), Board items 26-817 and 25-1053 and the Oct 6, 2026 Board presentation and Ordinance 4927 (opened).
- Source: https://smcgov.org/planning/inclusionary-affordable-housing-ordinance
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA), San Mateo County entitlement**
- Agency: San Mateo County Department of Housing (HCD entitlement grantee)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: 2019-2023 five-year formula estimate $7,257,300 (CY 2021 funds $2,068,889). Calendar year 2022 and 2023 funds of $1,831,715 ($1,036,938 plus $794,777) were authorized Dec 2, 2025 and awarded by HCD in July 2026 after the County's housing element came into compliance; $824,272 is committed through AHF 13 (moved from Laurel Landing to Rotary Gardens on Aug 25, 2026) and the balance is being allocated over the counter. HCD 2024-2028 Formula Allocation NOFA estimates: CY 2024 $849,022, CY 2025 $909,428, five-year $5,306,388.
- AMI requirement: County PLHA plan funds Activity 1 (rental development for extremely low to moderate income households with 55-year covenants through the AHF process) and Activity 6 (homeless services, shelter and navigation center operations and case management); 2024 HCD rule requires 40% of new funds for affordable owner-occupied workforce housing unless amended.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: PLHA is offered through the AHF NOFA, the Winter NOFA and over-the-counter processes as HCD releases each year's funds; CY 2022 and 2023 money must be spent on HCD's deadlines, which drove the Aug 2026 reallocation. Ask DOH whether CY 2024 and 2025 funds will appear in the fall 2027 AHF round.)
- Layering notes: Small and lumpy because HCD withheld San Mateo's PLHA for several years over housing element compliance; the County treats it as a swap source inside AHF awards (PLHA to the project closing soonest, Measure K to the slower one). State funds trigger prevailing wage. Figures from Board items 25-1006 and 26-706 read through the Legistar API and the HCD 2024-2028 PLHA NOFA.
- Source: https://sanmateocounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=95103&GUID=5E955EF2-AB26-4DAB-8CC5-3ABE4B451365&Options=&Search=&FullText=1
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Project-Based Vouchers, Housing Authority of the County of San Mateo (HACSM, Moving to Work agency)**
- Agency: Housing Authority of the County of San Mateo, a division of the Department of Housing, 264 Harbor Blvd, Belmont, 650-802-3300; housing@smchousing.org
- Type: other
- Typical award: HACSM assists about 4,600 households and project-bases 1,930 vouchers (FY 2027 MTW Plan), with 36 new PBVs planned for 493 Eastmoor Avenue in Daly City (72 units). As an MTW agency since 1998 HACSM may project-base up to 40% of budget authority (raised from 35% in FY 2021) and awards PBVs through periodic RFPs: the last offered 200 Section 8 PBVs and 35 HUD-VASH PBVs for new construction (released Dec 2022, due Dec 30, 2022, awards Jan 2023), with at least 25% of PBVs as supportive housing units, 50% on two- and three-bedroom units, and a per-project cap of about 50% of units (waivable for supportive housing). In Jan 2025 DOH told Homekey+ applicants no PBVs were available. HACSM also provides HAP contracts up to 20 years and the County Housing Voucher Program (local funds) for Homekey sites such as Casa Esperanza.
- AMI requirement: Households at or below 50% AMI at admission with ELI targeting; supportive housing units for homeless and special needs households referred through Coordinated Entry; new construction only in the last RFP; 20-year HAP contracts with extensions.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No PBV RFP open as of Nov 2026 and none since Dec 2022; the AHF NOFA states applications may not assume PBVs without an existing HACSM award letter. Watch the DOH NOFAs, Bids and Proposals page and the annual MTW Plan (FY 2027 plan posted 2026) for the next solicitation. HACSM covers the entire county; no city in San Mateo County runs its own housing authority.)
- Layering notes: HACSM is the operating subsidy partner for every County-funded PSH deal and uses MTW flexibility to award PBVs in bulk (1,930 outstanding), but supply is tight and awards come through RFPs rather than the AHF NOFA. A HAP contract is not a prevailing wage trigger; Davis-Bacon applies to PBV new construction or rehab of nine or more contract units. HACSM also recycles MTW HAP reserves into the Affordable Housing Fund (separate record). Sources opened: 2022 RFP page and PDF, FY 2027 MTW Annual Plan, Homekey+ RFP Q&A.
- Source: https://www.smcgov.org/housing/rfp-section-8-and-hud-vash-project-based-vouchers
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**San Mateo County Affordable Housing Fund (AHF) annual NOFA (Measure K, County impact fees, MHSA/BHSA, PLHA, HOME)**
- Agency: San Mateo County Department of Housing (DOH), Housing and Community Development division, 264 Harbor Blvd, Building A, Belmont; Raymond Hodges, Director; applications through OpenGov (procurement.opengov.com/portal/smcgov)
- Type: loan
- Typical award: AHF 14.0 (released April 20, 2026, pre-application meeting by April 30, applications due May 20, 2026): $29,347,311 offered, made up of $24,247,311 Measure K ($19,000,000 new FY 2026-27 plus $5,247,311 recaptured), $100,000 County impact fees (unincorporated projects only) and $5,000,000 MHSA/BHSA. Ten applications sought more than $70,000,000 for 810 units. Board awards Aug 25, 2026: $24,347,311 to six projects totaling 459 units: Sand Hill Rise City, Redwood City $9,389,000 (94 units); Mercy Housing 555 Kelly, Half Moon Bay $6,575,000 (40); Related 1051 Mission, South San Francisco $3,545,414 (158, plus $9,495,420 from AHF 11); MidPen 626 Walnut, San Carlos $3,000,000 (95, County-owned site); Novin 3051 Edison, North Fair Oaks $1,537,897 Measure K and impact fees (70); Habitat Ladera, Portola Valley $300,000 (2 ownership units). AHF 13.0 (NOFA June 2, 2025, due July 3, 2025): $41,146,470 to nine projects on Oct 7, 2025 from $19,000,000 new Measure K, $18,022,198 recaptured Measure K, $824,272 PLHA, $100,000 impact fees, MHSA and HHAP. Since 2013 the AHF has awarded more than $400,000,000 to 63 projects and 4,522 units (2,339 occupied, 588 under construction, 1,595 in predevelopment).
- AMI requirement: Units typically 30% to 60% AMI with a 55-year County regulatory agreement from completion (57 years from recording of the deed of trust); at least 5% of low-income units (or two units) reserved with a preference for people experiencing homelessness at 30% AMI with tenant-based subsidies and referrals held open 60 days; MHSA/BHSA units for behavioral health referrals at 30% AMI with PBVs or 20% AMI without; studio and one-bedroom rents assumed at no more than 50% AMI and larger units at 60% AMI.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: The DOH funding page lists the next Affordable Housing Development NOFA for fall 2027; AHF 14 closed in May 2026 and its awards were approved Aug 25, 2026. Recaptured funds are offered between rounds through an over-the-counter process (NOFA Appendix C). Pre-application meetings are mandatory and applications require third-party commitments of at least the lesser of $1,000,000 or $20,000 per unit.)
- Layering notes: The Peninsula's main gap source: about $19,000,000 of new Measure K sales tax a year plus recaptured funds, MHSA/BHSA capital, HACSM Moving to Work money and whatever HOME and PLHA the County holds, all run through one annual NOFA. Terms (Appendix D): 3% simple interest from certificate of occupancy, 55-year term, annual payments of at least 50% of residual receipts to the County (two-thirds pooled pro rata when other public soft lenders share), deferred developer fee paid only from the borrower share, DOH will not cut its rate unless the host city and state lenders do the same. Measure K and impact fees do not trigger Davis-Bacon, but the application requires a state prevailing wage determination and all projects must comply with the County Living Wage Ordinance, so budget prevailing wage. Awards are explicitly sized to make 4% and 9% tax credit, AHSC and Homekey+ applications competitive and ranked on readiness. The NOFA does not award PBVs; HACSM must issue its own commitment letter. Documents opened: AHF 14 NOFA PDF and Appendix D underwriting guidelines, the July 29, 2026 HCDC hearing deck, and Board items 25-838 and 26-679 via the Legistar API.
- Source: https://www.smcgov.org/housing/ahf14-nofa
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Tax-exempt multifamily bonds: County TEFRA approvals for CMFA, CSCDA and CEDA issuances (County not an active issuer)**
- Agency: San Mateo County Board of Supervisors with the County Attorney (TEFRA hearings); bonds issued by the California Municipal Finance Authority, California Statewide Communities Development Authority or California Enterprise Development Authority
- Type: bond
- Typical award: The County is a member of CMFA, CSCDA and CEDA and holds the required TEFRA hearings for projects in the unincorporated area or where it is the member jurisdiction: CMFA bonds up to $11,000,000 for Mercy Housing's 42-unit 903 Oceana Boulevard rehabilitation in Pacifica (April 7, 2026), plus CMFA (May 6, 2025), CEDA and CSCDA (June 10, 2025) hearings. CMFA shares part of its issuance fee with the County. No County-issued multifamily housing revenue bonds were found in 2025 or 2026 Board records.
- AMI requirement: CDLAC and Section 142(d) minimums (20% at 50% AMI or 40% at 60% AMI) under the JPA issuer's regulatory agreement; County loans in the same project add 55-year County restrictions.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Schedule the TEFRA hearing with the County Attorney's office at least seven days after newspaper notice and before the CDLAC application; the Board hears these on consent. For projects inside cities, the city holds the hearing.)
- Layering notes: Expect to use CMFA or CSCDA as issuer in San Mateo County; the County's role is the TEFRA approval and a small fee share, with no County credit or staff involvement. Bonds alone do not trigger state prevailing wage; AHF, HOME or HACSM money in the deal does. Board item 26-255 read through the Legistar API.
- Source: https://sanmateocounty.legistar.com/LegislationDetail.aspx?ID=94560&GUID=2D3E4F79-DC91-4DE6-A546-5FA23BF9060C&Options=&Search=&FullText=1
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Welfare Exemption (property tax), San Mateo County Assessor-County Clerk-Recorder**
- Agency: San Mateo County Assessor-County Clerk-Recorder and Elections (ACRE), Mark Church, Assessor; 555 County Center, Redwood City; 650-363-4500; institutional exemptions unit via smcacre.gov contact page
- Type: tax abatement
- Typical award: Full or partial exemption of property tax on units occupied by households at or below 80% AMI under Revenue and Taxation Code 214(g); worth roughly 1.1% or more of assessed value a year depending on the tax rate area. ACRE posts the full BOE-267 series (267, 267-L, L1, L2, L3, L4, H, O, R and income worksheets) and notes the annual BOE-267-A is issued by the office rather than downloaded.
- AMI requirement: Nonprofit or LP with eligible nonprofit managing general partner holding a BOE Organizational Clearance Certificate (plus a Supplemental Clearance Certificate for LP low-income housing); units restricted by recorded regulatory agreement or occupied by households at or below 80% AMI; BOE-267 first filing and annual supplemental affidavits by Feb 15 (late claims receive a reduced exemption under Rev. and Tax. Code 270).
- Cycle: rolling (next: 2027-02-15 (annual claim deadline for the 2027-28 roll). File the first claim on acquisition so the construction period is covered.)
- Layering notes: Standard in every tax credit operating budget; San Mateo's high assessed values make this the largest operating subsidy most projects receive. ACRE's institutional exemptions and forms pages (opened) list forms and the OCC and SCC requirements but no local deadline text or direct phone; the BOE county contacts page gives the main number.
- Source: https://smcacre.gov/assessor/institutional-exemptions-forms
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)

**Winter NOFA: HOME Consortium, Urban County CDBG, ESG and PLHA (annual federal and state funds round)**
- Agency: San Mateo County Department of Housing, Housing and Community Development division, as HOME Consortium lead and Urban County CDBG grantee
- Type: loan
- Typical award: FY 2026-27 Winter NOFA (issued Dec 2, 2025, applications due Jan 15, 2026, HCDC hearing March 12, Board April 21, 2026) sources: CDBG $2,315,528 plus $347,644 program income and returned funds; HOME $1,485,482 plus $1,428,857 program income and returned funds; federal ESG $208,430; state ESG $165,356; PLHA $0 (held pending housing element compliance); City of East Palo Alto minor home repair $125,000; total $6,076,297. Uses: $2,000,000 HOME for new affordable housing and preservation, $717,035 HOME CHDO set-aside left unallocated for an over-the-counter award, $620,815 CDBG rehab and public facilities, $493,716 Section 108 loan repayment (Trestle Glen). The December 2025 notice estimated about $5,000,000 in total CDBG, HOME and related funds; a separate capital projects guideline listed about $3,317,010 CDBG for capital and $1,738,713 for community development based on FY 2025-26 levels.
- AMI requirement: HOME: households at or below 80% AMI at HOME rents, 55-year County regulatory agreement (HOME units become AHF-restricted units for the balance of the 55 years when the HOME period is shorter); CDBG: low and moderate income benefit; County loan terms as in the AHF underwriting guidelines.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: FY 2027-28 Winter NOFA to be released Dec 1, 2026 after the HCDC needs hearing on Oct 22, 2026; applications are typically due mid-January with Board approval in April. Housing capital applicants should also watch the fall AHF round.)
- Layering notes: Urban County: unincorporated area plus Atherton, Belmont, Brisbane, Burlingame, Colma, East Palo Alto, Foster City, Half Moon Bay, Hillsborough, Menlo Park, Millbrae, Pacifica, Portola Valley, San Bruno, San Carlos and Woodside. HOME Consortium: the Urban County plus Redwood City, San Mateo and South San Francisco (Daly City is its own entitlement, Pass 3). Federal money is small next to Measure K, so HOME usually appears as a $1,000,000 to $2,000,000 piece inside an AHF-funded project. Davis-Bacon applies to HOME projects with 12 or more assisted units and CDBG projects with eight or more; County loans also carry state prevailing wage and the County Living Wage Ordinance. Sources opened: Winter NOFA page, FY 2026-27 Action Plan sources and uses PDF, FY 2027-28 hearing notice.
- Source: https://www.smcgov.org/housing/fy-2026-27-winter-federalstate-funds-nofa
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-06)


### Santa Clara County

**2016 Measure A Affordable Housing Bond (Housing Bond)**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Office of Supportive Housing (OSH), Housing and Community Development Division
- Type: loan
- Typical award: $950 million general obligation bond approved November 8, 2016. As of October 2026 the Board had committed $879.2 million; 65 multifamily rental developments in 12 cities received about $747 million ($714.3 million in Supportive Housing Development Program rounds 1 through 14 and off-cycle awards plus $11.9 million acquisition loans and $90.5 million County acquisitions). Individual commitments range from $1.0 million (The Veranda, Cupertino, 19 units) to $42.3 million (Mosaic Court, Milpitas, 134 units); Gateway Tower (220 units, San Jose) revised January 13, 2026 to $25,560,000, about $116,182 per unit.
- AMI requirement: Board goals by category: PSH for persons with disabling conditions, rapid rehousing units, ELI (30% AMI) and VLI (50% AMI) units, plus 300 mixed-income units and homeownership. 4,800 rental unit goal exceeded (5,255 goal units approved, 109%). Long-term County regulatory agreement recorded on each project.
- Cycle: closed/inactive (next: No new application round. As of October 2026 the remaining $67.8 million is reserved for three developments on County-owned land and one affordable homeownership development (Branham Station, VTA TOD). About $6.4 million of program-generated income is available to recommit to multifamily rental projects.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. The County's November 3, 2026 implementation report (first annual report, year 10 of a 10-year plan) states the bond is nearly fully deployed: 65 approved rental developments with 6,846 units (2,693 supportive), 38 in operation, 17 under construction, four fully financed and six in predevelopment; $30.5 million in program-generated income received, $23.5 million recommitted. Measure A averaged about $6 of outside funding per $1 of County bond money and has been the standard local soft source behind 4% and 9% tax credit deals in Santa Clara County; in 2025, 11 County-funded projects won tax credit awards. Awards are made through the over-the-counter Supportive Housing Development Fund NOFA (see separate record) and paired with SCCHA project-based vouchers (3,000 plus PBVs deployed). Do not confuse with the November 4, 2025 Measure A, a five-eighths cent County sales tax for safety-net services that is not a housing fund. The Board is studying a future housing fund (June 2, 2026 report) but no successor measure is on the November 2026 ballot; the statewide $10 billion housing bond (AB 736/SB 417) is the near-term pipeline source. County loans require state prevailing wage as public funds.
- Source: https://sccgov.iqm2.com/Citizens/Detail_LegiFile.aspx?ID=130214
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Behavioral Health Services Act (BHSA) Integrated Plan FY 2026-27 through FY 2028-29, housing interventions**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Behavioral Health Services Department (BHSD)
- Type: other
- Typical award: Total BHSA expenditure budget about $160 million a year; Housing Interventions component at the statutory 30 percent, $48 million a year (no transfer or exemption requested). Uses: tenant-based rental assistance for up to 700 individuals a year (500 permanent, 200 interim), continuation of the Casa de Novo interim housing program, Transitional Rent and Housing Deposits as a Medi-Cal managed care plan contracted provider from January 1, 2026, and licensed residential care facility support through a separate $8,038,653 CCE Preservation grant ($4,578,568 capital, $3,460,085 operating).
- AMI requirement: BHSA-eligible adults with serious mental illness or substance use disorder who are homeless or at risk; 50 percent of housing funds targeted to chronically homeless individuals, with a focus on encampments.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Draft Integrated Plan adopted by the Board June 2, 2026 and submitted to DHCS by June 30, 2026; plan period July 1, 2026 to June 30, 2029. Annual updates each spring.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. Santa Clara County elected not to use BHSA for capital development (the plan says MHSA housing funds go to interventions that provide more immediate access) and not to fund operating subsidies with BHSA, so unlike San Diego or Sacramento there is no BHSA capital or PSH operating line to underwrite here. BHSD contracts with two providers for on-site specialty mental health services at PSH developments and will refer BHSA clients to County and SCCHA project-based units through coordinated entry. The Casa de Novo site (Homekey) continues as interim housing. Plan PDF: sccgov.iqm2.com/Citizens/FileOpen.aspx?Type=4&ID=270066 (Attachment B).
- Source: https://sccgov.iqm2.com/Citizens/Detail_LegiFile.aspx?ID=128128
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**CDBG Urban County and HOME Consortium, FY 2026-2027 Annual Action Plan**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Office of Supportive Housing (OSH); Housing and Community Development Advisory Committee (HACDAC) recommends
- Type: grant
- Typical award: FY 2026-27 total $7,208,297 approved May 19, 2026: CDBG entitlement $1,432,939 plus program income and prior-year funds ($3,855,918 total); HOME entitlement $829,034 plus program income and prior-year funds ($3,352,379 total). HOME housing development project HO-27-01: $3,269,475 to OSH for new construction, acquisition or acquisition with rehabilitation. CDBG project SCC-27-02: $2,752,240 for acquisition, rehabilitation or preservation of multifamily housing, shelters and transitional housing.
- AMI requirement: HOME: 20 percent of units at 50 percent AMI, rest at 60 percent AMI, HOME affordability period 5 to 20 years (County typically records 55 years). CDBG: 51 percent of units low and moderate income.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: FY 2027-28 CDBG and HOME NOFA expected winter 2026-27 (HACDAC acted March 25, 2026 and the Board April 28 and May 19, 2026 for FY 2026-27). Development funds are pooled under OSH and released through the SHDF NOFA.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area and the small cities (Campbell, Los Altos, Los Altos Hills, Los Gatos, Monte Sereno, Morgan Hill, Saratoga); San Jose, Sunnyvale, Santa Clara, Mountain View, Milpitas, Cupertino, Palo Alto and Gilroy run their own CDBG programs. The County HOME Consortium adds HOME for those members. HOME development money is modest (about $3.3 million in FY 2026-27 including carryover) and is used as a layer under Measure A loans. Federal Davis-Bacon applies to HOME projects of 12 or more assisted units and CDBG projects of 8 or more units; NEPA review required. Funding recommendations PDF: sccgov.iqm2.com/Citizens/FileOpen.aspx?Type=4&ID=269017.

**Conduit multifamily and nonprofit housing bonds, County TEFRA approvals (CSCDA, CMFA)**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Board of Supervisors; TEFRA hearings delegated to the Director of Finance
- Type: bond
- Typical award: The County does not issue its own multifamily housing bonds; it is a member of CSCDA (joined July 13, 1993) and approves conduit issues for projects in unincorporated territory or multi-site plans. 2026 example: CSCDA plan of finance up to $125,000,000 for Odd Fellows Home of California (Saratoga Retirement Community, $110 million, plus a Napa facility), approved February 24, 2026 after a February 4, 2026 teleconference TEFRA hearing. Affordable projects inside cities obtain TEFRA approval from the city council (for example CMFA issues for Sunnyvale Block 15, Sango Court in Milpitas, Santa Clara Apartments $33.5 million CDLAC allocation in 2026). SCCHA itself used CMFA for the Laurel Grove Lane acquisition.
- AMI requirement: Federal 20 percent at 50 percent AMI or 40 percent at 60 percent AMI plus CDLAC requirements; County approval carries no additional covenant.
- Cycle: rolling (next: None. TEFRA hearings are held by Administration before the Board meeting at which the approval resolution is placed on the consent calendar; allow four to six weeks.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. Expect to go to CMFA, CSCDA or CalHFA for bonds and to the host city for TEFRA. The County's role is procedural and fee-free (the County incurs no cost or liability). SCCHA historically issued its own housing bonds (Blossom River 1998A still outstanding) but now acts as borrower through CMFA. Conduit bonds alone do not trigger state prevailing wage; County, state or federal subsidy in the same deal does.
- Source: https://sccgov.iqm2.com/Citizens/Detail_LegiFile.aspx?ID=127492
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**Continuum of Care CA-500 (San Jose/Santa Clara City and County) and HHAP funding**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Office of Supportive Housing (OSH) as CoC lead and collaborative applicant; City of San Jose and the CoC are co-applicants for HHAP
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HHAP Round 6 allocations (HCD, based on the 2024 PIT count): Santa Clara County $11,860,491.01; San Jose/Santa Clara City and County CoC $12,667,208.31; City of San Jose $25,329,964.46. The County's delegated HHAP-6 authority is $24,528,000 (County plus CoC shares); the state notified the region of its award March 17, 2026. The County is also administering about $10 million HHAP-2, $28 million HHAP-3, $25.8 million HHAP-4 and $27.9 million HHAP-5. HUD CoC program grants to the County total about $35.1 million for 23 renewals (2026-27) and the region receives roughly $48 million a year; the 2026 CoC competition application authority is up to $38.1 million for 24 grants.
- AMI requirement: Households experiencing homelessness referred through coordinated entry; Housing First required by the regional MOU; at least 10 percent of HHAP for youth.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: HHAP-6 obligation and expenditure deadlines run through June 30, 2030 under the amended agreement. HUD vacated its 2026 CoC NOFO after federal litigation; the County delegated authority to apply for up to 24 CoC grants when the competition reopens.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. The County uses HHAP for the Homelessness Prevention System, interim housing operations and supportive services for permanent housing, not capital. For a PSH deal the practical asks are a CoC or County services contract and PBVs; the County has committed to fund supportive services at every Measure A development. HUD's proposed 30 percent cap on permanent housing in the 2026 CoC round puts about 1,600 PSH households at risk per OSH (October 2025 coverage). The 2026-2030 Community Plan to End Homelessness (endorsed September 15, 2026) sets a goal of housing 15,000 people. HHAP-6 allocation table opened at hcd.ca.gov (hhap-6-allocations.pdf).
- Source: https://sccgov.iqm2.com/Citizens/Detail_LegiFile.aspx?ID=129202
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-07)

**County Affordable Housing Fund (AHF) and loan portfolio preservation**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Office of Supportive Housing (OSH), asset management
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Special fund established 2003; initial capitalization fully expended, now replenished by loan repayments, interest and administration fees. Recent uses: $626,294 toward Bella Vista Inn interim housing rehabilitation (October 2026); Administration plans to request up to $2,500,000 for preservation of First Community Housing properties that carry $48,128,560 in County loans (six developments, 548 units).
- AMI requirement: Follows the recorded County regulatory agreement on the assisted property; preservation uses retain existing covenants.
- Cycle: rolling (next: None. Case-by-case Board actions as loan modifications are presented (FCH portfolio report November 3, 2026).)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. Small and opportunistic. The AHF matters mostly for sponsors acquiring distressed County-financed assets (FCH announced it will transfer all or part of its portfolio) and for sponsors needing County consent to subordinate, restructure or extend an existing County loan. OSH coordinates workouts with SCCHA and other lenders. Also the vehicle through which program income from closed sources (HOME repayments, Homekey and NPLH interest) is recycled.

**County-owned land for affordable housing (East Santa Clara Street, Distel Circle, Cupertino educator housing, VTA TOD sites)**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Office of Supportive Housing (OSH) with the Santa Clara County Housing Authority
- Type: other
- Typical award: Ground lease or sale of County sites with Measure A gap loans: East Santa Clara Street, San Jose (4.6 acres; Eden Housing senior and Core Companies family rentals plus for-sale townhomes; up to $12.5 million County funds including Local Housing Trust Fund money, May 20, 2025, plus up to $2 million for replacement parking, April 28, 2026); 330 Distel Circle, Los Altos (EAH Housing, up to $14.6 million, under construction); 10333 North Wolfe Road, Cupertino (Eden Housing, about 250 units with educator priority, in entitlement); 8th and Alexander, Gilroy and 10 Kirk Avenue, unincorporated (SCCHA community engagement for preliminary design); four VTA transit-oriented development sites under a County-VTA MOU including Branham Station homeownership ($10.8 million reserved). $57 million of remaining Measure A is prioritized for three County-owned mixed-income sites.
- AMI requirement: Mixed-income and affordable per Board approval for each site; Surplus Land Act exempt surplus findings adopted for County conveyances.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No open solicitation. Developer partners were selected site by site; a WPUSA pilot site and Branham Station are expected to come to the Board in late 2026. Quarterly status reports go to the HLUET Committee.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. County land is where the last Measure A dollars and the mixed-income goal (300 units) will be spent, so these are the only County-sponsored new starts likely in 2027-2028 beyond the approved pipeline. The County pairs land with Measure A, LHTF and SCCHA PBVs and expects developers to secure 4% credits and bonds. Public land leases alone do not trigger prevailing wage but County loans do.
- Source: https://sccgov.iqm2.com/Citizens/Detail_LegiFile.aspx?ID=129854

**Countywide Inclusionary Housing Ordinance and density bonus (unincorporated area)**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Department of Planning and Development
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Regulatory requirement, not a funding source. Adopted October 2020 for residential projects of three or more units in unincorporated Santa Clara County: 16 percent of units (one sixth) deed restricted as affordable, or an in-lieu fee. The fee was set at $259,000 per required unit at adoption and is to be adjusted annually for construction costs; the current figure was not confirmed. In-lieu revenue supports County affordable housing efforts.
- AMI requirement: 16 percent of units affordable per the ordinance; projects may use state density bonus law (Gov. Code 65915) and County Zoning Ordinance Section 4.20.030 for bonus units, waivers and concessions. Stanford Community Plan market-rate housing is also subject to the 16 percent requirement.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Not applicable; applies at entitlement.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. Limited reach: the County had processed no density bonus projects under the ordinance as of the 2023-2031 Housing Element, and the unincorporated area has no Measure A goal units built. Relevant mainly to Stanford lands and rural infill. City inclusionary ordinances (San Jose, Sunnyvale, Santa Clara, Mountain View, Milpitas) govern most sites and are Pass 3 items. The current in-lieu fee schedule sits on the blocked county site and was not opened; confirm with Planning before relying on the $259,000 figure. Density bonus units carry no prevailing wage trigger on their own.

**Intellectual and Developmental Disabilities (I/DD) Housing Fund**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Office of Supportive Housing (OSH)
- Type: loan
- Typical award: $40 million County General Fund commitment for extremely low and very low income housing for persons with intellectual and developmental disabilities and their families; awarded through the SHDF NOFA. Board approved I/DD project delegations April 20, 2021 and February 10, 2026; $2,000,000 of I/DD funds replaced Measure A at La Bella (Mountain View, 2026) and $401,287 of bond program income was added to the FY 2022 I/DD fund. The County has also committed $10 million rounds for I/DD housing in prior years and supported an I/DD project in San Mateo County.

**Permanent Local Housing Allocation (PLHA), County formula allocation and consortium**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Office of Supportive Housing (OSH), lead for the County PLHA Consortium
- Type: grant
- Typical award: HCD 2024-2028 formula NOFA: Santa Clara County $517,137 (2024), $553,929 (2025), $3,232,106 estimated five-year total. For comparison San Jose receives $3.05 million to $3.27 million a year and the City of Santa Clara $336,570 to $360,516. The County also leads a PLHA consortium with small cities that defer their allocations.

Small relative to Measure A but one of the few recurring County capital sources after the bond. PLHA is state money and carries state prevailing wage when used for construction. The HCD allocation table was opened; the County's PLHA five-year plan itself sits on the blocked county site. The March 17, 2026 HLUET annual report confirms OSH administers the PLHA Consortium alongside the Urban County CDBG and HOME Consortium.

**Project-Based Vouchers, Santa Clara County Housing Authority (Moving to Work agency)**
- Agency: Santa Clara County Housing Authority (SCCHA)
- Type: other
- Typical award: SCCHA assists over 22,000 households and has deployed more than 3,000 project-based vouchers to Measure A developments, leveraging about $415 million and anticipating over $3 billion in HAP payments over 20-year contracts. FY 2027 MTW Annual Plan lists 495 vouchers to be newly project-based at 10 properties: 1510 N. First St (57, RAD rehab), 245 Weddell (60), Clara Gardens (30), East Santa Clara Senior (20), Gateway Tower (65), Mountain View Lot 12 (35), Rincon de los Esteros (120, SCCHA acquisition), Trillium (36), Terra Bella (23) and VTA Berryessa (49). Typical award is 20 to 65 PBVs per development, 25 to 50 percent of units.
- AMI requirement: PBV units serve households referred through coordinated entry (chronically homeless), families, seniors, veterans (HUD-VASH) and persons with disabilities; 20-year HAP contracts with County and SCCHA covenants.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No open PBV RFP. PBVs are awarded with County SHDF NOFA capital; MTW Activity 2016-2 allows non-competitive PBV selection for projects chosen through a federal, state or local government solicitation within the last 15 years. SCCHA procurement moved to PlanetBids (portal 66280) in 2026.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. PBVs are the operating backbone of Santa Clara County deals: Measure A capital plus SCCHA PBVs plus 4% credits is the standard stack, and the PBV HAP supports permanent debt sized to contract rents. Owners must certify that prevailing wages were paid for any work before HAP execution and that NEPA (24 CFR 50/58) was cleared before site work; HUD Davis-Bacon applies to PBV new construction or rehabilitation of nine or more contract units. SCCHA owns and acquires properties directly (Activity 2010-4, non-competitive PBVs for SCCHA-owned projects) and partners with the County on County-owned sites. The HCV interest list opened December 2020; SCCHA jurisdiction covers the whole county including San Jose (the former Housing Authority of the City of San Jose is administered by SCCHA).
- Source: https://www.scchousingauthority.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/FY2027-MTW-Annual-Plan-Public-Review.pdf

**Stanford Affordable Housing Fund (SAHF)**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Office of Supportive Housing (OSH); allocations approved by the Board of Supervisors
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Special revenue fund of in-lieu fees paid by Stanford University under the 2000 General Use Permit ($20 to $80 per square foot of academic floor area). $55.9 million collected since 2000; $61.3 million with interest; $1.7 million uncommitted as of June 30, 2026. Fees partially funded 423 new units in seven projects and preserved 237 units in two projects (660 units), with 250 more planned; awards have also gone to San Mateo County projects ($1.5 million, 965 Weeks Street, East Palo Alto).
- AMI requirement: Lower-income affordable housing within a six-mile radius of the Stanford campus; County regulatory agreement.
- Cycle: rolling (next: None. Apply through the SHDF NOFA; only $1.7 million remains uncommitted and the 2023 Stanford Community Plan removed the in-lieu fee pathway, so no new revenue is expected unless the Board restores it in the 2026 GUP.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. Historically the local gap source for Palo Alto area deals (Alta Torre, Stevenson House, 801 Alma, Buena Vista Mobile Home Park, 3001 El Camino Real). The June 30, 2026 HLUET report found the fund produced about 36 units a year and that Stanford's share averaged nearly a third of the typical 30 percent local funding needed for tax credit projects. Stanford has asked that an in-lieu option be restored in the pending 2026 GUP; the Board has not decided. Treat as exhausted for underwriting until that decision.

**Supportive Housing Development Fund NOFA (SHDF NOFA)**
- Agency: County of Santa Clara Office of Supportive Housing (OSH)
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Single over-the-counter solicitation that consolidates all County-controlled housing production funds since 2017 (current NOFA issued August 16, 2021). Sources: 2016 Measure A bond, No Place Like Home (County is an alternative county with a direct allocation of $106,085,749 over four rounds; $102.9 million committed), the $40 million I/DD Housing Fund, Stanford Affordable Housing Fund, HOME (about $1 million a year; $3,269,475 programmed for FY 2026-27), CDBG (site acquisition), PLHA, Local Housing Trust Fund match and Homekey+ (over $40 million awarded in 2025-26 for Algarve Apartments with Abode). Measure A acquisition and predevelopment loans were capped at $8 million.
- AMI requirement: Priority on extremely low-income and special needs households: homeless and chronically homeless, seniors, transition-age youth, agricultural workers, persons with disabilities. Since 2015 the County has funded 7,183 units, 2,997 set aside for homeless households.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Over the counter, no fixed deadline. With Measure A nearly committed, new awards depend on program income, NPLH interest, HOME, PLHA and state awards; the County Housing Element lists the SHDF NOFA as ongoing through 2031.)
- Layering notes: santaclaracounty.gov subdomains (osh, asr, bhsd, news, files) returned HTTP 403 to automated access on 2026-11-07; records were built from the Board of Supervisors IQM2 legislative files (sccgov.iqm2.com), the County Housing Element, Santa Clara County Housing Authority documents, HCD and BOE pages. The NOFA is the single door for County capital in Santa Clara County; applications are underwritten by OSH and taken to the Board in numbered rounds (Round 14 on May 20, 2025) or as off-cycle items. SCCHA project-based vouchers are awarded alongside County capital, and MTW Activity 2016-2 lets SCCHA attach PBVs without a separate competition to projects selected through the County NOFA within the last 15 years. OSH also manages a County Managed Housing Production Pipeline with the 15 cities and SCCHA, supports applications to CTCAC, CDLAC and HCD, and funded 11 tax credit awards in 2025. The County loan portfolio exceeds $1 billion across more than 12,000 units. Source is the County 2023-2031 Housing Element Program 1.01, supplemented by the March 17, 2026 HLUET annual report (sccgov.iqm2.com/Citizens/Detail_LegiFile.aspx?ID=127742) and the October 20, 2026 funding commitments item (ID=130362).
