Most recent example (developer fact sheet, not a City document): The Bridge at West Gateway / West Gateway Place II (60 units, 20% to 60% AMI, six PSH units at 20% AMI, opened June 2025) received $5,194,626 from the City Housing Trust Fund, $2,000,000 of City HOME program income and City land valued at $3,344,500 alongside $11.17 million HCD MHP and $24.76 million California Housing Accelerator. Earlier: $1,191,428 of CDBG program income for West Gateway Place Phase 1 infrastructure and $2.6 million AHSC. Fund balance not published in documents opened this pass.
- AMI requirement: Very low and low income rental units with 55-year covenants; Housing Element priority for extremely low income and large family units in the Bridge District, Washington and Grand Gateway areas.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No NOFA; the City negotiates commitments with its development partners (Jamboree with the West Sacramento Housing Development Corporation, Mercy Housing, New Hope CDC) ahead of state rounds. Ask the Housing Division for the current Fund 261 balance and the Grand Gateway affordable site schedule.)
- Layering notes: West Sacramento is the most active city funder in Yolo County: City land plus trust fund plus HOME program income have repeatedly made 100% affordable Bridge District projects competitive for MHP, Accelerator and AHSC (MOSA won $43 million AHSC in December 2025). The structure is a subordinate residual receipts loan plus ground lease or land conveyance. Layer order: AHSC or MHP, 4% bonds and credits (CMFA or CSCDA conduit with City TEFRA), Yolo County Housing PBVs, City trust fund, PLHA, CDBG for infrastructure. Successor agency land and EIFD financing are separate records. Blocked: cityofwestsacramento.org Housing Division pages, the Housing Policies and Studies page and the SB 341 report (403); the Legistar API only holds 2019 to 2022 matters and the Granicus feed is empty; the City now posts agendas on its own site. Facts are from the HCD-hosted 2021-2029 Housing Element plus the Jamboree fact sheet.
- Source: https://www.hcd.ca.gov/housing-elements/docs/west-sacramento-6th-adopted071521.pdf
- Confidence: needs_confirmation (researched 2026-11-08)


### City of Woodland

**Affordable Housing In-Lieu Fees Fund (Fund 1327) and Spring Lake off-site fee revenues: City gap loans to affordable projects**
- Agency: City of Woodland Community Development Department, Housing (Deputy Director Erika Bumgardner; Housing Analyst Franklin Cui, (530) 661-2019)
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Case-by-case residual receipts loans. Most recent: $1,500,000 to Yolo County Housing for Tupelo Family Apartments (73 units, 72 restricted, 30% to 80% AMI, Yolano-Donnelly Choice Neighborhoods early project), approved July 15, 2025 as a 55-year residual receipts loan at 3% simple interest with a 55-year regulatory agreement; about $20,833 per affordable unit, which staff compared with Mutual Housing at Spring Lake Phase I ($38,462 per unit) and Vista Del Robles Phase II ($41,667 per unit). Earlier the City lent $2,410,000 of in-lieu and Spring Lake off-site fees to Mutual Housing's farmworker phases (100 units) and granted $1.48 million plus free City land for the 61-unit East Beamer Way supportive housing campus. Fund balance not stated; staff noted that in-lieu receipts over the prior four years were enough to replace $500,000 of ARP funds.
- AMI requirement: Extremely low, very low and low income rental units with 55-year covenants; Council priority for projects leveraging tax credits, AHSC and other state funds.
- Cycle: rolling (next: No NOFA; developers request funding through Housing staff ahead of state or TCAC applications. Tupelo received a $27,999,100 AHSC award in December 2025 and will pursue tax credits in 2026 or 2027; staff planned a fall 2025 ARP spending plan update. Contact the Housing Analyst before an application round.)
- Layering notes: Woodland's pattern is a City commitment letter sized to make the AHSC or TCAC application competitive, with Yolo County Housing or Mutual Housing as developer and Sutter Health or Alta California Regional Center philanthropic layers (Tupelo also has $1.5 million from Sutter and $2.3 million from Alta). Loans are residual receipts and subordinate, so they fit under 4% bond deals. Fund revenue is lumpy (large projects and Spring Lake development agreements); confirm the balance before relying on it. Layer order: AHSC or MHP, 4% or 9% credits, Yolo County Housing PBVs, City Fund 1327, CDBG for off-site infrastructure. Agenda packets are on CivicClerk (woodlandca.api.civicclerk.com file streams open; the portal itself is a JavaScript app).
- Source: https://woodlandca.api.civicclerk.com/v1/Meetings/GetMeetingFileStream(fileId=7131,plainText=false)
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Affordable Housing Ordinance (Woodland Municipal Code Chapter 15.16, Ordinance 1662 of 2020) inclusionary requirements and in-lieu fee**
- Agency: City of Woodland Community Development Department (Housing Analyst Franklin Cui, (530) 661-2019) with the Community Services Department
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Requirement, not a subsidy. Citywide outside the Spring Lake Specific Plan: for-sale and multifamily rental projects of 30 or fewer units are exempt; 31 to 80 units provide 5% low income units; 81 or more units provide 10% low income, reduced to 5% if the project is built at 95% or more of maximum allowed density; in Downtown, Corridor Mixed Use and Neighborhood Commercial areas projects of at least 10 units built at maximum density (and at least 10 units per acre) owe 5% for-sale units. Very low income requirements were dropped in the April 2020 amendments. Alternatives: land dedication valued at the in-lieu fee times required units, or in-lieu fees per an adopted fee schedule (reviewed annually) or, if none is adopted, as negotiated in the inclusionary housing agreement and paid before building permits. Example: Ashley and Main (168 units, 2020) paid $469,413. Fees go to the Affordable Housing Fund (Fund 1327); up to 10% of annual income may fund administration.
- AMI requirement: Low income (80% AMI) rental units must be permanently affordable; for-sale affordable units 10 years minimum reset at each resale with a $1,000 silent second and equity share; rents calculated at 50% AMI for very low and 80% AMI for low income units.
- Cycle: rolling (next: None (ordinance). The 2020 temporary relief provisions were set to expire in April 2022 unless dwelling unit goals were met; the Housing Element (Program A.27) committed to a LEAP-funded study of a standardized square-foot in-lieu fee, including nonresidential. Confirm whether a fee schedule has been adopted with the Housing Analyst.)
- Layering notes: Woodland's inclusionary program feeds the only local soft money in the city (Fund 1327, see the in-lieu fund record). Thresholds are generous to market-rate builders (exempt to 30 units, 5% at 31 to 80), so most revenue has come from Spring Lake development agreements and large projects. Spring Lake has its own stricter rules (separate record). Rental inclusionary units must accept Section 8 vouchers (Yolo County Housing PBVs can layer). Chapter 15.16 also now contains the City's state density bonus procedures. No county inclusionary applies inside city limits (Yolo County Chapter 8-8 is unincorporated only). ecode360 hosts the official Woodland code and opened directly.
- Source: https://ecode360.com/43947885
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**CDBG entitlement program (2025-2029 Consolidated Plan, annual Action Plan NOFA) and PLHA formula allocation**
- Agency: City of Woodland Community Development Department, Housing (Housing Analyst Franklin Cui, (530) 661-2019, franklin.cui@cityofwoodland.gov)
- Type: grant
- Typical award: FY 2025/26 CDBG allocation $473,643 (FY 2024/25 $475,086; FY 2026/27 estimated $443,278), about $2.4 million over the 2025-2029 Consolidated Plan adopted July 15, 2025. Allocation pattern: 65% public infrastructure (ADA Accessibility Project $307,868 in FY 25/26 and $299,622 in FY 26/27), 15% public services capped (about $55,000 to $66,000 to food, shelter and fair housing), 20% administration. The five-year plan budgets only $100,000 for the Improve Housing Access and Quality goal, so direct CDBG support to rental development is minimal; past awards funded shelters, supportive housing services and fair housing. HCD's 2024-2028 PLHA NOFA lists Woodland at about $1.09 million over five years. Woodland is not a HOME participating jurisdiction and applies to the state HOME competition (used for first-time homebuyer loans; the interest list is closed).
- AMI requirement: CDBG at or below 80% AMI or low and moderate income area benefit; state HOME at or below 80% AMI.
- Cycle: annual NOFA (next: FY 2027/28 NOFA expected mid February 2027 with applications due about mid March (the FY 2026/27 cycle: NOFA February 18, 2026, applications due March 18, 2026, Council hearing July 21, 2026). Program year starts July 1.)
- Layering notes: Treat Woodland CDBG as a source for off-site infrastructure or ADA work adjacent to an affordable project rather than construction gap; the housing goal is $100,000 over five years. State HOME and PLHA are the city's other formula sources but have gone to homebuyer assistance; ask Housing staff whether PLHA could be redirected to a rental project. Yolo County's CDBG and HOME cover only unincorporated areas. Pair with Fund 1327 loans and Yolo County Housing PBVs. The CDBG page and DocumentCenter PDFs on cityofwoodland.gov open directly; some scanned PDFs (one-pager, SB 341 report) have no text layer.
- Source: https://cityofwoodland.gov/597/Community-Development-Block-Grant
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Density bonus ordinance (Woodland Code Sec. 25-21-25, now implemented in Chapter 15.16) with fee waiver and fee deferral incentives**
- Agency: City of Woodland Community Development Department, Planning Division
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Regulatory incentive. Projects of 5 or more units qualify with 10% lower income, 5% very low income, senior housing or 10% moderate income condominiums; base bonus 20% rising 1.5% per 1% of lower income units (2.5% per 1% very low) to a 35% cap under the posted ordinance, and Chapter 15.16 now grants bonuses, concessions, waivers and parking ratios as a matter of right per current Government Code 65915 (up to 50% and unlimited near transit). Listed local incentives include use of local affordable housing funds, waiver or reduction of City building permit, plan check and inspection fees, and waiver or deferral of City impact fees until certificate of occupancy.
- AMI requirement: State density bonus law thresholds (5% very low, 10% low, 10% moderate for-sale, senior or special needs) with covenants of 55 years for rental.
- Cycle: rolling (next: None; request with the planned development, design review or other entitlement application. The Housing Element (Program A.10) commits to keep the ordinance current with state law and to publish a one-page fact sheet.)
- Layering notes: The fee waiver and impact fee deferral incentives are the city's only fee relief for affordable units and are granted project by project with the density bonus agreement (see the fee deferral record). The 2021 Housing Element found the posted ordinance out of date with Government Code 65915; the ecode360 Chapter 15.16 density bonus section (Ordinance 1662, 2020) is the operative text. Woodland's realistic densities already run near maximums (Spring Lake 101-unit project at 80% of RM density), so the bonus matters most on CMU corridor sites such as Tupelo's CMU-A parcel. City fee waivers are public subsidy and can trigger prevailing wage; impact fee deferrals alone generally do not.
- Source: https://www.cityofwoodland.gov/DocumentCenter/View/425/Bonus-Density-Ordinance-PDF
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Development Impact Fee Deferral Program (MPFP fee deferral for multifamily, December 2008 ordinance) and case-by-case deferrals for affordable projects**
- Agency: City of Woodland Community Development Department and Administrative Services (Finance)
- Type: fee waiver
- Typical award: Deferral, not waiver. The December 2008 ordinance, extended through June 30, 2022, lets multifamily and nonresidential projects defer seven Major Projects Financing Plan (MPFP) impact fees (General City, Library, Police, Water, Roads, Administration, Storm Drain) for up to 24 months or until certificate of occupancy. Separately, the Council has approved interest-free deferral of a portion of impact fees for affordable housing projects to just before certificate of occupancy, and small units under 1,450 square feet pay the multifamily MPFP rate. Dollar value depends on the fee schedule (MPFP fees are several tens of thousands of dollars per multifamily unit in the Housing Element fee tables).
- AMI requirement: Case-by-case for affordable housing projects; the general multifamily deferral had no affordability test.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Unknown, verify with agency. The general program's June 30, 2022 sunset may have been extended; the 2021 Housing Element (Program A.16) commits the City to continue offering fee deferrals and reduced MPFP fees for infill and multifamily housing. Confirm status and the current MPFP schedule with Community Development, (530) 661-5820.)
- Layering notes: Deferral improves construction-period cash flow but is not a subsidy; combine with the density bonus fee waiver incentive and Fund 1327 loans. Woodland has no fee waiver fund and no Prohousing designation. Spring Lake has its own financing plan with payback and fee reduction mechanics tied to building unit allocations. Facts are from the HCD-hosted 2021-2029 Housing Element (Appendix D); the City's fee pages were not checked for a current ordinance.
- Source: https://www.hcd.ca.gov/housing-elements/docs/woodland-6th-adopted091321.pdf
- Confidence: needs_confirmation (researched 2026-11-08)

**Spring Lake Specific Plan Affordable Housing Plan: inclusionary requirements, Off-Site Affordable Housing Fee and 25% density bonus**
- Agency: City of Woodland Community Development Department (Spring Lake Specific Plan area)
- Type: density bonus
- Typical award: Requirement, not a subsidy. Multifamily projects in Spring Lake must make at least 20% of units affordable to and occupied by very low income households plus 10% low income, or alternatively 25% very low income, with a 25% density bonus for complying projects. For-sale projects owe 10% low income units (exempt under 10 units), with land dedication or, only at City initiative for projects under 50 units, an in-lieu fee equal to the appraised affordability gap per unit plus administration. All single-family market-rate units pay an Off-Site Affordable Housing Fee of $1,100 per unit (Council may reset it, not above $1,300), collected before final map approval and spent anywhere in the City on qualifying affordable units; up to 10% may fund administration.
- AMI requirement: Multifamily: 20% very low plus 10% low, or 25% very low income, permanently affordable; for-sale 10% low income for the longest feasible period.
- Cycle: rolling (next: None (specific plan requirement). The Housing Element (Program A.24) proposes amending the Spring Lake Specific Plan to clarify affordability provisions; several Spring Lake single-family projects have paid in-lieu fees under development agreements.)
- Layering notes: Spring Lake is where most of Woodland's affordable pipeline sits: Mutual Housing at Spring Lake (farmworker, 100 units), a 4.6-acre multifamily site dedicated in 2019 to a nonprofit in place of $900,000 of fees (112 lower income senior units required), and R-20 and R-25 sites intended for 25% very low income projects. A 100% affordable developer can acquire a Spring Lake R-20 or R-25 site and satisfy a market-rate builder's obligation, then draw on Fund 1327 and the off-site fee revenues as gap money. The Plan still names the Redevelopment Agency as fund recipient; the City is housing successor. Chapter 15.16 applies in Spring Lake only where the Specific Plan is silent.
- Source: https://www.cityofwoodland.gov/DocumentCenter/View/422/Spring-Lake-Affordable-Housing-Plan-PDF
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)

**Woodland Housing Successor Agency (former Redevelopment Agency) Low and Moderate Income Housing Asset Fund (SB 341 reporting)**
- Agency: City of Woodland as Housing Successor to the former Woodland Redevelopment Agency (Community Services and Community Development Departments)
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Unknown. The City posts an FY 2021/22 Senate Bill 341 report for the Woodland Housing Successor Agency on its Housing page, confirming the City holds housing successor assets (loans, covenants and any residual Low and Moderate Income Housing Asset Fund balance) from the former Redevelopment Agency, which once received Spring Lake off-site fees. No current fund balance, excess surplus calculation or deployment plan was found in documents opened this pass; recent City gap loans have been drawn from in-lieu Fund 1327, not successor funds, suggesting the LMIHAF is small or encumbered.
- AMI requirement: Health and Safety Code 34176.1 targets: at least 30% of LMIHAF expenditures for rental housing at or below 30% AMI, remainder at or below 60% AMI, with 55-year covenants.
- Cycle: closed/inactive (next: Unknown, verify with agency. Ask Housing staff for the latest SB 341 annual report (due with the Housing Element Annual Progress Report by April 1 each year) and any excess surplus.)
- Layering notes: Useful negative information: Woodland's post-RDA housing money is the inclusionary Fund 1327, not a successor fund. Any LMIHAF loan repayments or land sale proceeds would be an additional city-controlled layer with extremely low income targeting. The posted SB 341 report (DocumentCenter 9562) is a scanned PDF with no text layer and could not be read by script; the Housing page listing it was opened.
- Source: https://www.cityofwoodland.gov/261/Housing
- Confidence: needs_confirmation (researched 2026-11-08)


## Private & Philanthropic Sources


### San Luis Obispo County

**San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund revolving loans (private CDFI)**
- Agency: San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund, 71 Zaca Lane Suite 130, San Luis Obispo, 805-543-5970 (Kathy McClenathen, Deputy Director)
- Type: loan
- Typical award: Notice of Funding Available updated April 1, 2026: up to $3 million available, maximum loan $3 million per project, term generally up to 5 years, 5% to 7% fixed interest, 1% to 2% fees plus costs, real estate collateral (may be subordinate), loan-to-value 80% to 100% including senior debt. LIHTC Project Loans (soft, 15-year or longer, from LHTF and PLHA grant funds): up to $40,000 per extremely low income unit and $20,000 per low income unit, $1.5 million per project. Since 2003: 70 loans over $44 million for 1,700 units; $5.9 million closed in 2024 with $20 million outstanding.
- AMI requirement: Extremely low to moderate income (most loans at or below 80% AMI); can finance units to 120% or 160% AMI; priority for housing serving people with disabilities; 40% of resources committed to ELI households.
- Cycle: rolling (next: Rolling; four-step process starting with a phone or email inquiry, then invitation to apply. Maintains a 2026 Multi-Family Readiness List.)
- Layering notes: San Luis Obispo County's Department of Social Services Homeless Services Division (HSD, Devin Drake, Director; George Solis, Principal Administrative Manager; HSDgrants@co.slo.ca.us) runs one combined annual Action Plan NOFA each fall for CDBG, HOME, ESG, HHAP, PLHA, Title 29 and County General Fund Support on the Neighborly portal, and is the Continuum of Care collaborative applicant and administrative entity (CA-614). The Department of Planning and Building runs Title 22 land use incentives and assesses the new Title 31 in-lieu fee; the Behavioral Health Department holds BHSA; the Housing Authority of San Luis Obispo (HASLO) and its San Luis Obispo Nonprofit Housing Corporation, Paso Robles Housing Authority and Peoples' Self-Help Housing are the main developers; and the private nonprofit San Luis Obispo County Housing Trust Fund is the local CDFI gap lender. The Urban County covers the unincorporated area plus Arroyo Grande, Atascadero, Morro Bay, Paso Robles, Pismo Beach and San Luis Obispo (Grover Beach is outside). The County repealed its inclusionary ordinance on June 7, 2022 and replaced it in October 2025 with a voluntary Regional Housing Incentive Program and Regional Housing Fund. slocounty.ca.gov opens directly but rate-limits bursts (HTTP 429); Legistar web API is not enabled for the County. The Housing Trust Fund is the fastest local money in the county and has participated in nearly every recent affordable deal (Cambria Pines, Rolling Hills III, Cleaver and Clark Commons, River Walk Terrace). Use it for predevelopment and acquisition bridges before County and state awards, and ask about the LIHTC soft loan product funded by state LHTF matching grants ($1.5 million LHTF award reported in 2025).
- Source: https://www.slochtf.org/for-borrowers
- Confidence: verified (researched 2026-11-08)
